"Years" is a time horizon, not a growth rate, and "Deduct fund fee" is a boolean, so both were left as they were rather than sliding every control on the panel for consistency's sake.
- Each rate keeps two bound controls sharing one `$state` variable — a `type="range"` slider and the pre-existing `type="number"` field — rather than replacing the number input outright. A slider alone can't be typed into precisely (useful when copying a specific assumption from elsewhere) and can't reach values outside its drag range; keeping the number field means the full `-100..100`/`0..100` validation range `forecast.js` already accepts is still reachable, the slider is just the fast way to get there for realistic values.
- Slider drag ranges (`-10..15` for growth, `0..10` for spread, both new local constants) are an invented UI convenience, not spec — README.md gives no bounds for either assumption, same category of invention #16's `DEFAULT_SCENARIO_SPREAD` was. Chosen to cover realistic UK planning scenarios (equities ~5-8%, cash ~1-3%, a wide pessimistic/optimistic spread) without spreading a -100..100 range across one drag gesture, which would make every realistic value sit in a few pixels at the slider's centre. The paired number field is the escape hatch for anyone who wants -40% or a 50pp spread.
- Swapped each field's `<label for>` for a `<span id>` plus `aria-labelledby` on both the range and number inputs, since one visible label now names two controls instead of one input. Caught in review before committing: the first pass moved the `<label>` to a `<span>` without also adding `aria-labelledby` to the number input, which would have left it with no accessible name at all — fixed by pointing both inputs at the same label span.
- Verified the "live, no apply step" behaviour for real rather than trusting the reactivity chain by inspection, following the project's established pattern for untested Svelte wiring (#15, #16): built and served the production bundle, drove it with `puppeteer-core` against system Chromium, dragged the growth slider from 5% to 9.5% via a dispatched `input` event, and confirmed both the paired number field and the scenario cards below updated immediately (£450,907 → £1,088,794 realistic case) with no separate action. Also drove it the other direction — typing into the number field moved the slider thumb — confirming the two-way binding both ways. `puppeteer-core` and the verification script were removed afterwards; no test dependency was added to the project.
- Updated the Forecast tab's intro copy (`+page.svelte`) to describe the sliders now that they exist, dropping "Live growth sliders" from the list of features still to come.

**Trade-offs / deviations from prompt:**
- No chart exists yet (#12) for the sliders to visibly redraw a line against — the "live" result today is the scenario summary cards and table re-rendering, which is the entire UI this panel has. The sliders will drive the same `$derived` chain once #12 adds a chart underneath, with no changes needed here.
- No dedicated automated test for the slider markup itself — this codebase's established convention (no jsdom/testing-library) puts test weight on `$lib` logic, and there is no new `$lib` logic in this change to test; the Chromium run above is the verification, same as #15/#16's component-only changes.
- Slider drag ranges are fixed constants, not derived from the user's own data (e.g. centring on their existing rate) or configurable. A rate typed outside the drag range still works via the number field but the slider thumb sits pinned at its nearest end until the value is dragged back inside range — a minor visual quirk accepted over the complexity of a dynamic range that would need its own bounds logic.

## Retirement + net worth milestone markers on chart — 2026-08-06
<!-- METRICS:retirement-net-worth-milestone-markers-on-chart -->
- **Execution Duration:** 565 seconds
- **Model:** claude-sonnet-5
- **Turns:** 93
- **Input Tokens:** n/a (see "Why tokens are usually n/a" above)
- **Output Tokens:** n/a (see "Why tokens are usually n/a" above)
- **Estimated Cost:** $3.5071
- **Ballpark cost, comparable GPT-5-tier model:** $3.2879 (illustrative -- see methodology note above)
- **Ballpark cost, comparable Gemini-tier model:** $2.0092 (illustrative -- see methodology note above)


**Decisions:**
- Built the crossing-date math as a standalone `src/lib/milestones.js` rather than folding it into `forecast.js`, and wrote it against `Forecast`/`ForecastPoint` rather than any chart library, even though the issue title says "on chart" — #12 (the actual LayerChart-based net worth chart) is a separate, still-open issue with nothing built yet, so there is no chart to draw a pill or a vertical line onto. This follows the same forward-compatible pattern `forecast.js` already used for #12 (`forecastBand()`'s low/mid/high computed before any chart existed to read it): `milestoneCrossings()`/`retirementMarker()` are ready for #12 to import once it lands, instead of #12 having to reinvent crossing-date math or this issue building a throwaway chart of its own.
- Rendered the markers as pills/text in `ForecastProjections.svelte` for now, since that is the only forecast UI that exists. The component's header comment now documents why this is a deliberate placeholder rather than the finished feature, so a future #12 session understands the list is meant to be replaced/complemented by real chart annotations, not treated as already-done.
- "Achieved" milestones and "already reached" retirement age are computed off the anchor point (offset 0) once, not per scenario — reusing `forecast.js`'s own documented convention that offset 0 is identical across all three scenario series. A naive per-scenario check could otherwise disagree with itself (e.g. "achieved" under optimistic but not under pessimistic) despite all three sharing the same starting point.
- The retirement date is found once (off the realistic series) and net worth at that offset is then read from all three series, rather than computing three separate retirement dates — the date is calendar/age-driven, not growth-driven, so every scenario shares the same offset for a given age. This mirrors `forecastBand()`'s low/mid/high shape instead of inventing a new one.
- Age is computed from `Profile.dob_year` plus the optional `dob_month` (`ageAtPoint`): exact when the birth month is known, accurate to within a year when it isn't. `Profile` deliberately stores no day-of-birth (README.md's data model), so this is the most precision available without changing the schema.
- Since `store.js`/#5 (which would wire a real `Profile` into the app) hasn't landed, birth year, birth month and retirement age are typed directly into the `ForecastProjections` panel as session-only assumptions, never persisted — the same pattern the component already uses for its "no history yet" starting-position fallback. `createProfile()` is called locally just to shape these three fields into what `retirementMarker()` expects; none of `Profile`'s other fields are collected or invented.
- Milestone pills show the realistic-scenario crossing date as the headline with the optimistic–pessimistic range in brackets, rather than three separate dates or a realistic-only date — the same "confidence band" idea `forecastBand()` already established for the (still unbuilt) chart, applied here to a single date instead of a whole line.
- `formatMilestoneLabel()` special-cases round thousands/millions ("£250k", "£1M") to match README.md's own shorthand for the four standard amounts, falling back to a fully formatted amount for anything else — added for forward compatibility with README's separate custom-milestone feature, though not exercised by the four standard amounts today.

**Trade-offs / deviations from prompt:**
- No real chart exists to plot markers onto — the biggest deviation from the issue's literal wording ("shown ... on the forecast chart"). Building #12's LayerChart integration here too was out of scope: #12 is its own open issue with its own scope (add the LayerChart dependency, build the tracked+forecast lines, the confidence band), and duplicating that work risks conflicting with whatever session picks #12 up next. Flagged explicitly in both the component's header comment and the PR description so it doesn't read as accidental scope-cutting.
- Birth month is a bare number input (1–12) rather than a month-name dropdown, to stay consistent with every other numeric input in this panel and avoid the added complexity of a `<select>` bound to a nullable value; a future settings/profile form is the natural place for a nicer picker.
- No dedicated component test for the new markup — same established convention as #17's slider work (no jsdom/testing-library in this codebase), so test weight sits entirely on `milestones.js`'s 20 unit tests; the rendered pills/text were checked by hand via `npm run build && npm run preview` + `curl`.
- Retirement age validity is bounds-checked (16–120, matching `validateAppData`'s own range) but a bad value silently falls back to 67 rather than surfacing a per-field error the way the growth/spread/years inputs do with `assumptionsAreValid` — a smaller, one-field addition didn't seem to warrant duplicating that whole validity-banner pattern, though it is the one inconsistency in the panel's error handling.

## Age filter for forecast chart — 2026-08-06
<!-- METRICS:age-filter-for-forecast-chart -->
- **Execution Duration:** 872 seconds
- **Model:** claude-sonnet-5
- **Turns:** 98
- **Input Tokens:** n/a (see "Why tokens are usually n/a" above)
- **Output Tokens:** n/a (see "Why tokens are usually n/a" above)
- **Estimated Cost:** $5.2721
- **Ballpark cost, comparable GPT-5-tier model:** $4.9426 (illustrative -- see methodology note above)
- **Ballpark cost, comparable Gemini-tier model:** $3.0204 (illustrative -- see methodology note above)


**Decisions:**
- Same placeholder pattern #18 established for the still-missing chart (#12): built a new `src/lib/age-filter.js` written against `Forecast`/`ForecastPoint`/`ForecastSummaryRow`, not against any chart library, so #12 can filter the series it feeds a chart's x-axis domain with the exported `filterPointsByAge` directly instead of reinventing age-window math a second time. Today, with no chart to zoom, the filter is applied to the one forecast UI that exists — `ForecastProjections`'s scenario summary table.
- `filterPointsByAge` is deliberately generic over anything carrying `month`/`year` rather than typed to `ForecastPoint` specifically — it runs unchanged over `ForecastPoint[]`, `ForecastBandPoint[]` and `ForecastSummaryRow[]` (there's a test asserting the last of those), because a future chart series and this issue's table row are the same "point with a date" shape underneath.
- Filtering narrows what's shown; it never reprojects. `filterForecastByAge`-shaped reslicing of a whole `Forecast`'s `series` arrays was considered and rejected: `summariseForecast`, `forecastBand` and `milestoneCrossings` all index `forecast.series[scenario][offset]` assuming array index equals month offset, so slicing the arrays themselves would silently break that invariant for every consumer downstream. Age filtering instead happens only on the read side, after `forecastSummaryRow`-shaped rows already exist — the underlying `Forecast` handed to milestones/retirement is always the full, unfiltered one, so those keep working correctly regardless of what age window the table is currently zoomed to.
- Added `forecastSummaryRow(forecast, point)` to `forecast.js`, pulled out of `summariseForecast`'s loop body, so `age-filter.js`'s `summariseForecastByAge` builds identically-shaped rows by construction rather than a second hand-written object literal silently drifting out of sync with the first.
- `summariseForecastByAge` picks one row per year of age reached by default (`DEFAULT_AGE_SUMMARY_STEP_YEARS = 1`, overridable), rather than reusing `summariseForecast`'s fixed 1/5/10/20/30-year-from-now horizons — those horizons are meaningless once the axis being zoomed is age rather than "years from now", so a zoom to "55–65" now returns eleven rows, one per birthday, instead of whichever of five fixed points happen to fall inside the window (often none, for a narrow range).
- `forecastAgeBounds` reports the age at the forecast's anchor and at its final month, and `summariseForecastByAge` clamps a requested range to it — a `toAge` of 90 against a forecast that only runs to 70 is trimmed to 70 rather than producing an empty table, mirroring how the age inputs in the UI are disabled (not just validated) until a birth year exists to bound them against.
- The always-include-the-last-point rule is age-aware, not offset-aware, unlike `summariseForecast`'s own version of the same rule. The first implementation always appended the true last point of a clamped range by array identity (matching `summariseForecast`'s "always end where the projection does" convention exactly), but that produced two rows both labelled the same age whenever the request didn't land on a birthday boundary — jarring for a "single age" query like `fromAge: toAge: 50`, which returned two rows for age 50 a year apart in reality. Caught by the unit tests, not by inspection: the final point is now only appended when its age isn't already covered by the last stepped row, so a single-age query returns exactly one row, while a wide `stepYears` still surfaces the true requested edge even when it falls between step boundaries.
- In the component, one shared `dobYear`/`dobMonth` pair now feeds all three age-aware features (age filter, milestone pills, retirement marker) instead of the milestones section owning its own copy — the zoom inputs are otherwise meaningless without a birth year already on screen, so having two independent birth-year fields that could disagree was worse than moving the single existing one up to sit beside the new zoom controls. The "Milestones & retirement" section's copy was updated to say it reads the birth year from above rather than collecting its own.
- The zoom inputs are `disabled` (not just hidden or unvalidated) until a birth year is entered, with the "add a birth year to zoom by age" hint replacing the disabled fields, since a `fromAge`/`toAge` typed in before any birth year exists has literally nothing to compute an age against.
- Each table row now also shows `(age N)` next to its month, computed from the shared birth year via `milestones.js`'s existing `ageAtPoint` — a small addition, but it's what makes the *unzoomed* table legible as an age axis at all when the user is deciding what range to zoom to.
- 19 new tests in `age-filter.test.js` cover `filterPointsByAge` on all three point shapes, `forecastAgeBounds` (including the zero-month-horizon and unknown-birth-month cases), and `summariseForecastByAge`'s clamping, stepping, single-age dedup and step-boundary edge cases (389 total, up from 370).
- Verified in Chromium against the production build (`npm run build && npm run preview`, driven with a throwaway `puppeteer-core` script against system Chromium, both removed afterwards — no test dependency added to the project): the zoom fields are disabled until a birth year is typed in, then zooming to ages 50–55 replaced the five default rows with six age-labelled ones (screenshotted for a visual check), "Clear zoom" restored the default table and reset both fields to empty, a range past the forecast's own horizon (90–100 against a ~40–70-year-old forecast) showed the "no forecast months fall in that age range" message, and an inverted range (`from: 60, to: 50`) showed the validation message instead of silently swapping the bounds.

**Trade-offs / deviations from prompt:**
- No real chart exists to zoom — the same deviation #18's entry flagged for its own "on chart" wording. #12 (LayerChart integration, tracked+forecast lines, confidence band) is still open and unbuilt, so this issue's "zoom" acts on the scenario summary table, the only forecast UI that exists today. `filterPointsByAge` is the seam #12 is meant to reuse rather than rebuild.
- The scenario summary cards above the table (the three "£X in 30 years" headline boxes) are deliberately left showing the full, unzoomed horizon regardless of the age filter — only the table zooms. Making the cards track the zoomed range too was considered, but when a zoom range is empty or invalid the cards would need their own fallback story (show the full horizon anyway? show nothing?) for a headline number, and the issue's own wording ("zoom forecast to specific age range") reads as being about the detailed view, not the top-line summary. The table is where the zoom lives; the cards are unaffected on purpose.
- No inflation, real-terms, or partner/household adjustment to the age arithmetic — `ageAtPoint` is the same whole-years-accurate-to-a-month function #18 already wrote and validated; this issue only adds a second consumer of it, not a new age model.
- `stepYears` is exposed on `summariseForecastByAge` for #12 or a future control to use, but nothing in the UI lets the user change it today — the panel always asks for one row per year of age, which is the sensible default and keeps this change to two new inputs (from/to age) rather than three.

## Compounding effect panel (contributions vs growth split) — 2026-08-06
<!-- METRICS:compounding-effect-panel-contributions-vs-growth-split -->
- **Execution Duration:** 879 seconds
- **Model:** claude-opus-5
- **Turns:** 67
- **Input Tokens:** n/a (see "Why tokens are usually n/a" above)
- **Output Tokens:** n/a (see "Why tokens are usually n/a" above)
- **Estimated Cost:** $6.2220
- **Ballpark cost, comparable GPT-5-tier model:** $6.9998 (illustrative -- see methodology note above)
- **Ballpark cost, comparable Gemini-tier model:** $3.4999 (illustrative -- see methodology note above)


**Decisions:**
- The new `src/lib/compounding.js` does **no compounding arithmetic of its own** — it reads, divides and dates. #16 already accumulates cumulative `contributions` and `growth` onto every `ForecastPoint` as it walks each month ("the part of the month's value change the contribution didn't explain", recorded there precisely because the projection is the only place that knows it), so recomputing the split here from rates, frequencies and fund fees would produce a second answer that drifts from the lines the rest of the tab draws. That is stated as convention 1 at the top of the module, and it is what makes the issue's "verify the split math is internally consistent with the forecast projections" a checkable property rather than a hope.
- That verification is a function, not a comment: `reconcileCompounding(forecast)` walks every month of all three scenarios and reports the largest `|investments − (starting + contributions + growth)|`, which point produced it, and whether every scenario agrees about what was paid in. Tolerance is one penny (`COMPOUNDING_TOLERANCE`), because a penny is the most rounding can cost — `forecast.js` rounds both accumulators to whole pence at every point, and the value series is built from values already rounded to pence. The tests run it over a deliberately awkward 30-year position (fund fees, a per-holding rate override, monthly/quarterly/annual contribution frequencies, debts, and holdings/debts excluded from net worth: 1,083 points checked, worst residual under 2p) and then *break* three copies of that forecast — a tampered `growth`, a tampered `contributions`, and a sub-pound perturbation against a caller-supplied tolerance — to prove the check can actually fail. A consistency check that has never been seen to go red is not evidence of anything.
- Contributions vs growth is a split of the **gain**, but the projected pot has three parts, so both are carried. `contributionShare + growthShare === 1` over `contributions + growth` (the issue's wording taken literally: the split of what the forecast *adds*), while `starting` — the position the user is already in, neither contributed nor earned over the forecast period — sits alongside for the panel's stacked bar. Folding the starting position into "contributions" would have made the growth share of a large existing pot look permanently terrible; leaving it out of the bar entirely would have made a £10k pot and a £500k pot look identical.
- Contributions are a payment schedule, not a return, so all three scenarios pay in the same amount at every month and the pessimistic/optimistic comparison is a comparison of *growth alone*. The panel prints one contributions column beside three growth figures on the strength of that, so `reconcileCompounding` checks it rather than trusting it (`contributionsAgree`).
- The headline number is a **date**, not a total: `growthCrossover` finds the first month cumulative growth is worth more than cumulative contributions. An endpoint split ("59% of the gain is growth") describes a forecast; the crossing month is the thing compounding actually *does*, and it is the number that moves visibly when the growth slider moves. It is reported with the optimistic–pessimistic range in brackets, the same shape #18's milestone pills use, and returns `null` rather than a date past the horizon when a scenario never crosses (at 3% a year, £10k plus £500/month never does inside 30 years — the panel says "never" instead of inventing one).
- `summariseCompounding` reuses `summariseForecast`'s own chosen offsets rather than re-deriving the 1/5/10/20/30-year horizon rules, and in the UI the panel is handed the offsets of whichever rows the scenario summary table is currently showing. So the age zoom (#19) moves both tables together: zoom to ages 60–63 and the compounding table follows to those four months instead of sitting directly beneath a zoomed table showing a different set of years.
- Built as a separate `CompoundingPanel.svelte` taking a `Forecast` as a prop rather than as another section inside `ForecastProjections` (already ~620 lines) or as a component deriving its own forecast. Taking the forecast in means the growth/spread/horizon sliders and the fallback starting position drive the panel too, and there is no second set of assumptions on the tab that could disagree with the first.
- A losing scenario is representable, not an error state. `growthShare` goes negative and `contributionShare` past 1 when growth is negative (they still sum to 1), the stacked bar is suppressed because a negative segment has no width, and the copy switches to "you pay in £180,000 and lose £106,409 to negative growth — at this rate the money is shrinking, not compounding". Same for the two other degenerate cases: nothing paid in ("every pound of the gain is growth", and the crossover column reads "from the start" rather than a technically-correct month 1), and nothing invested and nothing paid in (`gain === 0`, shares `null`, one sentence instead of a bar).
- 35 new tests in `compounding.test.js` (424 total, up from 389). Beyond the reconciliation work above they pin the anchor's empty split, the share arithmetic, the negative-growth and zero-gain cases, offset handling past the horizon, the scenario ordering of growth and of crossing dates, and — the one test that checks the split against something *outside* the codebase — an independently calculated ordinary annuity, `P(1+m)^n + c((1+m)^n − 1)/m` at the geometric monthly rate, matching the projected value and growth to the penny over five years.
- Driven for real in Chromium against the production build (`npm run build && npm run preview` + a throwaway `puppeteer-core` script against the system Chromium, both removed afterwards — no test dependency added, `package.json` and the lockfile are untouched). At the default £10,000 + £500/month at 5% over 30 years the panel reports £180,000 contributions, £260,907 growth, a 59% growth share and a crossing in Oct 2049, and the bar renders 2%/40%/58% grey/blue/green; dragging growth to 8% moved those to £614,902, 77% and May 2040 (Apr 2037–Aug 2045) live; −6% swapped in the losing-scenario copy and dropped the bar; zooming to ages 60–63 moved the compounding table to those four months with ages labelled; clearing the monthly contribution switched the crossover column to "from the start"; and zeroing both the starting value and the contribution produced the "nothing to split" sentence.

**Trade-offs / deviations from prompt:**
- There is still no chart (#12, LayerChart integration, remains open), so "panel" here means a stacked bar plus two tables rather than the stacked area chart this feature usually implies. `compoundingSplit(forecast, scenario)` returns the full month-by-month split — the exact series such a chart would plot — so #12 has the data ready and this issue didn't invent a chart library choice the design docs deliberately leave open.
- The detail table is the realistic scenario only; all three are compared at the horizon in the scenario table above it. A scenario picker, or a nine-column table, was considered and dropped: the per-scenario differences are entirely in growth, which the horizon comparison already shows, and three sets of horizons in one table is unreadable at any width.
- The bar's segment colours are inline styles rather than Tailwind `bg-*` classes, which is a deliberate one-off deviation from the repo's convention. Tailwind's utilities are inert app-wide today — `src/app.css` still carries the v3 `@tailwind base/components/utilities` directives while Tailwind v4 is installed, which needs `@import 'tailwindcss'` — so a class-coloured bar would render as an invisible zero-height div. The width had to be an inline style anyway (it's a computed percentage), so the colour sits beside it. The rest of the panel uses classes as normal and will pick up styling when that one-line CSS fix lands in its own issue (flagged in #15's journal entry, still unfixed and still out of scope for a PR about compounding maths).
- `reconcileCompounding` is not surfaced in the UI. It is exported and the tests are its caller; a banner that only appears when the app's own arithmetic contradicts itself would be clutter on every normal render, and the tests already fail the build in that case.
- The split is expressed against invested value, not net worth. The *gain* is identical either way — debts carry forward unchanged through a forecast (#16's convention, since nothing in the data model says how a balance amortises), so a pound of investment growth is a pound of net worth growth — but the base differs, and the panel says so in a line of copy rather than offering both bases.
- Growth is net of each holding's fund fee, inherited from `forecast.js`, so the "growth" figure is what actually lands rather than a gross market return. There is no separate "fees cost you £X" breakdown; the fee checkbox above the panel already toggles the whole thing, and a fees-vs-growth attribution is a different feature from contributions-vs-growth.
- No inflation or real-terms adjustment: nothing in the app models inflation yet, and a "in today's money" toggle would need to apply to the scenario table and the milestone dates too, not just this panel.

## Stress test overlay — 2026-08-06
<!-- METRICS:stress-test-overlay -->
- **Execution Duration:** 1117 seconds
- **Model:** claude-opus-5
- **Turns:** 79
- **Input Tokens:** n/a (see "Why tokens are usually n/a" above)
- **Output Tokens:** n/a (see "Why tokens are usually n/a" above)
- **Estimated Cost:** $9.5593
- **Ballpark cost, comparable GPT-5-tier model:** $10.7542 (illustrative -- see methodology note above)
- **Ballpark cost, comparable Gemini-tier model:** $5.3771 (illustrative -- see methodology note above)


**Decisions:**
- Built the crash as a *seam in the existing projector*, not as a second projector. `forecast.js` gained one optional option — `adjustMonth(offset)`, returning a `ForecastMonthAdjustment` — and `stress-test.js` builds the whole crash-and-recovery path out of it. The alternative (a parallel month-walking loop in the new module) would have duplicated ~40 lines of the arithmetic every other number on the tab depends on, with drift caught only if someone noticed. Instead the overlay *is* the baseline's arithmetic with two months' worth of assumptions swapped, and the test suite pins that literally: a zero-magnitude stress deep-equals the unstressed forecast, point for point, so any future divergence between the two paths fails CI rather than quietly changing the numbers.
- A `ForecastMonthAdjustment` carries either a replacement `growthRate` or a stated `factor`, and the two mean different things on purpose. The recovery window is a *rate* (so the scenario's ±spread and each holding's fund fee still apply on top, and all three scenarios stay a band through the crash rather than collapsing onto one line); the crash month is a *move* (`× 0.65`, no rate, no compounding, no fee), because "a 35% crash" should mean the pot falls exactly 35%, not 35% plus or minus a month of ordinary growth. Composing the fall as an annual rate instead would have made a one-month −35% into a −99.4%/yr assumption fighting with the fund fee for the last decimal place.
- The crash is booked as *growth*, not as a separate kind of event: `growth += value − previousValue − contribution` is unchanged, so the contributions/growth identity #20 established survives. That is checked rather than asserted — `stress-test.test.js` runs #20's own `reconcileCompounding()` over a stressed 30-year forecast with fund fees, mixed contribution frequencies, debts and excluded holdings (1,083 points, worst residual under a penny). A crash that broke the compounding panel's arithmetic would fail the stress test module's own suite.
- A stressed forecast *is* a `Forecast` (with the config it was built from attached as `stress`), so `forecastBand`, `summariseForecast`, `milestoneCrossings`, `age-filter.js` and `compounding.js` all read it unchanged. That is what makes this an overlay rather than a parallel feature: when #12's chart lands it can plot the stressed series through the same code path that plots the baseline, with no stress-specific chart work.
- Zero magnitude means *no crash at all*, not "a 0% crash followed by a recovery window". The recovery rate would otherwise silently re-rate the year after a crash that never happened, and the overlay would differ from the baseline while claiming nothing had occurred. That rule is what makes the deep-equality test above meaningful.
- Timing is clamped to offset ≥ 1. Offset 0 is the anchor, which `forecast.js`'s convention 1 requires every scenario to share (it is the position the user is actually in, not a projection), so a crash "now" is the first projected month rather than a restatement of today's net worth.
- `stressImpact()` deliberately separates two questions the same number usually conflates: *getting back to the pre-crash figure* (`recoveredAt` / `monthsToRecover`) and *catching the unstressed forecast up* (`caughtUpAt`). At the default settings the pot is back to £16,071 nine months later but still lands £16,833 short at 30 years — the pounds the crash took never earned anything afterwards. Reporting only the first would have made a crash look like a temporary inconvenience; reporting only the second would have hidden the recovery the user actually experiences. It also walks for the true `trough` rather than assuming the crash month is the low point, which is wrong whenever the recovery rate is itself negative (a test pins the trough landing at the end of a negative recovery window, not at the crash).
- Extracted `positionFromEntries()` out of `forecastFromEntries()` so `ForecastProjections` derives the projected position once and hands the same object to both the baseline and the overlay. Re-deriving the anchor separately for each would have risked two projections of the same holdings anchored in different months — the exact bug `forecastScenarios` already guards against internally by resolving `currentCalendarMonth()` once.
- The panel takes the baseline `Forecast` plus that position and builds the overlay from `forecast.start` / `.months` / `.spread`, the same way `CompoundingPanel` takes a `Forecast` rather than deriving one: the growth, spread, horizon and fee controls above drive both lines, and there is no second set of assumptions on the tab that could disagree with the first. It is handed the summary table's current `offsets` too, so an age zoom (#19) now moves all three tables together.
- 45 new tests in `stress-test.test.js` plus 12 in `forecast.test.js` for the new seam and `positionFromEntries` (481 total, up from 424). They pin the exact-magnitude fall, contributions being paid on the unchanged schedule at post-crash prices, the recovery window's exact length and its interaction with the scenario spread and fund fees, per-holding growth overrides being ignored during a market-wide event, debts being untouched, the 100%-magnitude case rebuilding from contributions alone, a crash dated past the horizon leaving the projection identical, and every branch of `stressImpact` including the never-recovers, catches-up and negative-position cases.
- Driven for real in Chromium against the production build (`npm run build && npm run preview` + a throwaway `puppeteer-core` script against the system Chromium, both kept outside the repo and removed afterwards — no test dependency added, `package.json` and the lockfile are untouched). At the defaults the panel reports a 35% crash in Aug 2027 taking £5,125 off a £16,071 position, back to pre-crash in May 2028 (9 months) and £16,833 (4%) short at 30 years; a 60% crash at 10 years with a flat recovery reads 6.9 years to recover and 37% short; 25% for 60 months produced the "catches the unstressed one up by Jun 2033" copy; 90% at 28 years produced "never" in all three scenario rows; a crash dated past the horizon and a 0% magnitude each produced their own message instead of an empty table; dragging the magnitude range slider moved the paired number field and the whole panel live; and zooming to ages 60–63 moved the comparison table to those four months with ages labelled.

**Trade-offs / deviations from prompt:**
- Still no chart (#12, LayerChart integration, remains open), so "overlay" here is a two-bar horizon comparison plus a month-by-month comparison table rather than a second line drawn over the forecast — the same deviation #18, #19 and #20 each flagged for their own on-chart wording. Because `stressForecast()` returns an ordinary `Forecast`, #12 gets the stressed series for free rather than needing stress-specific chart work.
- The crash is instantaneous — one month, then the recovery window. Real bear markets grind down over quarters, and a "crash duration" dial would model that, but README.md names exactly four dials (magnitude, timing, recovery rate, recovery duration) and inventing a fifth would have changed what the other four mean. What this models is the size of the hole and how long it takes to climb out, which is the question the four dials ask.
- The crash hits every holding by the same percentage, cash and emergency funds included. There is no per-holding beta, asset-class tag or correlation in the data model to do better with — `Investment.type` distinguishes cash from equities, but deciding that cash falls 0% while crypto falls 1.5× the headline would be inventing a risk model the spec doesn't ask for and the user can't see or edit. Same reasoning for per-holding growth overrides being ignored during the crash and recovery: a market-wide event is not the place for a per-holding long-run assumption. Both are documented in the module's conventions rather than left implicit.
- Recovery is expressed as a growth rate held for a fixed window, not as "returns to the pre-crash trend line by month N". The trend-line version is tidier to describe but forces a specific rebound shape the user can't reason about; a rate for a duration is two numbers the user sets directly, and the panel then reports whether that was enough (which, at any realistic setting, it is not — the shortfall is permanent, and the copy says so).
- Nothing about the stress config is persisted. It lives as component state for the page session, like every other assumption on this tab (growth rate, spread, birth year, starting position) — `store.js`/#5 doesn't exist yet, and `AppData` has no home for planning scenarios. `normaliseStressTest()` is written to accept a partial, hand-edited object precisely so a future persisted `stress_test` block can be dropped in without changing the module.
- No component test for the panel's markup — the established convention in this repo (no jsdom/testing-library harness), so the test weight sits on `stress-test.js` and the UI was hand-driven in Chromium as described above.
- The comparison table is the realistic scenario only, with all three compared at the horizon in the table above it, matching the choice #20 made for the compounding tables and for the same reason: three sets of horizons in one table is unreadable at any width.
- The bar colours and their label/bar widths are inline styles rather than Tailwind classes — the same one-off deviation #20 documented, for the same unfixed reason: `src/app.css` still carries Tailwind v3 directives while v4 is installed, so utility classes are inert app-wide and a class-only bar would render as an invisible zero-height div. That CSS fix remains its own issue.
- No inflation or real-terms view, consistent with the rest of the tab: a crash of 35% in nominal terms is what the projection is expressed in, and a "today's money" toggle would have to apply to the scenario table, milestones and compounding panel too.

## Set up Svelte stores for global reactive state (lib/store.js) — 2026-08-06
<!-- METRICS:set-up-svelte-stores-for-global-reactive-state-lib-store-js -->
- **Execution Duration:** 779 seconds
- **Model:** claude-sonnet-5
- **Turns:** 64
- **Input Tokens:** n/a (see "Why tokens are usually n/a" above)
- **Output Tokens:** n/a (see "Why tokens are usually n/a" above)
- **Estimated Cost:** $4.4307
- **Ballpark cost, comparable GPT-5-tier model:** $4.1538 (illustrative -- see methodology note above)
- **Ballpark cost, comparable Gemini-tier model:** $2.5383 (illustrative -- see methodology note above)


**Decisions:**
- Used the classic `writable`/`get` API from `svelte/store`, not Svelte 5 runes, even though every component built since #4 uses `$state`/`$props`/`$bindable`. Runes only work inside `.svelte`/`.svelte.js` files; this issue's own title names the file `lib/store.js`, and every other `$lib` module is plain `.js` re-exported through `index.js`'s `export *`. A classic store is also the literal reading of "Svelte stores" in the issue title, and it is what lets `store.test.js` unit-test hydrate/debounce/error-recovery with plain `vitest` (mocking `./gist.js`) — no jsdom or component harness needed, consistent with this codebase's established test-weight convention.
- One store, not several. `CLAUDE.md`'s architecture section is explicit that the whole document is "one JSON blob" synced as a unit; a store per top-level field (`profile`, `pensions`, …) would let two of them independently hydrate or save out of step with each other for no benefit, since `saveAppData()` only ever writes the whole document anyway. `appData` holds the entire `AppData`; a caller that only cares about one slice reads/writes through it with `appData.update((d) => ({ ...d, profile: ... }))`.
- The debounced-save subscriber lives inside `store.js` itself, wired once at module load (`appData.subscribe(...)`), not as a function a consumer has to remember to call after every mutation. That means the "on change" half of the issue is true for *any* future caller — a forecast tab writing `profile`, a pensions tab writing `pensions` — with zero code in that caller beyond an ordinary `appData.update()`/`.set()`, matching how `gist.js` itself is meant to be the only thing that talks to storage.
- `persist()` reads `get(appData)` at the moment the debounce timer actually fires, rather than closing over the value that scheduled it. A debounce that saved a stale closure would be correct today (every change reschedules, so the "stale" and "current" values are always the same by construction) but wrong the moment any future caller batches multiple `.update()` calls between ticks; reading live avoids that trap existing rather than relying on nobody ever hitting it.
- Added a `suppressSync` flag, `true` until the first `hydrateAppData()` call resolves (success or failure), rather than gating on "has hydrate ever run": it stops two real footguns at once — hydrate's own `appData.set(loaded)` immediately re-triggering a save of the thing it just read, and a caller that mutates the store before the root layout has even called `hydrateAppData()` (there is no root layout with that call yet — see Trade-offs) persisting a change made against the placeholder empty document over whatever is actually stored. `store.test.js` pins the first as "does not immediately re-save the document it just loaded" and the second as "does not schedule a save for a change made before the first hydrate".
- `hydrateAppData()` de-dupes concurrent calls by returning the same in-flight promise rather than firing a second `loadAppData()` — a root layout's `onMount` and a future "sync now" button calling it around the same time should share one load, not race two.
- Reused `GistError` from `./gist.js` (added there in #3 specifically "so callers — the future `store.js` — can distinguish 'show a sync error' from 'this is just a first run'") for exactly that: `describeError()` uses a `GistError`'s own message verbatim (it is already a description meant for a human) and only synthesises a generic "Could not load/save: …" wrapper for anything else (a thrown non-`GistError`, in principle unreachable through `gist.js` today but not asserted as impossible). `syncState` surfaces `{ hydrated, syncing, error }` so a caller can render "Saving…"/an error banner without reaching into `gist.js` itself.
- Added `flushAppDataSync()` (save immediately, cancelling any pending debounced timer) alongside the debounced path — small, and the natural counterpart once a "save now" action or a beforeunload handler is wanted, though nothing in the app calls it yet (see Trade-offs).
- Kept the `import('./types.js').AppData` reference as an inline type in a differently-named local alias (`StoreAppData`) rather than a `@typedef` matching `model.js`'s public name — the same ambiguous-re-export workaround `gist.js` already documents for the same reason (`index.js`'s `export *`), but needed its own distinct alias here because `gist.js` had already claimed `AppDataDoc` as *its* local name; `svelte-check` flagged that exact collision once `store.js`'s `export *` landed alongside it.
- Wired the store into the one place a consumer already exists — `src/routes/+page.svelte` — rather than leaving it unconsumed until a later issue. `monthlyEntries` and `activityLog` map onto `AppData.monthly_entries`/`.activity_log` directly (same shape, same name), so the dashboard now calls `hydrateAppData()` in `onMount` and, once hydrated (`ready`), pushes local changes to those two fields back into the store via `$effect`; the store's own debounced subscriber does the rest. Verified for real rather than by inspection: `npm run dev` + a throwaway `puppeteer-core` script against system Chromium (removed afterwards, no dependency added — `package.json`/lockfile untouched) drove the actual "Add debt" form, confirmed the resulting activity-log entry appeared in `localStorage` (`uk-wealth-tracker:data`) after the debounce window, and — after a full page reload — confirmed the activity log entry was there again (rehydrated) while the debt itself was correctly gone (see Trade-offs for why).
- `DebtTracker`'s `investments`/`debts` stay local `$state` in `+page.svelte`, unwired. `AppData` has no top-level `investments`/`debts` fields at all — the schema only nests them per month inside `monthly_entries` (`MonthlyEntry.investments`/`.debts`) — so there is no field of the document for a flat dashboard-wide list to write into without inventing one. Inventing a new top-level field to give them a home would be exactly the kind of speculative schema change `model.js`'s "transcribed verbatim from README.md" rule warns against; the real fix is #8's monthly snapshot form giving these a per-month shape, at which point they plug into `monthly_entries` the same way `monthlyEntries` already does here.
- 14 new tests in `store.test.js` (495 total, up from 481): initial state, the pre-hydrate suppression, hydrate replacing `appData` and marking `syncState`, hydrate not re-saving what it just loaded, concurrent-hydrate de-duplication, hydrate failure leaving `appData` untouched and recording the error without throwing, recovery on a subsequent successful hydrate, the debounce window itself (no save before it elapses, exactly one save after, a burst of three changes collapsing into one save of the last value), a save failure recorded on `syncState` and cleared by the next successful save, and `flushAppDataSync` saving immediately and cancelling a pending debounced save rather than double-saving.

**Trade-offs / deviations from prompt:**
- No root `+layout.svelte` calls `hydrateAppData()` — there is no root layout file with any logic in it today (`src/routes/+layout.svelte` is the nav shell from #4), and every persisted field currently has exactly one consumer (`+page.svelte`), so hydrating there is equivalent to hydrating at the root for the app as it exists right now. This will need moving to the layout once a second route (e.g. a future pensions tab reading `profile`) also needs the store hydrated before it renders, so two routes aren't each racing their own `hydrateAppData()` call — de-duping concurrent calls (above) makes that safe today, but it is still one call site doing a job that will belong at the root.
- `activityLog`/`monthlyEntries` sync one level removed from "the store" in the strict sense: `+page.svelte` still holds its own `$state` copies (so `DebtTracker`/`AutoInvestFill`'s existing `bind:` props needed no changes) and pushes them into `appData` via `$effect`, rather than those components binding straight to `$appData.monthly_entries` themselves. Binding a Svelte component prop directly to a nested path of a classic store's value (`bind:x={$store.field}`) is a documented, supported pattern, but exercising it for the first time in a runes-mode codebase felt like a second, riskier change to fold into the same PR as the store itself; the `$effect`-based bridge is more code but keeps the store's own contract (`writable<AppData>`) exactly what `store.test.js` already verifies against.
- `flushAppDataSync()` has no caller yet — no "save now" button and no `beforeunload` handler wired up. It exists because it was a small, obviously-needed counterpart to the debounced path once written, but adding UI for it without a request for one felt like scope creep; a future issue that wants "your changes are saved" certainty before navigating away has the seam ready.
- `syncState`'s `error` is a plain string, not the richer `{ message, status }` `GistError` itself carries — enough for a banner, but a caller that wants to special-case "401, your token is bad" vs. "network blip" today has to string-match. Kept minimal since nothing in the app renders a sync error yet beyond the one line `+page.svelte` added; revisit if a real error-handling UI is ever built on top of it.
- No retry/backoff on a failed debounced save — if `saveAppData()` throws, `syncState.error` is set and the write is simply not retried until the *next* change to `appData` reschedules one. For a single-user app where the next edit is usually seconds away this is normally invisible, but a failure on the very last edit before closing the tab is not automatically retried on next load (loading doesn't re-attempt a save; only a change does). `flushAppDataSync()` gives an explicit escape hatch once something calls it.
- Still no validation gate: `appData.update()` will happily write a value `validateAppData()` (`./model.js`) would reject (a negative balance, a duplicate id), and it will be saved as-is once the debounce fires. This is the documented, deliberate split from `model.js`'s own convention (normalise vs. validate) — forms are expected to call `validateAppData()` themselves — but it means `store.js` alone cannot guarantee a Gist it writes is valid.

## Monthly snapshot entry form — investment holdings — 2026-08-06
<!-- METRICS:monthly-snapshot-entry-form-investment-holdings -->
- **Execution Duration:** __DURATION__ seconds
- **Model:** __MODEL__
- **Turns:** __TURNS__
- **Input Tokens:** __INPUT_TOKENS__
- **Output Tokens:** __OUTPUT_TOKENS__
- **Estimated Cost:** $__COST__ (from Claude Code's reported total_cost_usd)
- **Ballpark cost, comparable GPT-5-tier model:** $__GPT_BALLPARK__ (illustrative -- see methodology note above)
- **Ballpark cost, comparable Gemini-tier model:** $__GEMINI_BALLPARK__ (illustrative -- see methodology note above)

**Decisions:**
- Built `InvestmentHoldings.svelte` around "which month first, holdings second" rather than one flat holdings list: `MonthlyEntry` is the schema's actual unit (each month is re-stated in full, per `model.js`'s own convention), so the form is a month picker — select an existing month or create a new one, guarded against the same duplicate-month-key collision `validateAppData` flags — over whichever `MonthlyEntry.investments` array is currently selected, rather than inventing a flat cross-month holdings concept the data model doesn't have.
- Followed `DebtTracker`'s established shape closely (list + inline add form + `Card` + `ActivityLog`) rather than a new pattern, but added in-place editing, which `DebtTracker` doesn't have: issue #8 says "add/edit a month's per-holding rows" explicitly, and `logEntityUpdated` already existed in `activity-log.js` (added in #14 but unused until now) for exactly this. `editingId` toggles the same form between "Add holding" and "Save changes" rather than a second form, so there's one code path for the seven-field record either way.
- Scoped the form to issue #9's exact seven fields (name, type, current value, purchase price, year purchased, monthly contribution, account wrapper) and left `contribution_frequency`, `fund_fee`, `notes`, `exclude_from_net_worth` and `ownership_pct` at `createInvestment`'s defaults (monthly, 0, '', false, 100) rather than adding five more form fields the issue doesn't ask for. `exclude_from_net_worth` defaulting to `false` means every new holding counts toward the D/I ratio and any future net worth total unless a later issue adds a way to toggle it — flagged below rather than smuggled in as a `DebtTracker`-style toggle nobody asked for here.
- Discovered mid-build that `activityLog` becoming genuinely mixed-entity (debts since #14, now investments) exposes a real bug in `revertEntityRemoval`: it never checked `entity_type`, so `DebtTracker`'s own revert handler — reading from the same shared array — would happily "revert" a removed *investment* log entry and hand back a non-`Debt` snapshot for `debts` to absorb, corrupting that list. Fixed at the source: `revertEntityRemoval(log, logEntryId, expectedEntityType)` now no-ops on a type mismatch, both components pass their own type, and each renders its `ActivityLog` filtered to its own `entity_type` so the mismatched button isn't even visible. Two new tests in `activity-log.test.js` pin the guard.
- A removed holding's log snapshot carries one extra `_monthly_entry_id` key (stripped back off on revert) alongside the `Investment` fields, so "Revert" knows which month to restore it into. `ActivityLogEntry` has no field for this — it didn't need one before, since debts aren't month-scoped — and widening the shared schema for one caller felt like the wrong direction versus a caller-side sidecar key on the snapshot, which is already a loosely-typed `Record<string, unknown>` by design.
- Wired `InvestmentHoldings` into `+page.svelte` above `DebtTracker`, and changed `DebtTracker`'s `investments` prop from dead local `$state([])` (flagged as a placeholder in #5's own journal entry: "the real fix is #8's monthly snapshot form giving these a per-month shape") to a `$derived` of the latest recorded month's `investments` — so the D/I ratio now has real data to divide by for the first time.
- Hit a genuine Svelte 5 footgun while testing in a real browser: `bind:value` on `type="number"` coerces the bound variable to `number | null` (`to_number()` in Svelte's own `bind_value`), not a string, the moment the user edits the field. The purchase-price and year-purchased inputs need real nullable-string semantics (`''` means "not recorded", not `0`) and were calling `.trim()` on them, which threw once Svelte's coercion kicked in — caught by driving the actual built app in Chromium, not by `svelte-check` or the unit suite, since both are blind to runtime input coercion. Fixed by making those two fields `type="text" inputmode="decimal"`/`inputmode="numeric"` instead, so the bound value stays a genuine string. Current value and monthly contribution keep `type="number"`, since they only ever go through `Number(x) || 0`, which is coercion-agnostic.
- 497 tests (up from 495): the two new `revertEntityRemoval` guard cases in `activity-log.test.js`. No new test file for `InvestmentHoldings.svelte` itself, matching this repo's established convention (no jsdom/component-testing harness) — instead driven for real: `npm run build && npm run preview` plus a throwaway `puppeteer-core` script against the system Chromium (both removed afterwards, `package.json`/lockfile untouched). Confirmed: creating a month, adding a holding with all seven fields, the D/I ratio card picking up the new total, editing a holding's value in place, removing it, reverting the removal (holding reappears in the correct month), the duplicate-month guard blocking a second "Mar 2026", and — after waiting out the 800ms debounce and doing a full page reload — everything still present from `localStorage`.

**Trade-offs / deviations from prompt:**
- Whole-month deletion isn't supported — only individual holdings within a month can be removed. Issue #8 asks for "add/edit a month's per-holding rows", not month management, and an empty month is harmless (it just shows "0 holdings" until something is added to it or it's ignored).
- The Svelte number-coercion bug described above turns out to already exist in `AutoInvestFill.svelte`'s growth-rate field (`rate.trim()` on a `type="number"`-bound value, added in #15) — confirmed by reproducing it directly against the built app. Left unfixed: it's a different component from a different issue, and this repo's own convention (see #20's and #21's journal entries on the still-open Tailwind v4 CSS issue) is to flag a bug found outside an issue's own scope rather than fold an unrelated fix into the PR. Worth its own issue.
- No toggle for `exclude_from_net_worth` or `ownership_pct` on a holding — as noted above, issue #9's seven fields don't include them, so they stay at their `createInvestment` defaults (included, 100% owned) until a later issue asks for them, matching how `DebtTracker` only exposes `exclude_from_net_worth` because README.md explicitly names the mortgage toggle.
- The D/I ratio's `investments` now comes from the *latest recorded month only* (including auto-filled months), not a sum across history — matching what `sumInvestmentValues`/`debtToInvestmentRatio` already expect (a single snapshot's totals) and what `debts` itself represents (also un-scoped by month, since #68 hasn't landed). If debt entry later becomes month-scoped too, both sides should be read from the same selected month rather than each independently picking "latest".
- Client-side validation on the "add month" form (integer month 1–12, year 1900–2200, no duplicate key) duplicates bounds `model.js` keeps private (`MIN_YEAR`/`MAX_YEAR` aren't exported) rather than the fuller `validateAppData()`, which nothing in the save path calls yet (per #5's own journal entry). Good enough to stop obviously bad input reaching the store, not a guarantee of a valid document.

## Monthly snapshot entry form — debts — 2026-08-07
<!-- METRICS:monthly-snapshot-entry-form-debts -->
- **Execution Duration:** __DURATION__ seconds
- **Model:** __MODEL__
- **Turns:** __TURNS__
- **Input Tokens:** __INPUT_TOKENS__
- **Output Tokens:** __OUTPUT_TOKENS__
- **Estimated Cost:** $__COST__ (from Claude Code's reported total_cost_usd)
- **Ballpark cost, comparable GPT-5-tier model:** $__GPT_BALLPARK__ (illustrative -- see methodology note above)
- **Ballpark cost, comparable Gemini-tier model:** $__GEMINI_BALLPARK__ (illustrative -- see methodology note above)

**Decisions:**
- Rewrote `DebtTracker.svelte` from a flat, session-only `debts` list into a month-picker-first form, mirroring `InvestmentHoldings.svelte` (#8/#69) line for line where the shapes match: select an existing `MonthlyEntry` or create a new one (same duplicate-key guard), then add/edit/remove `MonthlyEntry.debts` rows within it. `MonthlyEntry.debts` and `normaliseDebt`/`normaliseMonthlyEntry` already existed in `model.js` — nobody had built the UI for them yet — so no data-model changes were needed, only the component and its wiring.
- Unlike the old component, this one supports in-place editing (`editingId` toggles the shared form between "Add debt" and "Save changes", same pattern as `InvestmentHoldings`) — issue #10's "debt entry UI" plus the fact that a balance is exactly the kind of monthly figure someone will want to correct after typing it. `logEntityUpdated` already existed and was unused by this component before now.
- Closed a gap #8's own journal entry flagged explicitly: with debts now month-scoped too, the D/I ratio card reads `selectedEntry.investments` and `selectedEntry.debts` from the *same* selected `MonthlyEntry` rather than each side independently picking "latest", so the ratio can never silently mix one month's debts against a different month's investments. Both `InvestmentHoldings` and `DebtTracker` still keep their own independent month selector (each can be pointed at a different month while editing), but the ratio itself is always internally single-month-consistent because it lives inside `DebtTracker` and reads off `DebtTracker`'s own `selectedEntry`.
- `+page.svelte` lost its placeholder `debts = $state([])` and the `investments` prop it derived for `DebtTracker` — both components now bind straight to the same `monthlyEntries` array, same as `InvestmentHoldings` already did, so `DebtTracker` no longer takes an `investments` prop at all.
- Removed-debt log snapshots carry the same `_monthly_entry_id` sidecar key `InvestmentHoldings` invented for this exact problem (which month to restore into on revert), rather than widening `ActivityLogEntry`'s schema for a second caller.
- One deliberate behavioural change from the old component: `handleTypeChange`'s mortgage-default-exclude side effect (flip `exclude_from_net_worth` on whenever `type` becomes `mortgage`) now only fires while adding a new debt, not while editing an existing one — the old component never had an edit form so this case didn't exist before. Editing an existing debt and changing its type no longer silently overwrites a checkbox the user may have deliberately unset.
- No new `lib/` tests: `debt.js`'s ratio/sum functions and `model.js`'s `Debt`/`MonthlyEntry` normalisation were already fully built and tested (nothing here changed their behaviour), so this issue is UI-and-wiring only. Verified for real instead: `npm run build && npm run preview` plus a throwaway `puppeteer-core` script against system Chromium (both removed afterwards, `package.json`/lockfile untouched) — created a shared month via the investment form, confirmed the debt tracker auto-selected the same month, added a mortgage debt (confirmed it defaulted to excluded and left the ratio at 0.0%/Healthy), added a second non-excluded debt (ratio correctly became 50.0%/Concern), edited its balance in place (ratio recalculated to 75.0%), removed it, reverted the removal via the activity log, confirmed the investment holding was untouched by the debt-entity revert (the cross-contamination guard from #8/#69 still holds in both directions), hit the duplicate-month guard on the debt tracker's own "add month" form, and confirmed everything survived a full page reload once the store's debounced sync had fired. 497 tests still pass (no lib-level behaviour changed), `npm run lint` clean, `npm run check` 0 errors (same 2 pre-existing `<slot>` warnings as #69).

**Trade-offs / deviations from prompt:**
- The two components still each own an independent month selector rather than sharing one "which month am I editing" control lifted to `+page.svelte`. A user can point `InvestmentHoldings` at one month and `DebtTracker` at another simultaneously while entering data — intentional (matches #8's own per-component selector, and a single shared selector would be a bigger, un-asked-for restructuring of the dashboard), but it does mean the ratio card's month is whatever `DebtTracker` itself has selected, which may not be the month currently open in the investment form above it. The ratio card shows which month it's reporting on to make this legible rather than hiding it.
- No whole-month deletion, same as `InvestmentHoldings` — issue #68 asks for entry/edit, not month management.
- No toggle for `notes` beyond the plain text field already in README.md's five-field debt outline — nothing else to add or leave out here, unlike investments' extra five fields, since `Debt`'s full field list (`id, name, type, balance, notes, exclude_from_net_worth`) is exactly what the form now covers.

## FIRE / Coast FIRE calculator — 2026-08-07
<!-- METRICS:fire-coast-fire-calculator -->
- **Execution Duration:** __DURATION__ seconds
- **Model:** __MODEL__
- **Turns:** __TURNS__
- **Input Tokens:** __INPUT_TOKENS__
- **Output Tokens:** __OUTPUT_TOKENS__
- **Estimated Cost:** $__COST__ (from Claude Code's reported total_cost_usd)
- **Ballpark cost, comparable GPT-5-tier model:** $__GPT_BALLPARK__ (illustrative -- see methodology note above)
- **Ballpark cost, comparable Gemini-tier model:** $__GEMINI_BALLPARK__ (illustrative -- see methodology note above)

**Decisions:**
- Put the maths in `src/lib/fire.js` — README.md's Project Structure names that exact file for "FIRE / retirement maths" — with the same shape every other `$lib` module in this repo has: a header stating the conventions the numbers obey, granular pure functions, and one composite (`fireSummary`) the component calls so a single set of assumptions is behind every figure on the tab. `FireCalculator.svelte` owns only the controls and the copy.
- **Everything is in today's money.** The target income slider is what the user wants to live on *now*, so every pot compared against it has to be in the same units. `growthRate` is nominal, `inflationRate` deflates it via `realGrowthRate` (Fisher: `(1+g)/(1+i)-1` — 5% growth and 2.5% inflation is 2.439% real, not 2.5%), and the accumulation, coast threshold and drawdown all then run at that one real rate. The alternative — projecting nominally — reports a seven-figure pot at retirement against a target income priced in 2026 pounds, which is a comparison that means nothing. `inflationRate: 0` says "the rate I gave you is already real" and the module works nominally instead.
- **The magic number follows the withdrawal-rate slider, not a hard-coded 25.** README.md asks for both "25× target annual income" *and* a withdrawal-rate slider; 25 is just `100/4`, so `withdrawalMultiple()` derives it. At 3% the multiple is 33.3×, at 5% it is 20×. Hard-coding 25 would have left the slider changing how fast the pot empties without changing the target it is measured against — the two would have disagreed on screen. `MAGIC_NUMBER_MULTIPLE = 25` is still exported so a test can pin the spec's own figure.
- **Accumulation and drawdown are one walk with the sign of the cash flow flipped**, and that walk is `auto-invest.js`'s: geometric monthly rate, growth credited before the month's cash flow, rounded to whole pence with the rounded value carried forward. A test asserts `projectAccumulation` agrees with `projectHoldingValue` month for month over 24 months rather than trusting the two to stay in step — this app now has three modules that compound a pot forward (`auto-invest`, `forecast`, `fire`) and they must not drift.
- "Will my money last?" is answered two ways because they answer different questions. `sustainable` is exact and analytic — one month's growth covers one month's income (`pot × r ≥ income/12`), so the pot never falls at all — while `months` is the simulated count of months funded *in full*. The distinction matters at the boundary README.md's own 4% rule sits on: drawing 4% from a pot growing at 4% is **not** sustainable, because each month's income leaves before it can compound. A test pins that, and the panel says it in words when a pot runs dry.
- The runway counts only months that paid a full withdrawal. The month the pot empties pays out whatever was left (£500 of a £1,000 draw) and appears in the series with `income` below the monthly figure — visible on the chart-to-be, excluded from "your money lasts N years", which would otherwise round a partial month up into a full one.
- **The pot is what funds drawdown, not net worth.** `investablePot` sums the investment holdings that count towards net worth and ignores debts entirely — a mortgage is not a negative income stream you can draw down, and the house behind it is not one you can sell a twelfth of each month. `debt.js`/`forecast.js` still own net worth. `monthlySavingFromHoldings` restates every contribution schedule as a monthly equivalent (£900 quarterly is £300/month; a `one_off` was paid once and counts as nothing).
- `fireStartingPoint` delegates its anchoring to `forecast.js`'s `positionFromEntries` rather than re-deriving "the latest snapshot" itself, so the FIRE tab and the forecast tab can never disagree about which month they are projecting from.
- Bounded the Coast FIRE search at the retirement date, which was a bug found by actually rendering the panel rather than by reading the code. Unbounded, the coast threshold past retirement is simply the magic number, so a plan retiring at 67 and only reaching its number at 94 was reporting "Coasting starts May 2080 — you could stop saving entirely and still hit your number by 67". Technically a threshold crossing; read as good news for a plan that misses by 27 years. Coasting now means "a month you could stop saving in and still arrive by the retirement date", and a plan with no such month says so.
- The retirement route hydrates the store (#5) and seeds the sliders from `profile` (`retirement_target`, `monthly_contribution`, `growth_rate`, `inflation_rate`, `retirement_age`, `dob_year`), but writes nothing back — the same session-only convention the forecast tab's sliders follow, and the reason the tab waits for hydration before rendering rather than seeding from defaults and re-seeding later.
- Added the repo's first component test. Earlier entries record component testing as unavailable here (no jsdom, `svelte/server` reportedly unresolvable) — it does resolve now from a plain vitest file, since vitest already transforms `.svelte` through the configured `sveltekit()` plugin. `FireCalculator.test.js` renders the panel server-side and asserts the sentences a user actually reads, which is what caught the coast bug above. It can only assert the initial render, not post-input state; the arithmetic behind every update is covered directly in `fire.test.js`.
- 65 new tests (58 in `fire.test.js`, 7 in `FireCalculator.test.js`), 562 total, up from 497. `npm run lint`, `npm run check` (0 errors, same 2 pre-existing `<slot>` warnings) and `npm run build` all clean.

**Trade-offs / deviations from prompt:**
- README.md's "accumulation and drawdown charts" are sampled tables with inline bars, not charts — #12 (LayerChart) is still open, the same position #18/#20/#21 left their own chart-shaped features in. `projectAccumulation`/`projectDrawdown` return plain `{offset, month, year, value, …}` point arrays written against no chart library, so #12 can hand them to a chart component without this issue inventing plotting maths first.
- **Tax is not modelled.** The income figures are what leaves the pot, not what reaches a bank account: a £30,000 drawdown from a SIPP is taxable, from an ISA is not, and this tab treats one pot with no wrapper. `Investment.wrapper` is on file and the tax calculator is M4, so the seam exists — but netting tax off here before `tax.js` exists would have meant inventing a second set of UK bands. Said plainly in the panel's footnote rather than left for the user to assume.
- **The State Pension is not netted off the target income**, which overstates the pot needed for anyone entitled to it — £241.30/week (2026/27) is roughly £12,500 a year, about £313,000 of magic number at 4%. That is #31's job (projection from NI qualifying years) and #33's (the income stream builder); wiring a hard-coded State Pension in here would duplicate both.
- Pensions, property and physical assets are not in the pot — only `monthly_entries[].investments`. A SIPP tracked in `pensions[]` (#29, not yet built) is invisible to this tab today even though it is exactly the kind of pot that funds FIRE. Deliberate: those collections have no UI yet, and quietly summing them in would produce a number the user cannot see the components of.
- One constant real return, applied every month, in both accumulation and drawdown. No sequence-of-returns risk, no crash overlay (the stress test is `stress-test.js`, written against a `Forecast`, not against these series), no variable-withdrawal or guardrail strategies. Monte Carlo is explicitly Phase 2 in README.md and is where the honest version of "will my money last" eventually lives; this is the deterministic answer, which is what the issue asks for.
- Drawdown takes a flat real income for life. Real retirement spending is not flat (it typically falls through the 70s and rises again with care costs), and the model has no way to say so.
- Ages are typed into the panel, not stored. `Profile` has `dob_year`/`retirement_age` and they seed the fields when present, but nothing in the app can *edit* a profile yet (#5 landed the store, not a form), so "your age now", "retiring at" and "money must last to" are session-only — as is the "invested today"/"saved each month" fallback pair shown when no snapshot exists. Nothing this tab collects is written back to the document.
- The saving slider is disabled when a snapshot exists, showing the contributions actually recorded rather than letting the user drag a number that the next re-render would overwrite. The trade-off is that "what if I saved £200 more?" needs the holdings edited on the dashboard — a per-scenario override would be a better answer and is not here.
- The tab renders "Loading your saved data…" until `hydrateAppData()` resolves, unlike the dashboard, which renders immediately and fills in. That is because the sliders' initial positions come from the profile and `$state` initialisers only run once: rendering first would seed every control from the default profile and silently ignore the stored one.
- `npm test` still isn't in `.github/workflows/ci.yml` (flagged in #2's and #3's entries; the app token lacks `workflows` permission), so the 562 tests — including the new component test — run locally but not on the PR. `build`/`check`/`lint` remain the only CI gates.

## Income tax bands + take-home calculation (E/W/NI + Scotland, 2026/27) — 2026-08-07
<!-- METRICS:income-tax-bands-take-home-calculation-e-w-ni-scotland-2026-27 -->
- **Execution Duration:** __DURATION__ seconds
- **Model:** __MODEL__
- **Turns:** __TURNS__
- **Input Tokens:** __INPUT_TOKENS__
- **Output Tokens:** __OUTPUT_TOKENS__
- **Estimated Cost:** $__COST__ (from Claude Code's reported total_cost_usd)
- **Ballpark cost, comparable GPT-5-tier model:** $__GPT_BALLPARK__ (illustrative -- see methodology note above)
- **Ballpark cost, comparable Gemini-tier model:** $__GEMINI_BALLPARK__ (illustrative -- see methodology note above)

**Decisions:**
- **Sourced the thresholds rather than recalling them, and it mattered.** The issue says "using the 2026/27 tax year figures in README.md", but README.md gives no band figures at all — only the taper range (£100k–£125,140), the HICBC thresholds, ISA allowances, the State Pension and the IHT nil-rate bands. So the numbers had to come from somewhere else, and CLAUDE.md's own rule ("match README.md's stated figures exactly rather than substituting general knowledge, since UK allowances change every tax year") is precisely the warning against filling that gap from memory. Fetched HMRC's "Income Tax rates and allowances for current and previous tax years" (gov.uk, 2026 to 2027 column, updated 6 April 2026) directly. The E/W/NI ladder was as expected; **the Scottish one was not** — 2026/27 uprated the starter and basic bands substantially (starter now runs to £3,967 of taxable income, basic to £16,956, versus £2,827/£14,921 in 2025/26), and a from-memory implementation would have shipped last year's bands under this year's label. Both ladders are pinned figure-for-figure in `tax.test.js` so a future tax year is a visible, deliberate edit rather than a silent drift.
- **Bands are stored on taxable income, not gross salary** — HMRC's own "income after allowances" framing, and convention (1) of the module. Storing them on gross would make every boundary move as the allowance tapers, which is the single easiest way to get the £100k–£125,140 region wrong. The cost is that the on-screen band ranges read as "£0 – £37,700" rather than "£12,571 – £50,270", so the table carries a sentence explaining what the numbers are measured against, and a test asserts that adding the full allowance back to the four Scottish ceilings reproduces the gross-income thresholds the Scottish Government publishes (£16,537 / £29,526 / £43,662 / £75,000). That test is the real cross-check on the transcription: two of those four are widely-quoted policy figures, so a mistyped taxable-income threshold fails loudly instead of quietly costing someone £200.
- **Each band is a half-open slice `[from, to)`.** HMRC writes "up to £37,700" then "£37,701 to £125,140"; as slices that is `[0, 37700)` and `[37700, 125140)`, which agrees at every whole pound and stays correct for the pennies in between, where the published whole-pound wording says nothing. Tests pin both sides of the boundary (£37,700 is entirely basic rate, £37,701 spills exactly one pound over).
- **Built the personal allowance taper here rather than deferring it to #27**, which is titled "Salary sacrifice + 60% personal allowance taper". The taper is not an optional extra on top of the bands — it *is* why the additional and top rates start at £125,140, and without it every salary above £100,000 gets a wrong answer. Issue #23's own instruction is "get the exact thresholds right — this is the core of the tax tab", which cannot be honoured while knowingly mis-taxing the top of the ladder. Salary sacrifice, the genuinely separable half of #27, is not built: `tax.js` takes one income figure and documents it as adjusted net income (convention 5), so #27 subtracts sacrificed pay before calling and everything downstream follows with no change here.
- **`marginalTaxRate` derives README.md's 60% band rather than special-casing it.** Inside the taper each extra £1 is taxed at the band rate *and* destroys 50p of allowance, which is then taxed at the same rate — so the rate is multiplied by 1.5. That falls out as 60% in England/Wales/NI and, because the surrounding Scottish band is the 45% advanced rate, **67.5% in Scotland**, a figure nothing in the spec mentions but which is what a Scottish taxpayer on £110,000 actually faces. A test checks the analytic answer against what the next £100 of income observably costs, for every region and six incomes, rather than trusting the derivation to stay in step with `incomeTax`.
- **The breakdown accounts for every pound earned, not just the taxed ones.** `allowance.used` plus every band's `amount` sums to gross income exactly, and the table renders the allowance as its own 0% row above the ladder with a "Total" foot that reconciles. A band-by-band view whose rows don't add up to the salary invites the reader to assume the missing pounds went somewhere; a test asserts the reconciliation across five incomes in both regions.
- **Every band in the ladder is returned, including the ones the income never reaches** (`amount: 0`, greyed in the table). Showing only the bands used hides the thing a tax table is most often consulted for — where the next raise lands.
- Added `compareRegions`, and a one-line "on £62,000 you'd pay £1,790.05 less a year in England, Wales & Northern Ireland" under the table. Not in the issue text, but "England/Wales/NI **and** Scotland bands" is one feature with two ladders, and the cross-border comparison is the question having both actually answers.
- **"Take-home" is labelled for what it is.** National Insurance is not modelled — it appears nowhere in README.md's tax feature list and has no issue in Milestone 4 (see the trade-offs below) — so the tile reads "After income tax", its subtitle says "before NI and any student loan", and the footnote states plainly that this is not net pay. The alternative, printing a take-home figure that silently omits the £2,994 of employee NI due on a £50,000 salary, would have been a worse kind of wrong than an honestly narrow one.
- 55 new tests (45 in `tax.test.js`, 10 in `TaxCalculator.test.js`), 617 total, up from 562. `npm run lint`, `npm run check` (0 errors, same 2 pre-existing `<slot>` warnings) and `npm run build` all clean. Also driven for real, as #69/#68 did: `npm run build && npm run preview` plus a throwaway `puppeteer-core` script against system Chromium (both removed afterwards, `package.json`/lockfile untouched). Confirmed against hand-worked HMRC figures at £9,000 (nothing due, 0% marginal), £50,000 in both regions (£7,486 vs £8,982.05, and the £1,496.05 gap in the comparison line), £110,000 in both (60% and 67.5% marginal, allowance down to £7,570), £150,000 (£53,703 — HMRC's own published figure for that salary) and £200,000, plus clearing the field to reach the validation message, dragging the slider to its £200,000 ceiling, and switching region with the salary held. No console errors.

**Trade-offs / deviations from prompt:**
- **National Insurance is not deducted, so nothing here is net pay.** This is the largest honest gap: employee Class 1 NI on a £50,000 salary is £2,994.40 (8% between the £12,570 primary threshold and the £50,270 upper earnings limit, 2% above), and this tab shows none of it. Not a sourcing problem — the figures sit in the same gov.uk publication the bands came from, and were checked while writing this — but a scope one: README.md's "UK Income Tax Calculator (2026/27)" enumerates eight features and NI is not among them, and no issue in Milestone 4 covers it. Folding in a whole second contribution regime nobody asked for would also have made this PR the place where NI's own edge cases (directors' annualisation, per-pay-period rather than annual assessment, the married women's reduced rate) got decided in passing. Worth its own issue; the module's convention (4) and the tab's footnote both say so rather than leaving the user to discover it.
