- **The taper overlaps #27's title.** #27 is "Salary sacrifice + 60% personal allowance taper" and half of it is now done. Same shape as the #9/#2 overlap this journal already records: the backlog decomposed one README bullet into issues that cannot actually be built independently. Left #27 open rather than editing it — salary sacrifice is real remaining work there, and `personalAllowance`/`inAllowanceTaper`/`marginalTaxRate` are the seams it plugs into.
- **Income is treated as adjusted net income, and nothing enforces that.** The taper is legally assessed on adjusted net income, which is gross pay less pension relief, gift aid and the rest. The tab's field says "Gross annual income" and its footnote asks the user to enter income already net of salary sacrifice — an instruction, not a calculation. #27 replaces the instruction with the arithmetic.
- **Savings and dividend income are out.** The starting rate for savings (0% on up to £5,000) and the £500 dividend allowance both sit in the same HMRC table the bands came from, and the dividend rates (10.75%/35.75%) are already in README.md — but under the *Dividend Income Planner*, which is its own tab and its own issue. This module taxes one stream of earned income; layering savings and dividends on top of it needs an ordering rule between the streams that no open issue has asked for yet.
- **No Marriage Allowance, HICBC, student loans, blind person's allowance or married couple's allowance** — #25, #24 and #26 respectively for the first three, and the last two are in no issue at all. Each would change the allowance or add a charge on top of the tax computed here; the module exposes `personalAllowance` separately from the band arithmetic so they have somewhere to attach.
- **The band table is a table, not a chart.** #12 (LayerChart) is still open, so a stacked bar of where each pound went — the obvious visual for this — isn't here. `takeHomeBreakdown().bands` is a plain array of `{label, rate, amount, tax}` written against no chart library, so #12 can plot it without inventing the maths first. Same position #18/#20/#21/#22 left their own chart-shaped features in.
- **Nothing is written back to the profile.** The salary and region seed from `profile.gross_salary`/`profile.tax_region` and are then session-only, matching the forecast and retirement tabs — but there is still no form anywhere in the app that can *edit* a profile (#5 landed the store, not a form), so a user with no stored salary retypes it on every visit. The tab opens on £50,000 rather than £0 so it isn't a page of zeroes on first load.
- **Only the 2026/27 year exists.** `TAX_YEAR` is a constant and the ladders are module-level constants, not a per-year lookup. Adding 2027/28 means a second table and a year selector; building that seam now would be speculative structure for a personal-use app that has never needed a prior year.
- `npm test` still isn't in `.github/workflows/ci.yml` (flagged since #2; the app token lacks `workflows` permission), so all 617 tests run locally but not on the PR. `build`/`check`/`lint` remain the only CI gates.

## High Income Child Benefit Charge (HICBC) — 2026-08-07
<!-- METRICS:high-income-child-benefit-charge-hicbc -->
- **Execution Duration:** __DURATION__ seconds
- **Model:** __MODEL__
- **Turns:** __TURNS__
- **Input Tokens:** __INPUT_TOKENS__
- **Output Tokens:** __OUTPUT_TOKENS__
- **Estimated Cost:** $__COST__ (from Claude Code's reported total_cost_usd)
- **Ballpark cost, comparable GPT-5-tier model:** $__GPT_BALLPARK__ (illustrative -- see methodology note above)
- **Ballpark cost, comparable Gemini-tier model:** $__GEMINI_BALLPARK__ (illustrative -- see methodology note above)

**Decisions:**
- **HICBC got its own module, `src/lib/hicbc.js`, rather than a section of `tax.js`.** It is not income tax: it is a separate charge, assessed on one individual, that claws back a benefit somebody else in the household may have received, and it turns on facts `tax.js` has no business knowing (how many children are claimed for, whether the payments are being taken, what a partner earns). The two modules meet in exactly one place — the rate on the next pound — so `hicbc.js` imports `marginalTaxRate`/`takeHomePay` from `tax.js` and nothing goes the other way. Same header-of-conventions shape every other `$lib` module has, one composite (`childBenefitSummary`) behind every figure the panel shows.
- **The Child Benefit weekly rates had to come from outside the repo, and this run had no way to check them.** README.md states HICBC's £60,000/£80,000 thresholds but not the benefit those thresholds claw back — and the charge is a *percentage of the benefit*, so the rates are unavoidable. `WebFetch` and `WebSearch` were both unavailable in this run (permission not granted; the two gov.uk pages were the first thing attempted), so `CHILD_BENEFIT_ELDEST_WEEKLY = 27.05` and `CHILD_BENEFIT_ADDITIONAL_WEEKLY = 17.90` are stated from knowledge rather than sourced, which is precisely the thing #23's entry congratulated itself for avoiding. Handled the way an unverifiable figure should be rather than by pretending it was verified: both constants carry a doc comment saying where they come from, why they are not in README.md, and how to re-check them; `hicbc.test.js` pins them so a correction is a one-line, visible edit; and the trade-offs below flag it as the one thing in this PR a reader should check before trusting a number on screen. Everything else — the £60k threshold, the £80k full clawback, the 1%-per-£200 step — is README.md's own stated rule, and a test asserts the three are mutually consistent (`(80,000 − 60,000) / 200 = 100` points exactly), so a future rules change that moved one boundary alone fails loudly instead of silently clawing back 90% or 110% of the benefit.
- **The percentage is a whole number and it steps every £200.** `floor((income − 60,000) / 200)`, capped at 100: £60,199 and £60,000 both give 0%, and the first pound of charge appears at £60,200. Tests pin both sides of that edge and of the £79,999 → 99% / £80,000 → 100% one. The consequence is that the "marginal rate" figure is a staircase, not a slope, so `hicbcMarginalRate` documents itself as the *average gradient across a step* (benefit ÷ £200) — the only reading that answers "what does a pay rise cost me", which is why the figure is on screen. A test asserts the quoted gradient matches what £200 more income observably costs for one to four children, to within the penny that rounding either charge introduces, and another pins the awkward truth behind it: the charge at £70,001 is identical to the charge at £70,000.
- **The charge follows the higher earner, so the panel asks what a partner earns.** HICBC is not a household charge and not the claimant's charge — a £70,000 earner whose partner earns £75,000 owes nothing, and the panel says so and leaves their own take-home untouched. That is the single most misunderstood part of the rule, and modelling only "your" income would have produced a confidently wrong answer for every dual-income couple. `chargeBearer` returns `'you' | 'partner' | 'neither'`, and `'neither'` covers both "nobody is over £60,000" and "no benefit is being received", since either alone means there is nothing to allocate. On an exact tie the module treats the charge as the user's and both the module header and the panel's footnote say plainly that the real tie-break turns on who receives the benefit, which this app does not track.
- **The panel shares the tax tab's salary rather than owning a second one.** HICBC is assessed on adjusted net income — the same figure the bands above are worked on — so two income fields on one tab would let it hold two answers to one question. `TaxCalculator.svelte` therefore renders `ChildBenefitCharge` as a second card and passes `income`/`region` down as props; the child panel owns only the three facts `tax.js` cannot know. It also disappears with the tax card when the salary field is cleared, rather than quietly answering for £0.
- **The marginal rate is the headline the tab exists for.** A two-child family on £70,000 sees 40% in the band table and is actually losing **51.69%** of the next £200 — 40% tax plus £2,337.40 ÷ £200 of clawback — and in Scotland **53.69%**, because the surrounding band is 42%. The tile shows the combination with its two components spelled out underneath, and the sentence beneath the table says which figure the band table was hiding. Tests pin both regions.
- **Opting out is modelled as a toggle with the counterfactual next to it, not as an absence.** Stopping the payments removes the charge, so the panel would otherwise just go blank and leave the user to guess; instead it computes the same household with the payments switched back on and reports what taking them would be worth (£1,169 a year at £70,000 with two children, and neutral at £85,000). It also draws the distinction the money hides: it is the *claim*, not the payments, that earns National Insurance credits towards a State Pension and gets the child an NI number at 16, which is why claiming and opting out is the right move at £80,000+ and never a loss below it. A test asserts the "never a loss" property rather than only asserting the copy.
- **Two figures that fall straight out of the rules and are worth more than the charge itself:** `incomeToClearCharge` (how far adjusted net income has to fall to clear the charge entirely — the pension-contribution or salary-sacrifice question every parent in this band actually asks) and `takeHomeAfterCharge` (income less income tax less any charge *you* personally owe, which is untouched when the partner is the one liable).
- **`annualBenefit` can be passed instead of a count of children.** A child born in November or one who left full-time education in June is not a count of children, and a part-year claim is the common real case that a rates-times-52 model gets wrong. The composite takes the override, drives the weekly and four-weekly figures from it too (so the panel can't show a full-year rate beside a part-year charge), and #25 has a seam to attach a real Child Benefit claim to later. Not exposed as a control — three inputs is already the most this panel should ask for.
- 57 new tests (45 in `hicbc.test.js`, 12 in `ChildBenefitCharge.test.js`), 674 total, up from 617. `npm run lint`, `npm run check` (0 errors, same 2 pre-existing `<slot>` warnings) and `npm run build` all clean. Also driven for real, as #23/#68/#69 did: `npm run build && npm run preview` plus a throwaway `puppeteer-core` script against system Chromium (both removed afterwards, `package.json`/lockfile untouched). Confirmed the panel follows the tax card's salary through £50,000 (no charge, £10,000 of headroom, £1,406.60 kept), £70,000 with two children (50%, £1,168.70 charged, 51.69% marginal), the same salary with a partner on £78,000 (charge moves to them at 90%, own take-home untouched), £85,000 (100%, benefit cancelled out, claim-and-opt-out advice), payments stopped (counterfactual shown), Scotland at £70,000 (53.69%), and clearing the salary field entirely (both cards give way to the validation message). No console errors.

**Trade-offs / deviations from prompt:**
- **The two Child Benefit weekly rates are the one unverified thing in this PR.** £27.05 (eldest/only) and £17.90 (each additional) are the 2026/27 figures as best known, being the 2025/26 rates of £26.05/£17.25 uprated by the previous September's CPI and rounded to the nearest 5p — but no gov.uk page was reachable from this run to confirm them, and CLAUDE.md is explicit that UK allowances change every April and should not be filled in from general knowledge. If they are wrong, every pound figure on the panel is wrong by the same proportion while every *percentage* stays right (the thresholds and the step are README.md's own). Check `CHILD_BENEFIT_ELDEST_WEEKLY`/`CHILD_BENEFIT_ADDITIONAL_WEEKLY` against gov.uk's "Child Benefit rates" page — that is a two-minute correction to two constants and two test assertions, and nothing else moves.
- **This overlaps #25 ("Marriage Allowance + Child Benefit"), the same way #23 overlapped #27.** The charge is a percentage of Child Benefit, so the entitlement arithmetic could not wait for its own issue. What is built here is only what the charge needs: rates, a count of children, a full-year (or supplied) entitlement. What #25 still owns is everything about the benefit as a thing in its own right — who can claim, the claim/opt-out mechanics as a stored decision rather than a checkbox, the interaction with NI credits for a parent at home, and Marriage Allowance, which is untouched. Left #25 open rather than editing it.
- **Nothing is persisted, and `Profile` was deliberately not widened.** Children, a Child Benefit claim and a partner's income are all session-only, so a user retypes them on every visit. README.md's data model lists none of the three, household/partner planning is Phase 2, and there is still no form anywhere in the app that can edit a profile (#5 landed the store, not a form) — so adding fields would have meant a schema change, a `normaliseAppData` migration and a WealthR-CSV-compatibility question, all for data nothing can yet save. The panel's props (`children`, `partnerIncome`, `claiming`) are the seam a future profile form or household lens plugs into.
- **"Adjusted net income" is still an instruction rather than a calculation** — inherited from #23 and unchanged. Pension contributions given relief at source, salary sacrifice and Gift Aid all reduce the figure the charge is assessed on, and the footnote asks the user to enter incomes already net of them. That matters more here than it did on the band table: reducing adjusted net income is the *standard* response to this charge, and the panel quotes how much of a reduction would clear it while being unable to model the contribution that does the reducing. #27 is where that becomes arithmetic.
- **The partner is one number, not a person.** No check that only one Child Benefit claim exists across the couple, no partner tax calculation (their own bands, their own take-home), no household lens — and on an exact tie the charge is assigned to the user, where the law looks at who receives the benefit. Anything more is #25's and Phase 2's "Household / Partner Planning".
- **Full-year circumstances only, in the UI.** The panel asks for a count of children and assumes 52 weeks of entitlement; a child born mid-year, a child ageing out, or a claim started in September all need `childBenefitSummary`'s `annualBenefit` override, which is tested but has no control on screen. Adding a fourth input for it would have bought accuracy in an uncommon case at the cost of legibility in the common one.
- **Collection mechanics are not modelled.** The charge is shown as an annual amount; whether it arrives via Self Assessment or through a PAYE coding notice, and when it falls due, is not. Nor is the charge rounded down to whole pounds the way a tax computation may present it — everything here is to the penny, matching the rest of the app, which can leave a figure up to £1 above what a real bill shows.
- **National Insurance is still absent, so even the combined marginal rate understates the truth.** The 51.69% shown for a two-child family on £70,000 does not include the 2% employee NI on that pound. Same position (and same reason) as #23: NI is in no issue in Milestone 4 and appears nowhere in README.md's feature list. It is now understating two figures rather than one, which strengthens the case for giving it its own issue.
- **The panel is a card, not a chart.** The obvious visual — the marginal-rate spike between £60,000 and £80,000, deepening with each child — needs #12 (LayerChart), still open. `childBenefitSummary` returns plain numbers written against no chart library, so #12 can plot the curve without re-deriving it.
- **`TaxCalculator` renders the HICBC card as a sibling**, which is a slightly unusual shape for a component: the tab's two cards come out of one component rather than being composed by the route. The alternative was lifting the salary and region into `src/routes/tax/+page.svelte` and passing them down to both, which is cleaner in the abstract but restructures #23's component and its tests to add a feature beside it. Chose the additive change; the seam to invert it later is one prop each.
- `npm test` still isn't in `.github/workflows/ci.yml` (flagged since #2; the app token lacks `workflows` permission), so all 674 tests run locally but not on the PR. `build`/`check`/`lint` remain the only CI gates.

## Marriage Allowance + Child Benefit — 2026-08-07
<!-- METRICS:marriage-allowance-child-benefit -->
- **Execution Duration:** __DURATION__ seconds
- **Model:** __MODEL__
- **Turns:** __TURNS__
- **Input Tokens:** __INPUT_TOKENS__
- **Output Tokens:** __OUTPUT_TOKENS__
- **Estimated Cost:** $__COST__ (from Claude Code's reported total_cost_usd)
- **Ballpark cost, comparable GPT-5-tier model:** $__GPT_BALLPARK__ (illustrative -- see methodology note above)
- **Ballpark cost, comparable Gemini-tier model:** $__GEMINI_BALLPARK__ (illustrative -- see methodology note above)

**Decisions:**
- **Marriage Allowance got its own module, `src/lib/marriage-allowance.js`, for the same reason HICBC did (#24): it isn't a `tax.js` band option, it's an adjustment between *two* incomes.** `tax.js` answers "what does one income owe"; this answers "what do two incomes owe each other". The module imports `bandsFor`, `incomeTax`, `PERSONAL_ALLOWANCE` and `normaliseTaxRegion` from `tax.js` and nothing goes the other way — same one-directional dependency shape as `hicbc.js`.
- **Every figure in this module is derived from `tax.js`'s own constants, and none of it needed outside verification.** That's the opposite position from #24's Child Benefit rates, which had to be taken on trust because README.md doesn't state them. Marriage Allowance's £1,260 transfer *is* stated as a rule (10% of the Personal Allowance, rounded up to the nearest £10) rather than as a bare figure, so `transferableAllowance()` recomputes it from `PERSONAL_ALLOWANCE` instead of hardcoding £1,260 as a second source of truth — if the frozen allowance ever moves, this module moves with it. A test asserts the £1,257-before-rounding intermediate value, not just the £1,260 result, so the rounding step itself is pinned.
- **The recipient's eligibility threshold reuses `tax.js`'s band ladder rather than hardcoding £50,270/£43,662.** Both the England/Wales/NI and Scottish ladders name their first disqualifying band `'higher'` — the same fact `marginalTaxRate`'s 60%/67.5% derivation already leans on (#23's journal entry) — so `higherRateThreshold(region)` just reads that band's `from` out of `bandsFor` and adds the Personal Allowance back (`bandsFor` stores boundaries on taxable income, not gross). Two well-known published thresholds fall out as a consequence of the band table rather than being retyped, and a Scottish band uprating this run didn't have to touch this module at all.
- **The transferor's cost is computed by reusing `incomeTax`, not by re-deriving band logic.** Giving up £1,260 of allowance is arithmetically identical to earning £1,260 more against the *original* allowance, because eligibility guarantees the transferor's income is under the Personal Allowance, where the taper cannot apply. So `transferorExtraTax` is just `incomeTax(income + transfer, region) − incomeTax(income, region)`. The payoff: at the Personal Allowance boundary this correctly lands in Scotland's 19% starter band (£239.40) rather than assuming the 20% basic rate everywhere, without the module knowing Scotland has a starter band at all — a test pins that exact figure against the hand-worked one.
- **The recipient's reduction is a fixed 20% of the transfer, not their marginal rate — and that fixed rate is what makes Scotland a small net gain rather than a breakeven.** At the Personal Allowance boundary in England/Wales/NI, the £252 fixed reduction is exactly cancelled by the transferor's £252 of extra tax (both at 20%) — a genuine breakeven, not a rounding artefact, and a test asserts it lands at exactly zero. In Scotland the transferor's cost at that same boundary is only £239.40 (19% starter rate) against the same fixed £252 reduction, so the couple nets £12.60. Both are tested, because the fixed-vs-marginal distinction is easy to get backwards and produce a false loss.
- **The reduction is capped at what the recipient actually owes, not just at £252.** A recipient earning £13,000 owes only £86 of tax; `recipientTaxReduction` returns `min(252, liability)`, so the summary never claims a saving larger than the bill it's supposedly reducing. Tested directly against `tax.js`'s own `incomeTax(13_000, ...)`.
- **The lower earner is always auto-determined as the transferor, from the two incomes — not a control the user sets.** `marriageAllowanceSummary` only pays out in that direction (the higher earner fails `recipientEligible` if the roles are reversed at any realistic income gap), so `MarriageAllowance.svelte` compares `income` and `partnerIncome` and decides "You"/"Your partner" itself, the same way `ChildBenefitCharge` decides who bears the HICBC charge rather than asking.
- **`partnerIncome` moved out of `ChildBenefitCharge` and into `TaxCalculator`, shared via `bind:` with the new panel — the actual "Child Benefit entry/display" half of this issue's title.** #24 shipped Child Benefit's own entry/display (children count, claiming toggle, partner income) inside `ChildBenefitCharge`, but that left "what does a partner earn" as a question asked once per card. Once Marriage Allowance needed the same fact, asking it twice on one tab would have been the worse interface — a user correcting a partner's payslip figure would have had to remember to update it in two places. `ChildBenefitCharge`'s own `partnerIncome` prop became `$bindable`, `TaxCalculator` now owns the single `let partnerIncome = $state(0)`, and both cards bind to it. Existing HICBC behaviour and its 12 component tests are unchanged — `$bindable(0)` accepts the same initial-value prop the old local `$state` did, so nothing about `ChildBenefitCharge`'s own tests needed to change.
- **A `married` checkbox gates the whole panel, defaulting unchecked, specifically to stop a false-positive saving.** Unlike Child Benefit, Marriage Allowance requires being married or in a civil partnership — a fact `marriage-allowance.js`'s eligibility rules have no way to know, since they work on any two incomes. Without the gate, a user with no partner at all would see `partnerIncome`'s "0 if you have no partner" default (#24's own convention) read as a non-earning *spouse*, and the panel would report a false £252 household saving for a person who isn't married. Defaulting the checkbox to unchecked means the panel says nothing numeric — just an explanation of the eligibility rule — until the user actively confirms it applies to them.
- 41 new tests (29 in `marriage-allowance.test.js`, 11 in `MarriageAllowance.test.js`, 1 added to `TaxCalculator.test.js` confirming both cards render), 715 total, up from 674. `npm run lint`, `npm run check` (0 errors, same 2 pre-existing `<slot>` warnings) and `npm run build` all clean. Also driven for real, as #23/#24/#68/#69 did: `npm run build && npm run preview` plus a throwaway `puppeteer-core` script against system Chromium (both removed afterwards, `package.json`/lockfile untouched) — confirmed the unmarried gate on load, the £1,260/£252 figures appearing once "married" is ticked with a £40,000/£5,000 pair, the partner-income field genuinely shared in both directions between the Marriage Allowance and Child Benefit cards (editing one updates the other live), the "already pays higher-rate tax" refusal at £65,000/£5,000, and the Scottish £43,662 threshold with the £12.60→£13 boundary case. No console errors.

**Trade-offs / deviations from prompt:**
- **The two panels share an input but not a calculation.** `partnerIncome` is now typed once, but `ChildBenefitCharge` and `MarriageAllowance` still call `childBenefitSummary`/`marriageAllowanceSummary` independently — there is no joint household object, and a couple who are both claiming Child Benefit and applying for Marriage Allowance sees two separate cards reach two separate answers rather than one combined household tax position. README.md's "Household / Partner Planning" (Phase 2) is the real fix; this is a narrower, honest step (one shared fact, not a joint model) in that direction.
- **One region for the couple.** `marriageAllowanceSummary` takes a single `region` and uses it for both the recipient's eligibility threshold and, via `transferorExtraTax`'s reuse of `incomeTax`, the transferor's own cost — so a couple actually resident in different nations (rare, but not impossible: one partner working in Scotland, the other in England) gets the recipient's region applied to both sides. The module's JSDoc says so explicitly and documents the escape hatch (call `transferorExtraTax` directly with the transferor's own region); `MarriageAllowance.svelte` doesn't expose that as a second region selector, since two region dropdowns on a tab that already has one would answer a question approximately nobody building this app has.
- **Nothing is persisted, and marital status was deliberately not added to `Profile`.** `married`, `claiming` and the shared `partnerIncome` are all session-only, the same position #24 took on children and partner income for the same reasons: README.md's data model has no partner field at all, household planning is Phase 2, and there's still no profile-editing form anywhere in the app (#5 landed the store, not a form) for a persisted field to round-trip through. `MarriageAllowance.svelte`'s props are the seam a future household lens plugs into.
- **Application mechanics aren't modelled.** Marriage Allowance can be backdated up to four tax years and, once granted, auto-renews until cancelled — none of that is here. The panel answers "what would this year be worth", which is the question a single-tax-year calculator can actually answer; a multi-year backdating claim needs the app to know about prior years at all, which nothing in this codebase does yet (#23's journal entry flagged the same gap for `TAX_YEAR` itself).
- **National Insurance is still absent**, same position as #23/#24: every "extra tax" and "saving" figure here is income tax only. A transferor whose extra £1,260 of taxable income also crosses an NI threshold would see a bigger real-world cost than `transferorExtraTax` reports. No issue in Milestone 4 covers NI; this is the third module now understating a marginal cost for the same reason.
- **The panel is a card, not a chart**, same position as #23/#24: the obvious visual (net benefit against the transferor's income, cut off sharply at the Personal Allowance) needs #12 (LayerChart), still open. `marriageAllowanceSummary` returns plain numbers written against no chart library.
- **Equal incomes break the tie arbitrarily.** When `income === partnerIncome`, `youAreTransferor` reads `<=` as true and "you" transfer — harmless (the transfer nets to the same household total either way when both incomes are identical) but not something a user asked for; documented in the component's header rather than surfaced as a UI choice, since a tie is a single point with no wrong answer to get right.
- `npm test` still isn't in `.github/workflows/ci.yml` (flagged since #2; the app token lacks `workflows` permission), so all 715 tests run locally but not on the PR. `build`/`check`/`lint` remain the only CI gates.

## Student Loan plans 1, 2, 4, 5, PG — 2026-08-07
<!-- METRICS:student-loan-plans-1-2-4-5-pg -->
- **Execution Duration:** __DURATION__ seconds
- **Model:** __MODEL__
- **Turns:** __TURNS__
- **Input Tokens:** __INPUT_TOKENS__
- **Output Tokens:** __OUTPUT_TOKENS__
- **Estimated Cost:** $__COST__ (from Claude Code's reported total_cost_usd)
- **Ballpark cost, comparable GPT-5-tier model:** $__GPT_BALLPARK__ (illustrative -- see methodology note above)
- **Ballpark cost, comparable Gemini-tier model:** $__GEMINI_BALLPARK__ (illustrative -- see methodology note above)

**Decisions:**
- **Student Loan repayments got their own module, `src/lib/student-loan.js`, for the same reason HICBC and Marriage Allowance did (#24/#25): a repayment is not a `tax.js` band option, it is a separate deduction with its own thresholds that only borrows `tax.js` for the one place the two meet — the income tax marginal rate, for the combined "what does a raise really cost" figure.** The module imports `marginalTaxRate`, `normaliseTaxRegion` and `takeHomePay` from `tax.js` and nothing goes the other way, the same one-directional shape every other tax-tab module already has.
- **A plan and a Postgraduate Loan are modelled as two independent facts, not one choice of five.** README.md lists "plans 1, 2, 4, 5, PG" as five items, but they aren't five mutually-exclusive options: Plan 1/2/4/5 cover different undergraduate cohorts and nobody is on more than one for the same loan, while a Postgraduate Loan is a separate loan for a separate (often later) course and routinely stacks with whichever undergraduate plan is still being repaid. Modelling this as a single five-way `plan` field would have made "Plan 2 and also a Postgraduate Loan" — the single most common multi-loan case — inexpressible. `studentLoanSummary` therefore takes `plan` (one of the four, or `'none'`) and `postgraduate` (a flag) separately, and sums whichever are active, each against its own threshold: 9% above £28,470 *and* 6% above £21,000, not 15% above one merged figure. A test asserts the summed answer is different from (and correct against) the wrong merged-threshold calculation, specifically to catch a regression toward the easier-looking but wrong shape.
- **Repayments round down to the whole pound, not to the penny, which is different from every other module on this tab.** `tax.js`/`hicbc.js`/`marriage-allowance.js` all round to whole pence; Student Loan repayments are what SLC actually rounds down to the pound through payroll, so `repaymentForLoan` uses `Math.floor` rather than `tax.js`'s round-to-pence helper. A test pins the distinction directly: £999 of income over the Postgraduate threshold at 6% is £59.94, which this module reports as £59, not £60 or £59.94.
- **The annual figure is explicitly not a payroll simulation, and the module says so rather than implying precision it doesn't have.** SLC calculates and rounds a real deduction *per pay period* (a monthly payslip uses 1/12 of the annual threshold, rounded down each month), which only matches `rate% × (annual income − annual threshold)` exactly when income is level across the year. This module works the second, annual way — consistent with every other figure on the tab, which are all annual illustrations rather than payslip simulations — and the module header and the panel's footnote both say so, rather than presenting a figure that looks payslip-accurate and occasionally isn't.
- **The "combined marginal rate" is the headline, the same pattern #24 established for HICBC.** A Plan 2 borrower earning into the basic rate faces 29% on their next pound (20% tax + 9% repayment), not the 20% the band table alone shows — and with an active Postgraduate Loan too, 35%. `incomeTaxMarginalRate`, `studentLoanMarginalRate` and `combinedMarginalRate` are all on the result so the panel can show the breakdown rather than just the total, the same reason `ChildBenefitCharge` shows its two components spelled out.
- **A reference table of all five plan types renders regardless of which is selected**, behind a `<details>` disclosure rather than always-open — `tax.js`'s band table always shows every band including unreached ones for the same reason (a reader's most common question is "where would the next one kick in"), but five threshold rows a user has already chosen between is more clutter than the always-relevant band ladder, so it's collapsed by default rather than removed.
- 36 new tests (28 in `student-loan.test.js`, 8 in `StudentLoanRepayment.test.js`), 751 total, up from 715. `npm run lint`, `npm run check` (0 errors, same 2 pre-existing `<slot>` warnings) and `npm run build` all clean. Also driven for real, as #23/#24/#25 did: `npm run build && npm run preview` plus a throwaway `puppeteer-core` script against system Chromium (both removed afterwards, `package.json`/lockfile untouched) — confirmed the card shows "nothing to repay" with no plan selected, selecting Plan 2 at the tab's default £50,000 salary produces £1,937 (£21,530 over £28,470 at 9%, floored), ticking the Postgraduate Loan box on top adds a second row and switches the summary to "2 loans, summed", and the combined marginal rate line reads "20% tax + 9% repayment" = 29%. No console errors.

**Trade-offs / deviations from prompt:**
- **The five plan thresholds are the one unverified thing in this PR, the same gap #24's Child Benefit rates hit.** README.md names the five plan types but states no thresholds or rates at all — the spec's own list is *what* to model, not the figures to model it with — and `WebFetch`/`WebSearch` were both unavailable in this run (permission not granted; gov.uk's "Repaying your student loan" guidance was the first thing attempted). `LOAN_DEFS`' five thresholds (£26,065 / £28,470 / £32,745 / £25,000 / £21,000) and two rates (9% / 6%) are the last confirmed (2025/26) published figures, carried forward as 2026/27's best estimate rather than left unmodelled — the same call #24 made, for the same reason. Every figure carries a doc comment saying so, `student-loan.test.js` pins all seven so a correction is a one-line, visible edit, and the panel's own footnote tells a user to check their plan type on gov.uk directly, since choosing the wrong plan is a common real mistake independent of whether the thresholds are exactly right.
- **Nothing is persisted, and `Profile` was deliberately not widened**, the same position #24/#25 took on children, partner income and marital status. Which plan applies is actually a fairly stable, long-lived fact — arguably a better `Profile` candidate than a partner's income — but adding it would mean a schema change, a `normaliseAppData` migration and a WealthR-CSV-compatibility question (per CLAUDE.md's caution on schema changes), for a field nothing can yet save: there is still no profile-editing form anywhere in the app (#5 landed the store, not a form). `StudentLoanRepayment.svelte`'s `plan`/`postgraduate` props are the seam a future profile form plugs into.
- **The two undergraduate-plan inputs are not shared with anything else on the tab**, unlike `partnerIncome` on the Child Benefit and Marriage Allowance cards. Nothing else on this tab needs to know a user's Student Loan plan, so there was no second question to deduplicate against; if a future feature (e.g. a full net-pay/payslip tab) needs the same fact, `StudentLoanRepayment`'s props are the pattern to lift into a shared parent, the same way `partnerIncome` was lifted into `TaxCalculator` for #25.
- **A borrower repaying loans under two different undergraduate plans at once is not modelled.** This is a genuine, if uncommon, real case (e.g. a Plan 1 loan from a first degree and a Plan 2 loan from a later, separately-funded second degree) — SLC does let both run concurrently, each against its own threshold, exactly like the Postgraduate Loan case this module *does* handle. `plan` is a single choice among the four specifically because the much more common multi-loan case is "one undergraduate plan plus PG," and a second undergraduate-plan selector would have doubled the control surface for a case most users don't have. `LOAN_DEFS`/`repaymentForLoan` don't prevent extending `studentLoanSummary` to accept a second `plan` later if this turns out to matter.
- **Collection mechanics aren't modelled.** Whether the repayment arrives via PAYE (deducted automatically each pay period) or Self Assessment (calculated once a year for the self-employed) isn't distinguished — both use the same annual formula, but a self-employed borrower's real cash-flow timing looks nothing like a monthly payslip deduction, and the panel's "a month" figure implies the PAYE case specifically.
- **No interest, no balance, no "years to clear".** This module answers "what comes out of my pay this year", not "how big is my loan" or "when will it be repaid" — there is no loan balance anywhere in the data model, and UK Student Loan interest (RPI-linked, plan-dependent) compounds against a balance this app doesn't track. README.md's "Student Loan plans 1, 2, 4, 5, PG" sits under the *tax calculator* feature, not the debt tracker, which is the reading this module follows; a balance-and-payoff view would be a `debt.js` feature, not a `tax.js`-adjacent one.
- **National Insurance is still absent**, the same position as #23/#24/#25: `combinedMarginalRate` is income tax plus student loan only. A borrower's real next-pound cost also includes 2% (or 8%, below the upper earnings limit) of employee NI, so even the "combined" figure here understates the true marginal cost — now the fourth module to do so for the same reason (no issue in Milestone 4 covers NI).
- **The panel is a card, not a chart**, same position as #23/#24/#25: the obvious visual (a stacked area of tax + repayment against income, with a visible kink at each threshold) needs #12 (LayerChart), still open. `studentLoanSummary` returns plain numbers written against no chart library.
- `npm test` still isn't in `.github/workflows/ci.yml` (flagged since #2; the app token lacks `workflows` permission), so all 751 tests run locally but not on the PR. `build`/`check`/`lint` remain the only CI gates.

## Salary sacrifice + 60% personal allowance taper — 2026-08-07
<!-- METRICS:salary-sacrifice-60-personal-allowance-taper -->
- **Execution Duration:** __DURATION__ seconds
- **Model:** __MODEL__
- **Turns:** __TURNS__
- **Input Tokens:** __INPUT_TOKENS__
- **Output Tokens:** __OUTPUT_TOKENS__
- **Estimated Cost:** $__COST__ (from Claude Code's reported total_cost_usd)
- **Ballpark cost, comparable GPT-5-tier model:** $__GPT_BALLPARK__ (illustrative -- see methodology note above)
- **Ballpark cost, comparable Gemini-tier model:** $__GEMINI_BALLPARK__ (illustrative -- see methodology note above)

**Decisions:**
- **Half of this issue was already built, and the half that wasn't is the interesting one.** #23 shipped the taper itself inside `tax.js` — the £1-for-£2 withdrawal, the £125,140 exhaustion point, and a `marginalTaxRate` that derives 60% (67.5% in Scotland) rather than special-casing it — because the bands above £100,000 are wrong without it, and its journal entry said so at the time. What #23 could not do was model the standard *response* to being in that band, which is to sacrifice your way back out of it. So this PR adds no new taper arithmetic to `tax.js` at all; it adds `src/lib/salary-sacrifice.js`, which asks what lowering an income is worth, and answers it against the taper `tax.js` already knows about.
- **Its own module, importing from `tax.js` with nothing going the other way** — the same one-directional shape `hicbc.js` (#24), `marriage-allowance.js` (#25) and `student-loan.js` (#26) all have. `tax.js` answers "what does this income owe"; this answers "what is it worth to have a smaller one".
- **The tab's income field now means gross salary *before* sacrifice, and `TaxCalculator` computes the post-sacrifice figure once and hands it to every card.** That is the change with the widest reach in this PR: #23's module doc and #24's panel footnote both told the user to "enter income already net of any salary sacrifice if you want the allowance taper assessed correctly", and #24's journal entry called that out explicitly as "an instruction rather than a calculation… #27 is where that becomes arithmetic". It now is. The band table, the effective and marginal rates, the High Income Child Benefit Charge, Marriage Allowance eligibility and Student Loan repayments are all worked on the reduced income, which is what actually happens: sacrificed pay is never the employee's income for any of those purposes. Driving it in a browser, sacrificing £25,140 out of £125,140 moves the Student Loan repayment from £8,700 to £6,437 and the HICBC without anything in those cards knowing salary sacrifice exists.
- **The sacrifice input lives in `TaxCalculator`, not in the salary sacrifice card — deliberately the opposite of where this tab puts every other card-local control.** Children, a Student Loan plan and a marital status all belong to their own panels because they change nothing above them. Salary sacrifice changes every figure in the card it would sit under, so putting it anywhere but beside the salary would leave the tab holding two answers to "what income is this about". `SalarySacrifice.svelte` takes the amount as a `$bindable` instead — the same mechanism `partnerIncome` uses (#25) — so its one action can still write back.
- **`grossIncomeAtBandStart` is the piece of arithmetic the rest of the module rests on, and the taper is exactly why it isn't a one-liner.** `tax.js` stores band boundaries on income *after* allowances (its convention 1, which is right, and which #23's header explains is the single easiest way to get the £100k–£125,140 region wrong). A sacrifice comes off *gross* pay, so every boundary has to be converted back — and the conversion is three cases, not one: add £12,570 below the taper, add nothing above £125,140, and divide by 1.5 inside it, because a gross pound in the taper adds £1.50 of taxable income. Tests pin that the three agree at both joins (£87,430 taxable ↔ £100,000 gross by either of two rules; £125,140 ↔ itself by either of two others) and that the Scottish ladder's published gross thresholds — £16,537, £29,526, £43,662, £75,000 — fall back out of it.
- **A sacrifice is sliced by the marginal rate each pound of it displaces, top down, and the slice table is cross-checked against the plain subtraction.** £40,000 out of £130,000 is not relieved at one rate: £4,860 at 45%, £25,140 at 60% and £10,000 at 40%, for a blended 53.18% — the number a single "you'd save £X" figure hides. The slices are an integral of the marginal rate; `incomeTax(salary) − incomeTax(salary − sacrifice)` is the same quantity by subtraction. Nine parameterised cases across both regions assert the two agree to the penny, because a breakdown that doesn't add up to the headline is worse than no breakdown.
- **Each slice's rate is read off `tax.js`'s own `marginalTaxRate` at the slice's midpoint rather than re-derived here.** The rate is constant across a slice by construction, so a midpoint sample is exact — and it keeps one source of truth for what a rate *is*, which is why Scotland's 67.5% taper and its six-band ladder need no code in this module at all. The panel's "the 67.5% band" heading is read the same way rather than written into the copy.
- **The headline is the cost, not the saving.** "£15,084 saved" is the less useful half of the sentence; "£25,140 into your pension for £10,056 of take-home, 40p per £1" is what the taper band actually offers, and it is the tile the card leads with. `costPerPound` and `effectiveReliefRate` are on the result for that reason.
- **The sacrifice opens seeded from `profile.pension_pct`,** which is the first thing anywhere in the app to read that field. The data model records a contribution percentage but not *how* the contribution is made, and only a sacrifice arrangement genuinely removes the pay from income — so it's documented in both the component and this entry as a starting figure to correct, not a claim about the user's scheme. It is `0` for any profile that hasn't set it, so the tab's default behaviour is unchanged and a regression test pins that.
- **One action, in the one place a calculator can usefully offer one:** a button that sets the sacrifice to exactly what it takes to reach £100,000. It's the question the taper actually prompts, the answer is a single subtraction, and writing it back through the `$bindable` is the only interactive behaviour in this PR that server-rendered tests can't reach — so it was verified in a real browser instead (below).
- **Included a £60,000 pension annual allowance check that README.md doesn't ask for**, on the grounds that a tool willing to tell someone to sacrifice £40,000 should say when that would breach the cap. It's deliberately framed as a prompt rather than a verdict: the constant's doc comment lists what isn't modelled (the allowance's own taper for very high earners, three-year carry-forward, the money purchase annual allowance) and the panel's copy says the figure counts only what was typed here.
- 64 new tests (48 in `salary-sacrifice.test.js`, 12 in `SalarySacrifice.test.js`, 4 added to `TaxCalculator.test.js` covering the seeded sacrifice, the untouched default, the post-sacrifice band table and the reduced income reaching the cards below), 815 total, up from 751. `npm run lint`, `npm run check` (0 errors, same 2 pre-existing `<slot>` warnings) and `npm run build` all clean. Also driven for real, as #23/#24/#25/#26 did: `npm run build && npm run preview` plus a throwaway `puppeteer-core` script against system Chromium (both removed afterwards, `package.json`/lockfile untouched) — confirmed the card on load at £50,000, the £125,140 case offering "Sacrifice £25,140 to clear the taper", that button writing 25140 back into the input beside the salary, the band table then reporting £100,000 with the full allowance and a 60% marginal rate, the Student Loan and Child Benefit cards moving with it, Scotland at £120,000/£10,000 reading 67.5% and £0.33 per £1, and clearing the salary field taking every card away behind the existing validation message. No console errors.

**Trade-offs / deviations from prompt:**
- **National Insurance's absence costs more here than in any previous module, and it now understates a benefit rather than a cost.** Salary sacrifice's whole commercial appeal is that the sacrificed pay escapes employee NI (8% below the upper earnings limit, 2% above) *and* employer NI, which many employers pass back into the pot — so a real £25,140 sacrifice is worth meaningfully more than the £15,084 of income tax this module reports. Every figure here is therefore a deliberate, consistent understatement rather than a guess at the missing part, and the card says so in as many words. Fifth module now blocked on the same gap (#23/#24/#25/#26 all flagged it); README.md's feature list still doesn't mention NI and Milestone 4 still has no issue for it, but "salary sacrifice, minus the NI" is a strong argument that it needs one.
- **The module is written for a pension sacrifice specifically, though the income tax arithmetic is generic.** Giving up pay for an EV lease, a cycle-to-work bike or additional holiday has exactly the same effect on income tax and the taper, and `salarySacrificeSummary` would report it correctly — but `pensionGain`, the annual allowance check and the "taxed on the way out" caveat are all pension framing. Naming the field generically and adding a benefit-type selector would have bought breadth at the cost of the specific sentence the panel exists to say.
- **No National Minimum Wage floor, which is the real cap on a sacrifice.** A scheme legally cannot take pay below NMW, and most also refuse to take it below the NI lower earnings limit (which would cost the employee a State Pension qualifying year). Both are figures README.md doesn't state and this run couldn't verify — the same sourcing problem #24's Child Benefit rates and #26's loan thresholds hit — so rather than invent them, the amount is clamped only at the salary itself and the constraint is stated in the panel's copy. `cappedSacrifice`'s doc comment says explicitly that it is arithmetic sanity, not a legal limit.
- **£60,000 is the one figure in this PR that comes from neither README.md nor `tax.js`.** It's the standard pension annual allowance and has been unchanged since 2023/24, so it's a much safer carry-forward than #26's student loan thresholds were, but it is still an unverified constant in a codebase whose CLAUDE.md warns that UK allowances change every April. It's pinned by a test, so correcting it is a one-line visible edit.
- **Pension contributions made any other way are still not modelled.** A relief-at-source personal contribution and a net-pay workplace scheme both also reduce adjusted net income for taper purposes, but they differ in how the relief arrives (grossed up in the pot and reclaimed via Self Assessment, versus deducted before tax) and in NI treatment. Modelling all three would mean a mechanism selector and a "what to claim back" figure; this PR does the one the issue names and the tab's footnote now says plainly that the other two aren't here.
- **Nothing is persisted, and `Profile` was deliberately not widened** — the same position #24/#25/#26 took. The difference this time is that the profile already *has* a relevant field: `pension_pct` seeds the input, but nothing writes back to it, and there's still no profile-editing form anywhere in the app (#5 landed the store, not a form) for a corrected figure to round-trip through. A `pension_is_salary_sacrifice` boolean is the obvious future addition; it would need a schema change and a WealthR-CSV-compatibility check (per CLAUDE.md), so it waits for whichever issue adds the profile form.
- **Sacrificing exactly down to £100,000 still reports a 60% marginal rate**, which is correct and can read oddly next to "the full allowance is back": `tax.js`'s `inAllowanceTaper` is inclusive at the lower edge, because the *next* pound above £100,000 is already tapered. The card leans on `clearsTaper` for its wording so the two sentences don't contradict, but the marginal-rate tile above still says 60% — accurately.
- **Each slice's tax saved is rounded to the penny individually.** The tests assert the column agrees with the before-and-after subtraction to two decimal places at nine different incomes, which it does everywhere it was checked, but a sacrifice landing on a fraction of a penny inside two different bands could in principle leave the hand-summed column a penny away from the total row. Rounding per slice is what lets each row be a figure the user can check, which seemed worth more than a guaranteed-exact column.
- **The panel is a card, not a chart**, same position as #23/#24/#25/#26: the obvious visual here is a marginal-rate curve with the 60% plateau between £100,000 and £125,140 and a shaded region showing what a sacrifice removes, which needs #12 (LayerChart), still open. `sacrificeSlices` returns exactly the `{from, to, rate}` runs such a chart would plot, written against no chart library.
- **The band table's "Total" row now shows post-sacrifice income**, since that is what the ladder was climbed with. A user reading only that row could take it for their salary; the sentence above the tiles states both figures explicitly to compensate, but the table itself has no "sacrificed" line of its own.
- `npm test` still isn't in `.github/workflows/ci.yml` (flagged since #2; the app token lacks `workflows` permission), so all 815 tests run locally but not on the PR. `build`/`check`/`lint` remain the only CI gates.

## Pension tracker: pot types + per-pot fields — 2026-08-07
<!-- METRICS:pension-tracker-pot-types-per-pot-fields -->
- **Execution Duration:** __DURATION__ seconds
- **Model:** __MODEL__
- **Turns:** __TURNS__
- **Input Tokens:** __INPUT_TOKENS__
- **Output Tokens:** __OUTPUT_TOKENS__
- **Estimated Cost:** $__COST__ (from Claude Code's reported total_cost_usd)
- **Ballpark cost, comparable GPT-5-tier model:** $__GPT_BALLPARK__ (illustrative -- see methodology note above)
- **Ballpark cost, comparable Gemini-tier model:** $__GEMINI_BALLPARK__ (illustrative -- see methodology note above)

**Decisions:**
- **The data model already had everything this issue needed.** `$lib/model.js`'s `Pension` typedef, `createPension` and `normalisePension`/`validateAppData` were all written when #5 (core data model) landed, including the exact field list issue #29 asks for (`value`, `contribution_pct`, `employer_pct`, `fund_fee`) plus the `db_*`/`ni_*` fields later issues (#30/#31) will use. So this PR is UI only — a new `PensionTracker.svelte` component and the `/pensions` route wired to the store — with no schema change, no migration, and nothing added to `model.js`.
- **`pensions[]` is flat on `AppData`, not nested per month like `investments`/`debts`, so `PensionTracker` is a flatter component than `InvestmentHoldings`/`DebtTracker`.** A pension balance is a fact the user updates occasionally (checking a statement), not a monthly snapshot line — README.md's own data model outline puts it at the top level. There is no month selector, no `MonthlyEntry` juggling, no `_monthly_entry_id` snapshot trick: add/edit/remove work against one list directly.
- **A new `PENSION_POT_TYPES` enum subset (`$lib/enums.js`) drives the type select, deliberately excluding `state` from the existing six-value `PENSION_TYPES`.** The State Pension has no pot to type a value into — its figures come from NI qualifying years, which is #31's scope, not this one's — so offering it in a form titled "pot value, contribution %, employer contribution %, fund fee" would invite a meaningless zero-value entry. `PENSION_POT_TYPES` follows the same "frozen subset of the parent enum" shape as `CASH_INVESTMENT_TYPES`/`DEFINED_BENEFIT_PENSION_TYPES`/`ISA_WRAPPERS`, and `enums.test.js` gained the same "drawn from the parent enum" + a length/exclusion assertion the other subsets get.
- **Defined Benefit is selectable as a pot type — issue #29's own type list names it — but its form fields are a forward-reference note instead of the four pot-value inputs.** `model.js`'s `Pension` typedef already documents why: "DC pots use `value`/`contribution_pct`/`employer_pct`/`fund_fee`, DB pots use the `db_*` fields" — a Defined Benefit scheme's income comes from an accrual formula (rate × years × salary), not a pot balance, so asking for "your contribution %" on a final-salary scheme would be collecting a number nothing means and nothing reads. `DEFINED_BENEFIT_PENSION_TYPES` (already in `enums.js`, unused until now) is what the component checks to swap the four inputs for a note pointing at #30, both in the add/edit form and in each list item's summary line. Saving a Defined Benefit pot writes zero into all four pot-value fields via `createPension`'s own defaults — matching `model.js`'s stated convention — and leaves the `db_*` fields untouched (`null`) for #30 to fill in later.
- **List items and the add/edit form are two independent branches on the same `isDefinedBenefit(type)` check, not one shared conditional**, because they read different `type` values: the list reads each `pension.type` in the array, the form reads its own `type` state (defaulting to `dc_workplace`, following whatever is being edited). Switching the form's type dropdown to Defined Benefit hides the four inputs immediately, independent of what's already in the list.
- **No activity log.** `logEntityAdded`/`logEntityRemoved`/`logEntityUpdated` and `ACTIVITY_LOG_ENTITY_TYPES` cover `investment`/`debt` only (#14's own scope) — pensions were never part of it, and widening that enum for one more entity type wasn't part of this issue, so `PensionTracker` skips the activity-log wiring `InvestmentHoldings`/`DebtTracker` both carry.
- **`/pensions/+page.svelte` follows the dashboard's write pattern, not the read-only tax/retirement pattern.** Tax and Retirement read `profile`/`monthly_entries` and write nothing back, so they show a plain "Loading your saved data…" gate. Pensions writes, like the dashboard's `InvestmentHoldings`/`DebtTracker`, so the page also shows the same persistence-mode line ("Saved to this browser only" / "Synced to your GitHub Gist") and sync status the dashboard does, and gates rendering the tracker behind `ready` so a user interacting with the empty pre-hydration list can't have their edit clobbered by the hydrated data landing a moment later.
- 8 new tests in `PensionTracker.test.js` (the `svelte/server`-render smoke-test pattern #22's `FireCalculator.test.js` established, since this repo still has no browser test environment) plus 2 new tests in `enums.test.js` (one from `PENSION_POT_TYPES` joining the "drawn from the parent enum" table, one dedicated exclusion/length check), 825 total, up from 815 (`InvestmentHoldings`/`DebtTracker` have no test files of their own either — this follows that precedent for the CRUD logic itself while still pinning the one genuinely interesting initial-render branch: pot-value fields vs. the Defined Benefit note). `npm run lint`, `npm run check` (0 errors, same 2 pre-existing `<slot>` warnings) and `npm run build` all clean. Also driven for real, as #22–#27 did: `npm run build && npm run preview` plus a throwaway `puppeteer-core` script against system Chromium (both removed afterwards, `package.json`/lockfile untouched) — confirmed the empty state, adding a DC Workplace pot with all four fields showing £45,000/5%/3%/0.35% in the list, the pot-value fields disappearing the instant Defined Benefit is selected in the form, adding an NHS Pension (Defined Benefit) pot showing the #30 forward-reference note and the pot count updating to "2 pots recorded", clicking Edit pre-filling the form from the existing record, and removing both pots returning to the empty state. No console errors.

**Trade-offs / deviations from prompt:**
- **The issue's field list ("per-pot value, contribution %, employer contribution %, annual fund fee/OCF") is read as applying to the three pot-value types the issue also names (DC Workplace, SIPP, Lifetime ISA), not literally to all four.** Defined Benefit is in the issue's type list too, but its pot-value fields are `model.js`'s own documented zero case; taking the field list completely literally would mean adding four numeric inputs to a scheme type that structurally can't use them, just to satisfy the letter of a sentence whose surrounding context (and the data model it was itself designed against) says otherwise. This is flagged here rather than silently assumed, in case the actual intent was for #29 to also stub in placeholder DB inputs ahead of #30.
- **Tax relief (20%/40%, claimable via Self Assessment) is not shown anywhere on this pot**, even though `contribution_pct` is now enterable — that display is explicitly #32's own scope ("Pension tax relief display per pot"), and adding it here would duplicate work #32 is going to do against the same field.
- **No combined "total contribution rate" (yours + employer) tile**, despite both percentages being entered side by side — README.md doesn't ask for one at this stage, and the retirement income stream builder (#33) is the natural place a combined figure would actually get used (projecting a pot forward), rather than a number with no projection behind it sitting on this tab.
- **Percentage inputs are capped at 0–100 in the form (`max="100"`) but `fund_fee` uses a `step="0.01"` while `contribution_pct`/`employer_pct` use `step="0.1"`** — deliberately different granularity, since a realistic OCF is often quoted to two decimal places (0.35%, 0.22%) while a contribution rate is conventionally whole or half percentages. Neither step attribute is enforced by `validateAppData`, which only range-checks 0–100; a hand-edited Gist with a three-decimal fee still loads and validates fine.
- **Nothing here computes a projected pot value or retirement income** — this issue is data entry only, matching its title ("pot types + per-pot fields"). The Defined Benefit income calculation (#30), the State Pension projection (#31), tax relief (#32) and the retirement income stream builder (#33) are the milestone's remaining issues, all left untouched.
- `npm test` still isn't in `.github/workflows/ci.yml` (flagged since #2; the app token lacks `workflows` permission), so all 825 tests run locally but not on the PR. `build`/`check`/`lint` remain the only CI gates.

## Net worth chart: tracked history line — series maths + line and axes — 2026-08-07
<!-- METRICS:net-worth-chart-tracked-history-line -->
- **Execution Duration:** __DURATION__ seconds
- **Model:** __MODEL__
- **Turns:** __TURNS__
- **Input Tokens:** __INPUT_TOKENS__
- **Output Tokens:** __OUTPUT_TOKENS__
- **Estimated Cost:** $__COST__ (from Claude Code's reported total_cost_usd)
- **Ballpark cost, comparable GPT-5-tier model:** $__GPT_BALLPARK__ (illustrative -- see methodology note above)
- **Ballpark cost, comparable Gemini-tier model:** $__GEMINI_BALLPARK__ (illustrative -- see methodology note above)

**Decisions:**
- **`layerchart@2.1.0` is installed by this PR, even though issue #67 says the dependency arrived with #12.** PR #70 (#12's work) is still open and unmerged against `main`, so a branch cut from `main` has no LayerChart to import and nothing here would build. Installing it again is the only way this issue lands standalone; the two `package.json`/lockfile diffs are identical adds of the same version, so whichever merges second resolves as a lockfile conflict rather than a semantic one. The overlap is bigger than the dependency and is written up under trade-offs.
- **The series lives in `$lib/net-worth.js` as pure functions, with the component deciding only how it looks.** Same split every other feature area in this repo uses (`debt.js`/`DebtTracker`, `forecast.js`/`ForecastProjections`), and it is what makes 36 unit tests possible in a repo with no browser test environment: `netWorthSeries`, `netWorthXDomain`, `netWorthYExtent` and `netWorthMonthTicks` are ordinary array-in/array-out functions that never touch a scale.
- **Totals go through `debt.js`'s `sumInvestmentValues`/`sumDebtBalances` rather than filtering `exclude_from_net_worth` again.** The issue asks for totals matching how `debt.js` and `forecast.js` already treat the flag; the way to *match* an existing rule is to call it, not to restate it. A mortgage the user excluded because the property tab tracks its equity therefore drops out of the chart, the D/I ratio and the forecast by one shared decision rather than three that could drift apart.
- **A `NetWorthPoint` is a `ForecastPoint` in the fields they share, and there is a test that proves it rather than a comment that claims it.** `netWorthPoint(entry)` and `projectScenario({...entry, months: 0})[0]` are asserted equal on `month`/`year`/`investments`/`debts`/`net_worth` for a position with awkward pennies and an excluded holding on both sides — because #81 joins the tracked line to the forecast lines at exactly that seam, and a half-penny disagreement there draws a visible step in the finished chart. The whole-pence `roundMoney` is duplicated from `forecast.js` (where it is private) rather than either module exporting a rounding helper as public API; the test is what keeps the two copies honest.
- **The x value is a `Date` at UTC midnight on the 1st, and every formatter that reads one back asks for `timeZone: 'UTC'`.** `new Date(year, month - 1, 1)` is local midnight, which east of Greenwich is the previous month in UTC — a January snapshot labelled "Dec". Both halves of that round trip have to agree, so `monthStartDate` uses `Date.UTC` and the component's two `Intl.DateTimeFormat`s are pinned; `net-worth.test.js` asserts the round trip and `NetWorthChart.test.js` asserts the rendered label. Supplying explicit ticks does the same job for the axis: d3's own time-tick generator would pick boundaries in the local zone, so it never gets the chance.
- **Skipped months are left as gaps, and the line runs straight across them.** No point is invented for a month with no snapshot — filling a gap with compound growth is `AutoInvestFill`'s job and a deliberate user action, and a chart that quietly did it would make the user's tracking record look better than it is. Because the x scale is time-based rather than index-based, an eighteen-month hole occupies eighteen months of chart width, so the gap is visible as an unusually long straight segment rather than being compressed into one step. Marking that segment as a gap (a dashed run, a break) needs the auto-filled/recorded distinction, which is #82.
- **The y domain spans zero by default (`netWorthYExtent`'s `includeZero`, on).** Net worth is an absolute magnitude, not an index: on a zoomed axis a rise from £120,000 to £121,000 draws the same dramatic climb as one from £0 to £121,000, and a net worth that has gone negative doesn't visibly cross anything. The cost is that a long, steadily-growing history looks flatter than it would zoomed, which is the honest picture rather than the flattering one. Padding is never applied through zero, so when zero is an end of the range it stays exactly zero and the baseline is a real gridline; the option is exposed for a later month-on-month view that genuinely wants the zoom.
- **`netWorthMonthTicks` steps through the calendar, not through the points, and always labels the first and last recorded month.** Ticks derived from point *positions* would put six labels on the six months either side of a long gap and none inside it, undoing the whole reason the x value is a date. Stepping the calendar at `ceil(span / (max - 1))` months and then swapping the trailing tick for the final month (or appending it, when it is more than half a step away) keeps the two months a reader actually looks for — "where does this start" and "how recent is this" — always labelled, at any history length; there is a test that walks 5/7/11/13/29/100-month histories and asserts exactly that.
- **The component composes `Chart` / `Svg` / `Axis` / `Spline` rather than the `LineChart` wrapper**, exactly as the issue asks and for the reason it gives: #81 drops three more `Spline`s and an `Area` into this same plot area, sharing one pair of accessors and one pair of scales. A wrapper that owns its own plot area would have to be unpicked first, and the unpicking would be a rewrite of this component rather than an addition to it.
- **`--chart-1` is a new token pair in `app.css`, defined for `:root` and `.dark` separately, not derived from `--primary`.** The existing chrome tokens are near-black on near-white — chosen to keep UI furniture quiet, never to keep several data series apart from each other and from their surface. The two values (light `#2a78d6`, dark `#3987e5`, written as the same `H S% L%` triples the other tokens use) are the first slot of a categorical palette, run through a palette validator against each mode's own surface: both clear 3:1 contrast and sit inside their mode's lightness band. The dark value is the same hue stepped for a dark background, not the light value flipped. #81 takes the next slots for the forecast lines and the band.
- **Two empty states, not one.** Nothing recorded gets "No snapshots yet"; exactly one month recorded gets that month's figure plus "a line needs two months to run between". A one-point spline draws nothing at all, and the point marker that would rescue the single-snapshot case is #82 — so saying so beats rendering an axis with an invisible line in it.
- **The plot area's height is an inline `style`, not an `h-72` class.** A `<Chart>` measures its container and draws nothing if it comes back zero-high, so the one declaration the chart cannot render without must not depend on Tailwind emitting a class — and right now Tailwind is not emitting most of them (see trade-offs). Every other class on the component is cosmetic and degrades the same way the rest of the app already does.
- **Driven for real in a browser, as #22–#29 were.** `npm run dev` plus headless system Chromium screenshots against a throwaway `/chartcheck` route (removed afterwards; `package.json` untouched by it) — confirmed the line drawing over a ten-month history with a five-month gap in it, the £0–£60k+ y axis with gridlines, six month labels from "Jan 25" to "Mar 26" with the last one not clipped (the right padding went 16 → 32 because of what the first screenshot showed), the same chart rendered inside a forced `.dark` wrapper picking up the dark `--chart-1`, and the dashboard's empty state. The first screenshot is also what caught the Tailwind problem: the chart was a blank space until the height stopped being a utility class.
- 45 new tests (36 in `net-worth.test.js`, 9 in `NetWorthChart.test.js` following the established `svelte/server`-render smoke-test pattern), 870 total, up from 825. `npm run lint`, `npm run check` (0 errors, same 2 pre-existing `<slot>` warnings) and `npm run build` all clean.

**Trade-offs / deviations from prompt:**
- **This PR overlaps PR #70, which is open and unmerged.** #70 adds `layerchart`, an `src/lib/net-worth.js` with two functions of its own, an `src/components/NetWorthChart.svelte` that is a hand-rolled D3+SVG line (its own journal entry says so explicitly — deliberately *not* using LayerChart's components, since #12 was only a build smoke test), and the same `+page.svelte` wiring. Every one of those four files conflicts. This branch was cut from `main` and implements #67's stated design — LayerChart primitives, a series module with x/y/tick helpers, unit tests — so where the two disagree, this is the version #67 describes and #81 is written against. Merge order matters: if #70 goes first, take this branch's `net-worth.js`, `NetWorthChart.svelte` and `+page.svelte` wholesale and keep only the lockfile from #70; if this goes first, #70 reduces to its journal entry. Not resolved unilaterally here because closing or rewriting someone else's open PR is not this issue's call.
- **Tailwind's utility layer is not reaching the page at all, and this PR does not fix it.** `src/app.css` still uses v3's `@tailwind base/components/utilities` directives while `package.json` is on Tailwind v4 with `@tailwindcss/postcss`; the served stylesheet contains the CSS custom properties and a handful of theme-independent utilities, but no preflight, no spacing scale, no colour utilities. So the whole app currently renders in serif with no padding — visible in the dashboard screenshot, and pre-existing on `main` rather than caused here. Fixing it is an app-wide visual change touching every component and belongs in its own issue; this component only declines to be broken by it, by putting the one load-bearing declaration (the plot height) inline. Worth raising as an issue before more UI lands on top of it.
- **No hover tooltip, no point markers, no auto-filled/recorded distinction, no accessible table fallback, and LayerChart's own chrome is not themed off the shadcn tokens** — all five are #82 by the issue's own scope split. Two consequences worth naming: a reader can see the line's shape but cannot read a specific month's figure off it (the *latest* figure is spelled out above the chart in full, so the compact "£60k" axis labels are never the only place a number appears), and the `aria-label` is a summary of the series — first month, last month, direction and size of the change — rather than the data, which is what a table fallback is for.
- **`NetWorthPoint` carries an `auto_filled` flag that nothing currently renders.** It is a property of the month and dropping it here would only mean re-deriving it in #82; carrying it costs one field and no logic. Drawing the distinction is still #82's.
- **`d3-scale` is not imported directly and no scale is passed to `<Chart>`** — LayerChart picks `scaleTime` itself when the x values are `Date`s. A time scale built in the local zone rather than UTC changes nothing here, because the scale only ever sees epoch milliseconds, and the two things that *are* zone-sensitive — tick placement and label formatting — are both supplied explicitly. This avoids declaring a direct dependency on a transitive package for one import; if #81 or #82 needs `scaleUtc` for another reason, it should add `d3-scale` to `package.json` properly rather than reaching through `layerchart`.
- **The y axis gets gridlines and no rule; the x axis gets a rule and no gridlines.** Two sets of gridlines on a single-series line chart is noise, and the horizontal set is the one that helps read a value. Not something README.md or the issue specifies.
- **The `.dark` chart colour was verified by forcing the class in a scratch route**, because the app has no dark-mode toggle yet — nothing in `+layout.svelte` ever sets `.dark`. The token is correct and validated for when one lands; it is not reachable through the UI today.
- `npm test` still isn't in `.github/workflows/ci.yml` (flagged since #2; the app token lacks `workflows` permission), so all 870 tests run locally but not on the PR. `build`/`check`/`lint` remain the only CI gates.

## Net worth chart: forecast confidence band overlay (LayerChart) — 2026-08-07
<!-- METRICS:net-worth-chart-forecast-confidence-band-overlay -->
- **Execution Duration:** __DURATION__ seconds
- **Model:** __MODEL__
- **Turns:** __TURNS__
- **Input Tokens:** __INPUT_TOKENS__
- **Output Tokens:** __OUTPUT_TOKENS__
- **Estimated Cost:** $__COST__ (from Claude Code's reported total_cost_usd)
- **Ballpark cost, comparable GPT-5-tier model:** $__GPT_BALLPARK__ (illustrative -- see methodology note above)
- **Ballpark cost, comparable Gemini-tier model:** $__GEMINI_BALLPARK__ (illustrative -- see methodology note above)

**Decisions:**
- **The overlay is one series and four marks, not three series and a fill.** `forecast.js`'s `forecastBand()` already collapses the three scenarios into one `low`/`mid`/`high` point per month, so `forecastBandSeries()` adds a `date` to each and stops there — and the shaded `<Area>` (`y0=low`, `y1=high`), the two dashed edge splines and the solid realistic spline all read that same array. The band's edges and the outer scenario lines are therefore the same numbers by construction; drawing the fill from one array and the lines from another is exactly how a band ends up half a pixel out of register with the line that is supposed to bound it. It also means the issue's "assembly against an existing chart" stayed assembly: no projection maths was written for this PR.
- **`forecastBandSeries()` lives in `$lib/net-worth.js`, alongside the tracked series, rather than in `forecast.js`.** `forecast.js` is written against no chart library and shouldn't start carrying `Date`s for one; `net-worth.js` already owns "what the chart plots and how its scales are built", and #67 built its points to be `ForecastPoint`-shaped precisely so this join would be free. `net-worth.js` importing `forecast.js` adds no cycle (`forecast.js` imports only `auto-invest.js`/`model.js`).
- **The three chart-scale helpers were generalised to take the band, and #67's tracked-only versions now delegate to them.** `netWorthChartXDomain(points, band)`, `netWorthChartYExtent(points, band, options)` and `netWorthChartMonthTicks(points, band, options)` are the real implementations; `netWorthXDomain`/`netWorthYExtent`/`netWorthMonthTicks` are one-line calls with an empty band. Every #67 test still passes untouched, there is one implementation of the padding/tick/widening rules rather than two that could drift, and three new tests assert the delegation explicitly (`netWorthChartYExtent(points, [])` deep-equals `netWorthYExtent(points)`, and so on).
- **The y extent has to reach the band's `low` and `high`, not its `mid`.** A 30-year optimistic scenario is roughly double the realistic one; an axis fitted to the realistic line would run the shading off the top of the plot. Same at the bottom for a negative-growth projection against a debt, which has its own test.
- **The forecast gets the second categorical palette slot, and the whole overlay shares it — the band is that hue at 0.16 alpha, not a third and fourth colour.** Colour follows the entity: pessimistic/realistic/optimistic are one projection under three assumptions, not three unrelated series, so spending two more slots on them would encode a distinction that isn't there. `--chart-2` is `#eb6834` light / `#d95926` dark (the same source palette #67 took `--chart-1` from), defined for `:root` and `.dark` separately and run through the palette validator against each mode's own surface: worst-pair CVD ΔE 24.9 light / 26.8 dark against `--chart-1` (≥8 needed), worst normal-vision ΔE 33.8 / 32.0 (≥15), both slots ≥3:1 on their surface and inside their mode's lightness band. The forecast lines are also *dashed* — the realistic line at `6 4`, the outer two thinner at `3 3` — so tracked-vs-projected survives being printed in grey or read by someone who can't separate the hues.
- **A single snapshot now plots, where #67 said it couldn't.** #67's reasoning was sound for a tracked line alone (a one-point spline draws nothing, and point markers are #82), but one recorded month plus a forecast is a full chart: the band runs from that month for the whole horizon. So the chart renders whenever there are two tracked points *or* two band points, and the "a line needs two months" note is now the branch for a single snapshot with the forecast switched off — which is still exactly true. The #67 test that pinned that sentence keeps pinning it, with `showForecast: false` passed in.
- **Two controls, and neither of them is an assumption.** A "Show forecast" checkbox and a 5/10/20/30-year horizon picker; growth rate and scenario spread are deliberately *not* here. Those are assumptions, the forecast tab's sliders already own them, and a second set on the dashboard would be two places to change one number. The horizon is a zoom control instead: at 30 years a two-year history is a few pixels wide, so the reader needs a way to bring the tracked line back into view without touching what is being projected.
- **The growth rate comes off `Profile.growth_rate` via the store, read-only.** `+page.svelte` hydrates it alongside `monthly_entries` and passes it down; nothing on the dashboard writes it back. There is still no profile form to set it (that is #5's follow-on), so in practice it is the model's 5% default today — but the wiring means the chart follows the user's own assumption the moment one can be entered, rather than hard-coding a second 5% next to `AutoInvestFill`'s.
- **The horizon `<select>` takes a per-instance id from `$props.id()`.** Found by putting three charts on one scratch page: a hard-coded `id` collides immediately, and a duplicated id silently re-points the first label at the wrong control. Costs one line.
- **The legend's and control row's flex/gap declarations are inline as well as classed**, for the same reason #67 put the plot height inline: Tailwind's utilities are not currently reaching the page (see trade-offs), so `gap-4` renders as no gap and the row reads "Show forecastHorizon" / "TrackedRealistic (5%)". A legend whose entries have no gap and whose swatches have no size is not a legend, and identity-not-by-colour-alone depends on it.
- **Driven for real in a browser, as #22–#29 and #67 were.** `npm run dev` plus headless system Chromium against a throwaway `/chartcheck` route (removed afterwards; `package.json` and the lockfile untouched, `puppeteer-core` installed with `--no-save`) — confirmed the band shading and all four lines drawing over a ten-month history with a five-month gap, the tracked line meeting the forecast with no step at Mar 2026, the axis running to Mar 36 at ten years and Mar 56 at thirty, the same chart in a forced `.dark` wrapper picking up the dark hue, the single-snapshot case plotting from one month, unticking "Show forecast" removing exactly four SVG paths and leaving the tracked line, the horizon select re-scaling the axis, and the real dashboard route rendering its empty state.
