- **Ballpark cost, comparable Gemini-tier model:** $__GEMINI_BALLPARK__ (illustrative -- see methodology note above)

**Decisions:**
- **`src/lib/csv-import.js` is the mirror of `csv-export.js`, not a rewrite of its row-shaping.** Dataset detection compares a file's header row against `xlsx-export.js`'s own `NET_WORTH_HISTORY_COLUMNS`/`HOLDINGS_COLUMNS`/`DEBTS_COLUMNS` `header` strings — imported, not re-typed — so a future change to either sheet's shape can't silently drift the export and import formats apart. Per-cell parsing is the algebraic inverse of `formatCsvValue`: dates round-trip through `YYYY-MM-DD`, currency through a bare `Number()`, percentages by stripping the trailing `%` (no fraction round-trip needed — `csv-export.js` already writes the same whole-number percent `Investment.fund_fee`/`ownership_pct` store, just suffixed), enum labels resolved back to codes via an inverted copy of the relevant `enums.js` `*_LABELS` map, and `Included in Net Worth`'s `Yes`/`No` inverted back to `exclude_from_net_worth`.
- **A hand-rolled RFC 4180 parser (`parseCsv`), not a dependency.** The grammar this needs to read back is narrow and fully specified by `csv-export.js`'s own writer (BOM, `"..."` quoting with doubled `""`, `\r\n`/bare `\n` line endings, one trailing blank line dropped) — small enough that a ~50-line character-by-character state machine is both correct and easier to audit than pulling in a CSV parsing library for a feature this scoped. It throws only on the one shape it genuinely cannot recover from (an unterminated quoted field); every other malformed shape (wrong column count, a bad cell) is reported per-row/per-column instead of aborting the whole file.
- **Every parse failure names its row and column** (`row 4 (Fund Fee): must be a percentage, e.g. "5.00%"`), collected across the whole file rather than stopping at the first bad cell — matching the issue's explicit "never a silent default" requirement and letting a caller (#150) show every problem in one pass rather than a fix-one-reimport-repeat loop.
- **Merge by month, not full replace**, via a small `mergeRowsIntoEntries` helper: parsed rows are grouped by month, then upserted into a copy of `monthly_entries` — a month the file mentions gets its `investments` (Holdings) or `debts` (Debts) replaced wholesale (matching `MonthlyEntry`'s own restate-the-snapshot-fresh convention) while the *other* collection on that entry, and every month the file doesn't mention, is left byte-identical. A month with no existing entry gets a fresh one via `createMonthlyEntry`. `auto_filled` is explicitly reset to `false` on any *existing* entry the file touches (a brand-new entry is already `auto_filled: false` by construction) — once a month has real imported data it is no longer `auto-invest.js`'s bridged placeholder.
- **The merged document is revalidated through `model.js`'s `validateAppData`, not a bespoke check.** A CSV row can parse cleanly (a percentage string like `"150.00%"` is structurally fine) and still be out of range once turned into a stored value — reusing the same validator JSON import (`data-transfer.js`) already relies on means the two import paths can't quietly diverge on what counts as valid.
- **Net Worth History is rejected with a specific, actionable message**, not folded into the generic "unrecognised file" path — it names why (a derived total with no way back to the individual holdings/debts that produced it) and points at Holdings/Debts or the JSON export, per the issue's explicit instruction.
- **Result shape mirrors `data-transfer.js`'s `parseImportDocument`**: `{ ok: true, dataset, data, summary }` on success (`summary` gives #150 the counts a confirm step needs — records, distinct months, new vs updated), `{ ok: false, reason, message, errors }` on failure with a four-way discriminated `reason` (`invalid-csv` / `unrecognised-csv` / `read-only-dataset` / `invalid-data`) exactly as the issue specifies. Never throws, never mutates `currentData` — confirmed by a dedicated test that snapshots the input document and asserts it is unchanged after a successful import.
- **WealthR's own CSV format is explicitly out of scope, per the issue.** A `TODO(#130)` comment sits at the top of `csv-import.js`; DESIGN.md's "Data Migration" section and README.md's Phase 2 line are both updated to say the generic/own-format half is done and the WealthR half is blocked on a real sample file, not guessed at.

**Trade-offs / deviations from prompt:**
- **No `DataManager.svelte` wiring, no browser drive.** This issue's own scope note is explicit that the file picker / confirm step / error reporting is #150, built on top of this module's exported API — there is no UI surface yet to click through. Unlike every prior export/format issue in this journal (which had a real button in `DataManager.svelte` to drive), `parseCsvImport`/`parseCsv`/`detectCsvDataset` are exercised end-to-end only via the test suite below; there is nothing to screenshot until #150 lands.
- **Row numbers in error messages count the header as row 1** (the first data row is reported as "row 2"), matching what a reader would see opening the file in a spreadsheet, rather than a 0- or 1-based index into the data rows alone — a judgement call, since the issue's own example (`row 4 (Fund Fee)`) doesn't pin down which convention it meant.
- **38 new tests in `csv-import.test.js`** (1720 total, up from 1682): `parseCsv` unit tests (BOM, quoted/comma/newline fields, doubled-quote escaping, `\r\n` vs bare `\n`, trailing-blank-line-dropped vs a genuine mid-file blank line, the unterminated-quote throw), `detectCsvDataset` for all four outcomes, full round-trip tests against `csv-export.js`'s own `exportHoldingsCsv`/`exportDebtsCsv` output (including null-stays-null fields, comma/quote-bearing text, every enum label, and multiple months), one test per rejection reason, and the four merge cases the issue names explicitly (new month, existing month's collection replaced, the other collection on a touched month left alone, `auto_filled` reset). `npm test` still isn't in `.github/workflows/ci.yml` (flagged since #2), so these run locally but not on the PR; `build`/`check`/`lint` remain the CI gates and all three pass.

## Scenario: income shock (job loss / illness) — 2026-08-08
<!-- METRICS:scenario-income-shock-job-loss-illness -->
- **Execution Duration:** __DURATION__ seconds
- **Model:** __MODEL__
- **Turns:** __TURNS__
- **Input Tokens:** __INPUT_TOKENS__
- **Output Tokens:** __OUTPUT_TOKENS__
- **Estimated Cost:** $__COST__ (from Claude Code's reported total_cost_usd)
- **Ballpark cost, comparable GPT-5-tier model:** $__GPT_BALLPARK__ (illustrative -- see methodology note above)
- **Ballpark cost, comparable Gemini-tier model:** $__GEMINI_BALLPARK__ (illustrative -- see methodology note above)

**Decisions:**
- **`src/lib/income-shock.js` follows `stress-test.js`'s (#21) `adjustMonth` overlay shape exactly, per the issue's own instruction**: a config → normaliser → per-month hook → `*Forecast` wrapper → impact reader → comparison-row reader, the same five-function shape `stress-test.js` established. `incomeShockForecast` returns an ordinary `Forecast` with the config attached as `.shock`, so every existing `Forecast` reader (`forecastBand`, `summariseForecast`, `milestoneCrossings`, `compounding.js`) already knows how to read it.
- **A new `ForecastMonthAdjustment.contributionFactor` field in `forecast.js`, the deliberate mirror image of the stress test's `growthRate`/`factor`.** The stress test's own module doc states its convention as "a crash changes the market, not the standing order" — an income shock is exactly the opposite event, so it needed the opposite hook: something that scales the month's contribution and leaves growth alone, rather than the other way round. `projectHoldingValue` (`auto-invest.js`) gained a matching `contributionFactor` option (default `1`, so every existing caller — `fillMissingMonths` included — is unaffected) so `forecast.js`'s projection loop and the primitive it delegates to can't compute two different contribution amounts for the same month.
- **Four dials, matching the issue's own wording**: `dropPct` (0–100%, `100` = "contributions stop", less = "reduce to a stated level"), `atMonth` (timing), `durationMonths` (how long the full drop lasts) and `rampMonths` (the issue's "optionally, with a recovery ramp" for illness — `0` is a job loss that ends cleanly, resuming in full the month after; a positive value tapers the contribution linearly back to normal, for an illness that gets better by degrees rather than snapping back). Defaults (`100% / 12 months out / 6 months / no ramp`) describe a clean, recognisable job-loss scenario nobody has to configure to see something meaningful, the same reasoning `stress-test.js`'s own defaults give for picking a 2020-sized crash.
- **Nothing is ever paid back.** A missed contribution is missed for good — there is no catch-up month, because nothing in the data model records an intention to double up later. This is what makes the gap `incomeShockImpact` reports (`contributionsForgone`, `shortfall`, `compoundingLoss` — the extra cost beyond the missed pounds themselves, i.e. the growth they'd have earned) permanent rather than something that "recovers" the way a stress-test crash's `recoveredAt` can: once the ramp closes, the shocked line runs parallel to the baseline rather than converging back onto it, so there's no recovery-date metric to compute (a deliberate scope cut, not an oversight — see the trade-offs below).
- **`IncomeShockPanel.svelte` is `StressTestPanel.svelte`'s layout copied wholesale** — same off-by-default checkbox, same slider-plus-number-field dial pattern, same two-bar horizon comparison and two tables — with the headline prose and column headers rewritten for a contribution event rather than a market one. It takes the same `forecast`/`position`/`options`/`offsets`/`dobYear`/`dobMonth` props as `StressTestPanel` and is wired into `ForecastProjections.svelte` immediately below it, so both overlays read the same baseline anchor/horizon/spread rather than either deriving its own.

**Trade-offs / deviations from prompt:**
- **No "catches up with the baseline" metric**, unlike the stress test's `caughtUpAt`. A market crash can rebound past its own recovery rate and close the gap; an income shock's missing contributions never get made up (see above), so under any non-negative growth assumption the shocked line can only fall further behind or hold steady, never catch up — a metric for an event that structurally can't happen would be dead code, not a feature.
- **Slider bounds (0–100% drop, 0–36 months duration, 0–24 months ramp) are invented UI convenience, same caveat `StressTestPanel`'s own dial comments give** — README.md names the scenario but no numbers, and `normaliseIncomeShock` itself still accepts and clamps the full range up to `MAX_FORECAST_MONTHS` via the paired number field regardless of where the slider handle stops.
- **Driven for real in a browser**, continuing the pattern from #22-#38/#98/#61-#64/#100/#111/#116/#117/#118/#127/#128/#149/#150. `npm run build` + `vite preview`, headless system Chromium over the DevTools Protocol (`playwright-core` installed with `npm install --no-save` in a scratch `/tmp` directory). Used the Forecast tab's existing "no history yet" assumed-starting-position fallback (£10,000 invested, £500/month) rather than seeding IndexedDB, since the panel only needs a position, not real history. Ticked "Model an income shock", confirmed the collapsed/enabled/table states all render with the app's own defaults (a 100% drop a year out, lasting 6 months: "£3,000 in contributions never gets paid in... by 30 years that gap has grown to £12,224 (3%) less net worth... £9,224 of that is growth the missing contributions would themselves have earned"), then changed all four dials (50% drop, starts in 2 years, lasts 4 months, 3-month ramp) and confirmed the headline text, the per-scenario table and the per-horizon comparison table all recomputed correctly. No console messages or page errors in either state. The driver script and its scratch `node_modules` were removed afterward, nothing left in the working tree.
- **47 new tests** across `forecast.test.js` (8 new `contributionFactor` cases, 240 total in that file up from 232) and a new `income-shock.test.js` (39 tests): config normalisation/clamping, the per-month adjustment hook (the drop window, the linear ramp, the no-ramp instant resume, never touching `growthRate`/`factor`), `incomeShockForecast` (identity at a 0% drop, growth left untouched, every scenario contributing correctly), and the impact/comparison readers (`occurs` in both directions, exact forgone-contribution arithmetic, the shortfall exceeding the bare contributions total once compounding is accounted for, calendar dates for the drop/ramp boundaries, `null` shares at a non-positive baseline). 1767 tests total, up from 1720. `npm test` still isn't in `.github/workflows/ci.yml` (flagged since #2), so these run locally but not on the PR; `build`/`check`/`lint` remain the CI gates and all three pass.

## Scenario: mortgage rate rise — the engine (src/lib/mortgage-rate-rise.js) — 2026-08-08
<!-- METRICS:scenario-mortgage-rate-rise-the-engine -->
- **Execution Duration:** __DURATION__ seconds
- **Model:** __MODEL__
- **Turns:** __TURNS__
- **Input Tokens:** __INPUT_TOKENS__
- **Output Tokens:** __OUTPUT_TOKENS__
- **Estimated Cost:** $__COST__ (from Claude Code's reported total_cost_usd)
- **Ballpark cost, comparable GPT-5-tier model:** $__GPT_BALLPARK__ (illustrative -- see methodology note above)
- **Ballpark cost, comparable Gemini-tier model:** $__GEMINI_BALLPARK__ (illustrative -- see methodology note above)

**Decisions:**
- **The remaining mortgage term is solved from the standard annuity formula (`n = -ln(1 - r·B/P) / ln(1 + r)`), not stored**, since `Property` has no term/end-date field — only `mortgage_balance`, `monthly_payment` and `interest_rate`, exactly the three numbers that formula needs. `remainingMortgageTermMonths` returns `null`, never `NaN`, for every case with no finite answer: a payment that does not exceed the interest it accrues, a payment of `0`, or (handled as its own branch, not folded into the "no answer" case) a balance already at `0`, which is a term of `0` rather than an absence of one.
- **This codebase's `MortgageType` enum has no `'interest_only'` value** (`fixed | tracker | svr | none`, per `types.js`) — the issue asked what that value should mean here, and the honest answer turned out to be that there's nothing to special-case, because it doesn't exist as a value to check for. What makes a mortgage interest-only is structural: its `monthly_payment` exactly covers the interest and nothing more, which is precisely the boundary `remainingMortgageTermMonths` already treats as "never clears" — so an interest-only mortgage (or an underwater one, or one with no payment on record) falls into the same `null`-term branch automatically, with no `mortgage_type` read anywhere in the module.
- **One amortisation loop, not two.** The single month of reducing-balance amortisation that used to sit inline inside `property.js`'s `propertyEquityProjection` is now `property.js`'s own exported `amortiseMortgageMonth(balance, monthlyRate, payment)`; `propertyEquityProjection` calls it twelve times a year and samples annually, `mortgage-rate-rise.js`'s new `projectMortgageBalance` calls it monthly to find the balance outstanding at an arbitrary future month. Deliberately un-rounded per call, matching `propertyEquityProjection`'s existing behaviour, so many months of compounding don't accumulate rounding drift — confirmed by a test that cross-checks `projectMortgageBalance` against `propertyEquityProjection`'s own year-10 sample on the same property.
- **The config's `keepTerm` boolean is the one real-world choice a remortgage makes**: `true` keeps the number of payments left the same and lets the payment rise to match (the usual "my mortgage got more expensive" story, and the default); `false` keeps the payment the same and lets the term run longer instead. Both branches reduce to the same formula for `extraInterestOverRemainingTerm` — `(newPayment × newTerm) − (oldPayment × oldTerm)` — since the shared starting balance cancels out of the difference, so there's one calculation, not two, for "what did this cost in total interest."
- **The overlay only ever touches `ForecastMonthAdjustment.contributionFactor`, exactly as `income-shock.js` does**, and only when the payment actually changes: a `keepTerm: false` config leaves `monthly_payment` untouched, so it produces no cashflow adjustment and no forecast difference at all — `mortgageRateRiseForecast` returns the baseline byte-for-byte in that case (a property the tests assert by deep equality, mirroring `stress-test.js`'s zero-magnitude guarantee). The whole cost of choosing "keep the payment" is the extra interest paid over a longer term, which `forecast.js` has no way to show (it carries every debt, and every mortgage balance, forward unchanged rather than amortising it) — so `mortgageRateRiseImpact` reports `extraInterestOverRemainingTerm` as its own figure rather than folding it into a net-worth shortfall that would stay `0` regardless of how large the real cost is.
- **When the extra payment exceeds a month's total scheduled contribution, the contribution factor floors at `0` and the remainder is dropped, never carried to a later month** — the same "nothing is ever paid back" convention `income-shock.js` documents, applied here to a fixed £ shortfall instead of a percentage drop.
- **Every function that takes a property is tolerant of `null`, an unmatched `propertyId`, and a `mortgage_balance` of `0`**, per the issue's explicit point 6 — `mortgageRateRiseTerms` returns one designated "empty" shape (`hasMortgage: false`, everything else `0`) for all three, so #158's panel has one condition to check rather than guarding at every call site.
- **A rate "rise" is really a rate *change*** — nothing stops `newRatePct` from being lower than the property's current rate, which lowers the payment and reports a negative `delta`/`extraInterestOverRemainingTerm` instead of erroring or clamping. README.md only names the rise scenario, but the underlying arithmetic doesn't care about direction, and special-casing it to reject a cut would be effort spent making the engine less generally useful for no README-stated reason.

**Trade-offs / deviations from prompt:**
- **Engine only, per the issue's own narrowing.** No `MortgageRateRisePanel.svelte`, no property picker, no `ForecastProjections.svelte`/`src/routes/forecast/+page.svelte` wiring, and consequently no browser drive this time — unlike every prior scenario-panel issue in this journal, there is no UI surface yet to click through; that's explicitly #158, following the same engine-then-panel split this milestone already used for #131→#132 and #130→#150.
- **No `durationMonths`, unlike `IncomeShock`/`StressTest`.** A mortgage rate, once changed, stays changed until the next remortgage — out of this scenario's scope — so the overlay runs to the end of the forecast horizon rather than for a stated window; this is a deliberate reading of the issue's silence on the point, not an oversight.
- **`compareMortgageRateRise` and `mortgageRateRiseImpacts` were added even though the issue's six numbered points don't name them**, mirroring `income-shock.js`/`stress-test.js`'s own exported surface (a per-scenario impact map, a month-by-month comparison table) on the reasoning that #158's panel will want the same shape its two sibling panels already consume, and the marginal cost of keeping the three scenario modules' APIs parallel is small.
- **56 new tests in a new `mortgage-rate-rise.test.js`**, plus `property.js`'s existing 34 unchanged (the amortisation-loop extraction is behaviour-preserving, confirmed by the full suite passing before and after): config normalisation/clamping, `remainingMortgageTermMonths`/`annuityPayment`/`projectMortgageBalance` (including the zero-rate branch, the never-clears branch, and the cross-check against `propertyEquityProjection`), `mortgageRateRiseTerms` for both `keepTerm` branches, a rate cut, the interest-only boundary case, and a mortgage that clears before the rate change lands, the `adjustMonth` hook (before `atMonth`, floored at `0`, nothing to scale when no contribution is due), `mortgageRateRiseForecast`'s baseline-identity guarantees, and the impact/comparison readers. 1823 tests total, up from 1767. `npm test` still isn't in `.github/workflows/ci.yml` (flagged since #2), so these run locally but not on the PR; `build`/`check`/`lint` remain the CI gates and all three pass.

## Scenario: one-off large costs — the engine — 2026-08-08
<!-- METRICS:scenario-one-off-large-costs-the-engine -->
- **Execution Duration:** __DURATION__ seconds
- **Model:** __MODEL__
- **Turns:** __TURNS__
- **Input Tokens:** __INPUT_TOKENS__
- **Output Tokens:** __OUTPUT_TOKENS__
- **Estimated Cost:** $__COST__ (from Claude Code's reported total_cost_usd)
- **Ballpark cost, comparable GPT-5-tier model:** $__GPT_BALLPARK__ (illustrative -- see methodology note above)
- **Ballpark cost, comparable Gemini-tier model:** $__GEMINI_BALLPARK__ (illustrative -- see methodology note above)

**Decisions:**
- **None of `forecast.js`'s existing three adjustment fields could express "take a fixed £ amount out"**, so a fourth, `ForecastMonthAdjustment.withdrawal`, was added to the shared `projectScenario` seam alongside `factor`/`contributionFactor` — the issue's own foundational half. `growthRate`/`factor` describe a market move and `contributionFactor` scales the standing order; a lump-sum cost is neither. It is applied after that month's growth and contribution are computed, spread pro rata across every holding by its *opening* value that month — the same "no per-holding beta" reasoning `stress-test.js`'s crash uses for its own market-wide `factor`, since nothing in the data model says which account a wedding gets paid out of — and booked as negative growth, the same bucket a crash's `factor` already uses, so `compounding.js` → `reconcileCompounding` keeps holding with no third accumulator to add. Ran the full suite before and after touching the shared seam: all three existing adjustment fields' tests stayed green, and `compounding.test.js`'s reconciliation checks pass unchanged.
- **A cost bigger than the pot drains it to zero and the remainder is dropped, never carried to a later month or borrowed against a future one** — `income-shock.js`'s and `mortgage-rate-rise.js`'s own "nothing is ever paid back" convention, applied here to an outflow instead of a missed inflow. The cap is checked against the month's *opening* pot value specifically (before that month's own growth and contribution land), which is the conservative reading — a `forecast.test.js` case pins this with `growthRate: 0` so the "drains to exactly zero" assertion is deterministic.
- **The config is a list, not a slot — the issue's own explicit instruction**, since real use is rarely just one cost. Unlike its three siblings (`StressTest`/`IncomeShock`/`MortgageRateRise`, each a single config object), `one-off-costs.js`'s config is `OneOffCost[]`: any number of named, dated, priced rows, each with a stable `id` from a `createOneOffCost` factory matching `model.js`'s `createInvestment`/`createDebt` pattern. Two costs due the same month simply add together into one combined withdrawal for that month via `oneOffCostsAdjustment`'s per-offset fold, rather than being treated as a conflict or requiring a second hook call.
- **The five-function shape (config → normaliser → `adjustMonth` hook → `*Forecast` wrapper → impact reader → comparison rows) is mirrored from `stress-test.js`/`income-shock.js`/`mortgage-rate-rise.js` again**, but the impact reader's shape had to change: with N dated items instead of one dial, a single `occurs` boolean says nothing useful. `oneOffCostOccurrences` dates every configured cost against a forecast (`occurs`/calendar `date`, sorted soonest-first) — the per-cost readout #161's panel needs instead of guarding at every call site — and `oneOffCostsImpact` separates `totalConfigured` (every cost, regardless of date) from `totalOccurring` (only the ones that actually land inside the horizon), so a cost priced today but dated fifty years out still shows up as "configured" without inflating the "here's what actually hit the projection" figure.
- **Reviewed, rather than reused blind, the unmerged `origin/scenario-one-off-large-costs` branch (`cc8583d`) the issue points to as prior art.** Its `forecast.js` seam and `one-off-costs.js` module matched the conventions above closely enough (opening-value pro-rata split, cap-then-drop, negative-growth booking, the five-function shape) to adopt after independent read-through and a full local test run, rather than rederiving the same design from scratch; its `OneOffCostsPanel.svelte` and `ForecastProjections.svelte` wiring were left out entirely, since those are explicitly #161's scope, not this issue's.
- 8 new tests in `forecast.test.js` (a `withdrawal` describe block alongside the existing `contributionFactor` one: applies to the named month only, books as negative growth with the split still reconciling, still pays the month's contribution first, splits pro rata across two holdings by opening value, caps at the opening pot and never goes negative, has no effect at `0`, combines with `factor`/`contributionFactor` in the same month, and reaches every scenario) and 51 new tests in a new `one-off-costs.test.js` (config/list normalisation and clamping, `createOneOffCost`'s fresh ids, the `adjustMonth` hook combining same-month costs and keeping different-month ones apart, `oneOffCostsForecast`'s baseline-identity guarantees at an empty or all-zero list, `oneOffCostOccurrences`' sorting and in/out-of-horizon dating, and the impact/comparison readers including the `totalConfigured` vs `totalOccurring` split). 1882 tests total, up from 1823. `npm run build`, `npm run check` (0 errors, same 2 pre-existing `<slot>` warnings) and `npm run lint` all pass.

**Trade-offs / deviations from prompt:**
- **Engine only, per the issue's own narrowing** (its text records that the original attempt hit `error_max_turns` trying to build the seam, the maths module, the panel and the wiring in one session). No `OneOffCostsPanel.svelte`, no add/remove row list, no `ForecastProjections.svelte`/`src/routes/forecast/+page.svelte` wiring, and consequently no browser drive this time — that's #161, the same engine-then-panel split this milestone already used for #134→#158, #131→#132 and #130→#150.
- **No upper bound on a cost's `amount`, only a floor at £0.** README.md's examples (wedding, car, home renovation) imply no ceiling, and nothing else in the data model caps a lump sum (property/investment values aren't capped either); `normaliseOneOffCost` accepts any non-negative number.
- **Nothing about the configured costs is persisted** — there is still no slot on `AppData` for a planning scenario (`store.js`/#5 territory), matching every sibling scenario module's own trade-off. That remains true regardless of this split, since persistence was never part of any of the four scenario-engine issues.
- `npm test` still isn't in `.github/workflows/ci.yml` (flagged since #2), so these run locally but not on the PR; `build`/`check`/`lint` remain the CI gates and all three pass.

## Scenario: Budget 2026–2031 policy changes overlay — 2026-08-08
<!-- METRICS:scenario-budget-2026-2031-policy-changes-overlay -->
- **Execution Duration:** __DURATION__ seconds
- **Model:** __MODEL__
- **Turns:** __TURNS__
- **Input Tokens:** __INPUT_TOKENS__
- **Output Tokens:** __OUTPUT_TOKENS__
- **Estimated Cost:** $__COST__ (from Claude Code's reported total_cost_usd)
- **Ballpark cost, comparable GPT-5-tier model:** $__GPT_BALLPARK__ (illustrative -- see methodology note above)
- **Ballpark cost, comparable Gemini-tier model:** $__GEMINI_BALLPARK__ (illustrative -- see methodology note above)

**Decisions:**
- **This scenario is not a `forecast.js` `adjustMonth` overlay, and could not be.** Its four siblings in this milestone (`stress-test.js`, `income-shock.js`, `mortgage-rate-rise.js`, `one-off-costs.js`) all ask questions about a monthly savings path, so all four hang off the same hook. These two measures change nothing about what the pot is worth — they change how much of it survives death — so `src/lib/budget-policy.js` is an overlay on an *estate valuation* instead, and shares no machinery with the other four. It keeps their house style (numbered conventions in the module comment, `DEFAULT_*` + `normalise*` + a `*Impact` reader + a per-row comparison) and none of their plumbing.
- **The two measures are data, not code paths.** `BUDGET_POLICY_MEASURES` is an exported frozen table with, per measure, the Budget that announced it and its date, when it bites, the figures it turns on, a gov.uk source, and — the point of the exercise — a `confidence` field. The issue asked for the same rigour `tax.js`/`hicbc.js` hold themselves to; those two could cite HMRC's published tables, and this session had no network access, so the honest version of that rigour is a machine-readable confidence marker a panel can render next to the pounds rather than a footnote nobody reads. The module comment carries a "Sourcing note — read before trusting a number here" section saying the same thing at length.
- **`BAND_FREEZE_END_TAX_YEAR = 2030` (2030/31, ending 5 April 2031) is the one figure marked `medium` and made overridable.** That the nil-rate bands are frozen to at least 5 April 2030 is settled (extended there at the Autumn Budget of 30 October 2024, from the 5 April 2028 set in November 2022). A further year to 5 April 2031 is what this issue's own title ("Budget 2026–2031") describes and what the constant encodes, but I could not verify it, so `BudgetPolicy.freezeEndTaxYear` exists purely as the override and a test pins that every tax year before the disputed one is byte-identical either way. A wrong end date can therefore only move one row of a five-row table, and the code says so at the point of use rather than in a commit message.
- **The counterfactual is "these two changes never happened", and it is a counterfactual, not history.** Every impact figure is the difference between two complete IHT calculations on the same estate: `withChanges` (pensions in the estate from 2027/28, bands frozen) against `withoutChanges` (pensions outside it, bands uprated by the user's own inflation assumption). The bands have not actually risen since 2009/10 (NRB) and 2020/21 (RNRB), so calling the comparison "before" or "old rules" would be a lie; it is `withoutChanges` throughout. The uprating reproduces the statutory default the freeze suspends — CPI, rounded **up** to the nearest £1,000 (IHTA 1984 s.8) — because a comparison against un-rounded compounding would price the freeze slightly wrong every year.
- **The split between the two measures is sequential, and the order is stated in the API.** Tax is not additive across overlapping thresholds: a pension entering the estate is worth more once the bands are frozen than it would be if they had kept up, so "how much of this is the freeze?" has no order-free answer. `budgetPolicyImpact` computes a third, intermediate liability (`freezeOnly`) and reports `fromFrozenBands` and `fromPensionsInEstate` against it, which makes the two parts add to `extraTax` exactly by construction rather than approximately — a property asserted for every modelled year rather than for one worked example.
- **The pension measure switches on at the tax year, not at the calendar year.** `PENSION_IHT_TAX_YEAR = 2027` means 2027/28, i.e. deaths on or after 6 April 2027, so a 2026/27 estate never counts a pot however the config is set. Getting this one year out is the easiest available mistake here, so it is convention 7 in the module comment and has its own test either side of the boundary.
- **"Unused pension funds" means Defined Contribution pots and nothing else** — `retirement-income.js`'s existing `definedContributionPot`, so DC workplace pots and SIPPs. A DB scheme has no fund to leave and the survivors' pensions it pays are outside the measure; the State Pension is a promise, not a pot. A Lifetime ISA recorded on the Pensions tab is an ISA in everything but the tab it was typed into, so it sits in the base estate on *both* sides of the change — the same reading `retirement-income.js` already takes of a LISA — rather than appearing in 2027/28 as if it were newly taxable.
- **The residence nil-rate band's £2m taper is modelled here even though it is arguably #138's**, because without it the pension measure's headline effect is materially understated: a pot does not merely add itself to the taxable estate, it drags the estate through the taper and can cost more than 40% of itself. A test pins exactly that — a £400k pot costing £200k rather than £160k on an estate that crosses £2m only once the pot is counted.
- **No qualifying home recorded means no residence nil-rate band**, rather than "unknown, assume the full £175,000". An estate valued from this app's own data with no property in it has no home inside its total either, so granting a home-related allowance against it would quietly understate the bill — and understating a real IHT exposure is the failure mode worth designing against. Only a `primary_residence` counts (a buy-to-let or a holiday home does not, however valuable), the band is capped at what that home is worth net of its mortgage, and negative equity reads as nothing to attach the band to rather than a negative allowance.
- **The estate is valued from exactly what counts towards net worth, plus the pension pots.** The `exclude_from_net_worth`/`include_in_net_worth` flags exist to stop the same money being counted twice — above all the mortgage toggle, which hides a mortgage because the property tab is already netting it off — so honouring them is what keeps this figure reconcilable with the net worth headline every other tab shows. The cost, stated in the module comment rather than left implicit, is that a holding deliberately hidden from net worth is also outside this estate figure even though it would be inside a real one.
- **64 new tests in a new `budget-policy.test.js`**: the statutory figures against CLAUDE.md's stated 2026/27 domain rules, the measure table's provenance fields and frozen-ness, tax-year labelling including the century rollover, config normalisation/clamping, `indexedBand`'s round-up-to-£1,000 rule and its compounding, the freeze holding every modelled year flat and then resuming *from the frozen level* rather than from where it would have been, the taper (partial, exhausted, and doubled by a transferred band), the residence-value cap, DC-only pot selection, the LISA's both-sides treatment, the exclusion flags, growth with debts held flat, insolvent and under-band estates, the pension measure's 2027/28 boundary, the parts-sum-to-the-whole property across every year, the taper interaction, and the projection/summary readers. 1946 tests total, up from 1882. `npm run build`, `npm run check` (0 errors, same 2 pre-existing `<slot>` warnings) and `npm run lint` all pass.

**Trade-offs / deviations from prompt:**
- **The precise policy details are *not* all confidently known, and the issue asked to say so here rather than guess.** Stated from knowledge with no network access to verify against gov.uk: the £325,000 nil-rate band, the £175,000 residence nil-rate band, the £2,000,000 taper threshold and the 40% rate are `high` confidence (they are also CLAUDE.md's and README.md's own stated 2026/27 figures, matched exactly rather than substituted from general knowledge); 6 April 2027 for unused pensions entering the estate is `high` (Autumn Budget 2024, confirmed in the July 2025 consultation response); **the end of the band freeze is `medium`** — certainly 5 April 2030, probably a further year to 5 April 2031, not verified; and the CPI-rounded-up-to-£1,000 statutory uprating rule behind the counterfactual is `medium`. Nothing here is a placeholder in the sense of an invented number, but the freeze end date is the one figure a reader should check before relying on a 2030/31 row.
- **Engine only — no panel, no route wiring, no browser drive.** The estate tab is still a five-line stub, and #138 ("Estate & IHT: nil-rate band core calculation") is the open, unbuilt foundational issue for `src/lib/estate.js`. Building an estate UI on top of a scenario overlay before the calculation it overlays exists would guarantee rework, so this follows the engine-then-panel split this milestone already used for #134→#158, #136→#161, #131→#132 and #130→#150 — except that, unlike those, no follow-up issue exists yet for the panel. Worth filing one alongside #138.
- **The band arithmetic here overlaps #138 and should be refactored onto `estate.js` when it lands.** `availableNilRateBands` and `estateIht` are this module's own NRB/RNRB/taper/40% calculation, written because an overlay needs something to overlay and #138 has not been built. Transferable allowances are modelled as a single `transferredBandsPct` dial rather than from a partner record, since the data model has no partner (#142). The duplication is deliberate and flagged in the module comment; the alternative was blocking this issue on an unstarted one.
- **The 36% reduced rate for estates leaving 10%+ to charity is not modelled**, though README.md lists it under the Phase 1 estate calculator. It is orthogonal to both Budget measures — it would scale every figure in the table by the same 0.9 without changing which measure caused what — and it belongs with the rest of the estate calculation in #138. Lifetime gifts and taper relief (#139) are out for the same reason.
- **Growing an estate to a future tax year is one nominal rate on the asset side with debts held flat**, not a `forecast.js` projection. Reusing `forecastScenarios` would have brought monthly contributions, per-holding growth rates and fund fees into a five-row IHT table that needs none of them, and would still have had nothing to say about properties, physical assets or pension pots, which `forecast.js` does not carry. It is a coarse figure and the module comment labels it as one.
- **Nothing about the configured policy is persisted** — there is still no slot on `AppData` for a planning scenario (`store.js`/#5 territory), the same trade-off every sibling scenario module in this milestone records.
- `npm test` still isn't in `.github/workflows/ci.yml` (flagged since #2), so these run locally but not on the PR; `build`/`check`/`lint` remain the CI gates and all three pass.

## Estate & IHT: nil-rate band core calculation — 2026-08-08
<!-- METRICS:estate-iht-nil-rate-band-core-calculation -->
- **Execution Duration:** __DURATION__ seconds
- **Model:** __MODEL__
- **Turns:** __TURNS__
- **Input Tokens:** __INPUT_TOKENS__
- **Output Tokens:** __OUTPUT_TOKENS__
- **Estimated Cost:** $__COST__ (from Claude Code's reported total_cost_usd)
- **Ballpark cost, comparable GPT-5-tier model:** $__GPT_BALLPARK__ (illustrative -- see methodology note above)
- **Ballpark cost, comparable Gemini-tier model:** $__GEMINI_BALLPARK__ (illustrative -- see methodology note above)

**Decisions:**
- **The foundational module arrived second, so `budget-policy.js` was re-based onto it rather than left as a rival copy.** #137 merged an hour before this issue started and had already declared the £325,000 nil-rate band, the £175,000 residence nil-rate band, its £2,000,000 taper threshold, the 40% rate and a taper/transfer/cap calculation — its own comment says in terms that "#138's `estate.js` is where a fuller valuation belongs". Two statements of a correctness-critical statutory figure is exactly the thing this issue exists to prevent, so `estate.js` now declares all five and `budget-policy.js` imports them back and re-exports them unchanged (same binding, so `index.js`'s `export *` from both resolves to one declaration). Its `availableNilRateBands` and `estateIht` became four-line adapters over `estateAllowances`/`inheritanceTax`, converting its single `transferredBandsPct` dial and its year's own uprated bands into the shape `estate.js` takes; all 64 of its existing tests pass untouched, which is the check that the delegation is behaviour-preserving rather than merely plausible. The same extraction pattern `mortgage-rate-rise.js` used on `property.js`'s amortisation loop, run in the other direction.
- **The £2,000,000 taper is measured on the net estate, before the spouse exemption** — the single easiest thing to get wrong here, and the reason `estateAllowances` takes a whole `Estate` rather than one number. An estate of £2.4m leaving everything to a spouse pays no tax at all *and* still loses the entire residence band; a calculator that tapered on the chargeable estate would report the band intact and hand the survivor an allowance they do not have. Liabilities come off before that measure (a £2.2m estate with a £300k mortgage is under the threshold, not over it); the spouse exemption does not.
- **The residence band is set against the estate before the nil-rate band.** It changes nothing about this death's bill — both are charged at nil and the total available is identical either way — but it decides how much of the £325,000 is left over, and therefore how much a surviving spouse brings forward. An estate of £400,000 with a £300,000 home to children uses £175,000 of residence band and £225,000 of nil-rate band, transferring 30.769% of the latter; charging the whole chargeable estate against the nil-rate band instead would transfer nothing and make the residence band worth strictly less than it is.
- **Transferred allowances are two percentages, not one and not an amount.** A first estate can easily leave one band unused and not the other, so `Estate` carries `transferredNilRateBandPct` and `transferredResidenceNilRateBandPct` separately — `budget-policy.js`'s single dial feeds both, which is the right simplification for a panel about Budget measures and the wrong one for the estate engine itself. Percentages rather than amounts is what makes the transfer survive a change in the bands: `transferableAllowances` measures what went unused against the bands in force at the *first* death (carried on the calculation as `bands` for exactly this reason) and prices it at whatever the survivor will face.
- **`transferableAllowances` closes the loop the issue's "spousal/civil-partner transfer" half opens**, and states its three arguable readings out loud: the unused nil-rate band is measured after the residence band has been used (above); a residence band the taper had already removed still transfers in full, because the percentage is measured against the untapered enhancement, so a first estate over £2m that used no residence band passes on 100% of it; and each percentage is capped at 100%, because the statutory ceiling is one extra band however many the first estate itself was entitled to. Feed the two percentages back into a second `inheritanceTax` call and the familiar "£1,000,000 for a couple" falls out — which is a test, not a claim.
- **The seven worked examples in the module comment are the ones the module was written against**, each with a test of the same name: £70,000 on a £500,000 estate with nothing to descendants; nothing on the same estate with a £300,000 home to children; nothing at £1,000,000 with both bands transferred (and 40p at £1,000,001, which pins the boundary); £660,000 at £2,100,000 after a partial taper; £810,000 at £2,350,000 once the taper has taken the band entirely; £62,000 at £600,000 where a £120,000 home caps the band below £175,000; nothing at £900,000 where £400,000 passes to a spouse.
- **A `null` residence means "no qualifying home", not "unknown"** — the `EstateInput` type accepts it because that is precisely what `budget-policy.js` reports for a position with no primary residence in it, and no home means no residence band, which is the legally accurate reading and the only one consistent with an estate figure that has no home inside it either.

**Trade-offs / deviations from prompt:**
- **No 36% charity rate, and no charity input at all**, even though README.md's free-tier estate list names it and no other issue in the milestone covers it. Charitable legacies being exempt is the easy half; the reduced rate turns on a "baseline amount" whose interaction with the residence nil-rate band I could not verify — this session had **no network access** (`WebSearch` is not granted here, the same constraint #137's sourcing note records), and the issue asks for exact figures against worked examples on a calculation that "directly informs real inheritance tax exposure". Accepting charitable gifts as an exemption without the rate would quietly charge 40% where 36% was due, so the module takes no charity input: an estate with charitable legacies cannot be modelled here yet, which is visible, rather than being modelled wrongly, which is not. The gap is recorded in the module's "what this deliberately does not model" section for whoever picks it up.
- **Lifetime gifts are a documented seam, not an implementation.** Chargeable transfers in the seven years before death use the nil-rate band first, and that is #139's issue in this same milestone; the module comment names the one place it plugs in (reducing `AvailableBands.nrb` before anything is charged) rather than half-building it here.
- **Also deliberately absent**, each because nothing in this app's data model holds it: Business and Agricultural Property Relief, quick succession relief, trusts and settled property, grossing up where a specific gift is left free of tax, the instalment option, and the deemed £100,000 residence band for a spouse who died before 6 April 2017.
- **Engine only, per the issue's own narrowing ("no UI yet").** `src/routes/estate/+page.svelte` is still the placeholder it was — the Estate tab's views are #140 — so there was no UI surface to click through and, unlike the panel issues in this journal, no browser drive this time. Valuing an estate from tracked holdings/properties/pensions is likewise untouched: `budget-policy.js`'s `estateValuation`/`qualifyingResidenceValue` already do it, and #140 is where the two halves meet.
- **The bands are a parameter, not a constant, everywhere they are used.** Strictly the issue only asked for 2026/27, but `budget-policy.js` exists to model what happens when the freeze ends, and a second copy of the taper/cap arithmetic for uprated bands is precisely what this issue was meant to stop. Callers who only want this tax year pass nothing and get `STATUTORY_NIL_RATE_BANDS`.
- **70 new tests in a new `estate.test.js`**: the statutory figures against CLAUDE.md's stated 2026/27 rules, estate normalisation and clamping, `netEstate`/`chargeableEstate` (including an insolvent estate and one passing wholly to a spouse), `transferredBand`, `estateAllowances` (no home, no descendants, both bands transferred, transferred independently, partial and total taper, taper measured before the exemption and after liabilities, the residence-value cap, taper and cap biting together, another year's bands, and an agreement check against `budget-policy.js`'s delegating wrapper), the seven worked examples, band-ordering and effective-rate readings, and every `transferableAllowances` case including both round trips. 2016 tests total, up from 1946. `npm test` still isn't in `.github/workflows/ci.yml` (flagged since #2), so these run locally but not on the PR; `build`/`check` (0 errors, same 2 pre-existing `<slot>` warnings) and `lint` remain the CI gates and all three pass.

## Estate & IHT: lifetime gifts with 7-year taper relief — 2026-08-08
<!-- METRICS:estate-iht-lifetime-gifts-with-7-year-taper-relief -->
- **Execution Duration:** __DURATION__ seconds
- **Model:** __MODEL__
- **Turns:** __TURNS__
- **Input Tokens:** __INPUT_TOKENS__
- **Output Tokens:** __OUTPUT_TOKENS__
- **Estimated Cost:** $__COST__ (from Claude Code's reported total_cost_usd)
- **Ballpark cost, comparable GPT-5-tier model:** $__GPT_BALLPARK__ (illustrative -- see methodology note above)
- **Ballpark cost, comparable Gemini-tier model:** $__GEMINI_BALLPARK__ (illustrative -- see methodology note above)

**Decisions:**
- **Taper relief reduces the tax on a gift, never the value of the gift** — this is convention 1 of the new `src/lib/lifetime-gifts.js`, it is the single most commonly mis-stated rule in UK Inheritance Tax, and getting it the popular-but-wrong way round changes six-figure answers. A £300,000 gift made six and a half years before death still consumes £300,000 of the nil-rate band and leaves the estate with £25,000; the 80% relief it "qualifies for" is 80% of a nil bill and is worth exactly nothing. `GiftAssessment.taperRelief` is therefore `0` on every gift the band covers, and there is a test whose whole job is to assert that a long-ago gift the band shelters gets no relief *and* still takes the band.
- **Gifts are cumulated oldest first, which means the band shelters the gift with the most relief and exposes the one with the least.** Two £200,000 gifts, one 6y11m before the death and one 1y11m, produce £30,000 of tax — not because of the older gift's 80% relief, which it never gets to use, but because the older gift eats £200,000 of the band and leaves the recent one, entitled to no relief at all, with £75,000 above it. `LifetimeGiftLedger` reports `cumulativeChargeable` per row so a table can show the order doing the work rather than just the total.
- **The band the gifts spend is the band at death, transferred share included, and it is applied exactly once.** `inheritanceTaxWithGifts` reads `estateAllowances(estate, bands).nrb` — so a `transferredNilRateBandPct: 100` doubles the band *before* anything spends it — hands that to the ledger, then re-states the estate with `transferredNilRateBandPct: 0` and gives `estate.js`'s `inheritanceTax` whatever came back. Passing the reduced band through the estate's own transfer percentage would have applied the doubling twice, to a number the gifts had already eaten into. The residence nil-rate band is passed through untouched, because no lifetime gift can ever consume an allowance that only attaches to a home passing on death.
- **Seven years is a calendar period with an inclusive boundary, so complete years alone are not enough to band a gift.** IHTA 1984 s.7(4) draws its lines at "not more than N years" and s.3A(4) exempts at "seven years or more", which means a gift made exactly three years before the death is still charged at the full 40% while one made a day later gets 20% relief, and a gift made exactly seven years before is exempt while one a day later is not. `yearsBetween` therefore returns `onAnniversary` alongside `years`, and `taperReliefBand` uses the pair to pick a row. 29 February falls back to 28 February in a non-leap year, so a leap-day gift is out of account on 28 February seven years later. None of this can be done with epoch milliseconds, so the module carries its own small calendar-arithmetic layer rather than borrowing `assets.js`'s `new Date(...)` approach.
- **The £3,000 annual exemption is applied automatically and the other six are declared per gift**, which is the split the law itself makes. s.19 is not elective — it attaches to the earliest transfers of the tax year and is simply lost if nothing uses it — so the engine allocates it in date order, current year's own £3,000 before anything brought forward, one year of carry-forward only. Everything else (spouse, charity, normal expenditure out of income, the £250 small gifts exemption, the three wedding caps) turns on facts this app has no way to infer from an amount and a date, so `Gift.exemption` is a declared field with `none` as the default and as the fallback for anything unrecognised — a typo overstates the bill rather than quietly exempting a gift.
- **The £250 small gifts exemption is modelled as the all-or-nothing thing it actually is.** s.20 cannot cover the first £250 of a larger gift, is counted per recipient per tax year, and is barred to anyone who has already had annual exemption that year — so a declared `small` gift that fails any of those tests falls back to being an ordinary potentially exempt transfer, reported as `exemptionApplied: 'none'` beside the `exemption: 'small'` the user asked for rather than silently ignored. `recipient` is matched case- and whitespace-insensitively, since it is free text and is doing real work as a key.
- **Two counterfactuals, because they answer different questions.** `taxIfGiftsSurvived` is "what happens if I make it through the countdown" (the same estate with the whole band); `taxIfNothingGifted` is "was giving it away worth anything at all" (the estate grown back by everything gifted, no gifts to charge). The second is the one that shows a gift dragging an estate back under the £2,000,000 residence-band taper, which is often the larger half of what gifting achieves — a test pins £230,000 of saving on a £2.4m estate where only £600,000 of that is the 40% on the gift itself. A recent gift correctly reports a saving of nil and a `costOfFailedGifts` equal to what dying now costs.
- **`transferableAllowancesAfterGifts` exists because `estate.js`'s own version would over-report.** Convention 5 there measures the unused percentage against `IhtCalculation.bands`, which after this module's rewiring is the band *net of the gifts* — a first estate that gifted £200,000 away would look like it had far more left to pass to the survivor than it does. The wrapper restates the calculation against the true statutory band with the gifts' share counted as used and then defers to `estate.js` for the arithmetic and the three readings it documents, rather than growing a second copy of them here.

**Trade-offs / deviations from prompt:**
- **Chargeable lifetime transfers, and therefore the "14-year rule", are not modelled.** Gifts into most trusts are chargeable when made (20% above the band) and are re-cumulated at death, and a CLT made up to seven years *before* a failed gift reduces the band available to that gift — which is where the fourteen years come from. Nothing in this app's data model describes a trust, every `Gift` here is a potentially exempt transfer to an individual, and a half-built cumulation would understate the bill on exactly the estates complicated enough to care. Named in the module's "what this deliberately does not model" section, alongside gifts with reservation of benefit, the pre-owned assets charge, BPR/APR on a lifetime gift and its clawback, fall-in-value relief, the instalment option, and grossing up where the giver agreed to bear the tax.
- **The taper percentages and the seven years are stated from knowledge, not verified.** This session had **no network access** (the same constraint #137's and #138's sourcing notes record), so IHTA 1984 s.7(4)'s 100/80/60/40/20 proportions, s.3A(4)'s seven years, and the exemption amounts (£3,000 with one year forward, £250, £5,000/£2,500/£1,000) are cited in the doc comment as the issue asked but were not re-read against legislation.gov.uk or IHTM14611 at the time of writing. They are the high-confidence core of the module — the taper table is also the one the issue's own text quotes, and `effectiveRatePct` is derived from `estate.js`'s `IHT_RATE` rather than being a second hard-coded copy, so the two can never drift — but a reader relying on this for real planning should check them.
- **The annual exemption assumes a full £3,000 was available to carry into the earliest recorded tax year.** Nothing in the data model records the year before the first gift, and a giver who had used it would presumably have recorded that gift too, so the generous reading is the default — but it *is* a reading, and `carriedForwardAnnualExemption` is an option so a caller who knows better can pass `0`. `applyAnnualExemption: false` is there for the other case, a user copying figures off an IHT403 that are already net.
- **One documented simplification inside the small gifts rule.** s.20 is barred where the same person has had annual exemption in the same tax year; the engine checks that in date order only, so a small gift is not retroactively disqualified by a *later* gift to the same recipient in the same year. Processing it strictly is circular — disqualifying the small gift makes it eligible for the annual exemption, which changes the allocation that disqualified it — and the case is vanishingly rare.
- **Engine only, and no persistence — the same shape #138 landed in.** `src/routes/estate/+page.svelte` is still the five-line placeholder, #140 is the issue that builds the Estate tab's views, and there is no slot on `AppData` for a list of gifts (the same gap every scenario module in this milestone records). `giftCountdown` defaults its death date to today precisely so #140's "if I died today" view is a no-argument call rather than something it has to assemble. Unlike the panel issues in this journal there was no UI surface to click through, so no browser drive this time.
- **87 new tests in a new `lifetime-gifts.test.js`**: the statutory table against both the s.7(4) and the gov.uk effective-rate forms and against `estate.js`'s 40%; calendar arithmetic (exact anniversaries, a day either side, 29 February in both directions, invalid and reversed dates, the 6 April tax-year boundary and a century rollover); every taper band and both sides of each boundary; normalisation; the six worked examples the module comment prints; oldest-first cumulation; each of the seven exemptions including all four ways a small gift can fail to qualify; the four gift statuses and the sort order; the countdown; and `inheritanceTaxWithGifts` end-to-end, including a transferred band, the untouched residence band, another year's bands, both counterfactuals and the round trip back through `transferableAllowancesAfterGifts` into a survivor's estate. 2103 tests total, up from 2016. `npm test` still isn't in `.github/workflows/ci.yml` (flagged since #2), so these run locally but not on the PR; `build`/`check` (0 errors, same 2 pre-existing `<slot>` warnings) and `lint` remain the CI gates and all three pass.

## Estate & IHT: property gift-on-sale — 2026-08-08
<!-- METRICS:estate-iht-property-gift-on-sale -->
- **Execution Duration:** __DURATION__ seconds
- **Model:** __MODEL__
- **Turns:** __TURNS__
- **Input Tokens:** __INPUT_TOKENS__
- **Output Tokens:** __OUTPUT_TOKENS__
- **Estimated Cost:** $__COST__ (from Claude Code's reported total_cost_usd)
- **Ballpark cost, comparable GPT-5-tier model:** $__GPT_BALLPARK__ (illustrative -- see methodology note above)
- **Ballpark cost, comparable Gemini-tier model:** $__GEMINI_BALLPARK__ (illustrative -- see methodology note above)

**Decisions:**
- **This is allocation arithmetic on top of #139, not a new piece of tax law, and the new `src/lib/property-gift-on-sale.js` is sized accordingly** — 5 exported functions against lifetime-gifts.js's 15-odd, no statutory figures of its own, and its module comment carries no "Sourcing" section because there is nothing here to source. The issue called this "a specific, fairly narrow case on top of already-built machinery" and the module tries to actually be that, rather than growing a second estate-planning surface next to it.
- **The whole point is stated as convention 1: net proceeds are `salePrice - sellingCosts - mortgageRedemption`, floored at zero.** A sale that clears less than is owed on the mortgage leaves nothing to gift — not a negative gift, which IHT has no concept of — and the module would rather report `£0, no gifts` than let a negative number wash through into `lifetimeGiftLedger` and come out the other side as a mysterious negative chargeable transfer.
- **Not every pound of the proceeds has to be given away, so `giftedPct` (100% by default) sits above the beneficiary split, not inside it.** `retainedProceeds` — what `giftedPct` leaves over — is deliberately not a `Gift`: it is kept by the seller, an ordinary disposal, and the module's job stops at the boundary between "gifted" and "not". Modelling the split this way (a single dial for how much is given away, then a separate one for how it's divided) keeps "I'm selling the house and keeping half for care costs, splitting the rest between my two children" a two-number change rather than a beneficiary list that has to sum to some fraction of 100.
- **Beneficiary `sharePct` values are relative weights, normalised against each other inside `propertySaleGifts`, not against 100.** `1`/`1`/`1` and `33.3`/`33.3`/`33.3` produce the identical split — a test asserts exactly that — and a list that happens to sum to 90 or 110 doesn't silently under- or over-gift the difference. This was the one real design choice in the module: the alternative (shares as literal percentages of the sale, with anything short of 100% simply left ungifted) would have made `giftedPct` and the beneficiary list redundant with each other, since a user could achieve "keep 20%" either by setting `giftedPct: 80` or by leaving the beneficiaries 20 points short — two ways to say the same thing is exactly the kind of ambiguity a form built on top of this would have had to paper over.
- **The last beneficiary in the list absorbs the rounding remainder**, the same "round the parts, fix the last one" convention `lifetime-gifts.js`'s cumulative total and `budget-policy.js`'s sequential split both already rely on — three-way splits of £100,000 land on `£33,333.33 / £33,333.33 / £33,333.34`, and a test pins the total against `giftedProceeds` to the penny rather than trusting that three independent roundings happen to agree.
- **Every produced `Gift` is otherwise unremarkable**: dated at completion, run through `createGift` from `lifetime-gifts.js` itself rather than a hand-rolled object literal, so there is exactly one place in the codebase that knows a `Gift`'s shape. A beneficiary's own `exemption` field (`'none'` by default) is passed straight through — set it to `'spouse'` where the recipient is a spouse or civil partner, exactly as a hand-entered gift would be — and the description reads `"Share of sale proceeds: <property name>"`, falling back to a generic phrasing where no name was given.
- **`propertySaleGiftsFromProperty` is the seam the issue's own text names** ("when a property (`src/lib/property.js`) is sold"): it defaults `propertyName`/`salePrice`/`mortgageRedemption` from a real `Property` record's `.name`/`.value`/`.mortgage_balance` — a residential sale routinely redeems the whole mortgage through the conveyancing solicitor, and nothing in this app's data model records a part-redemption — while letting an explicit `sale.salePrice`/`.mortgageRedemption` override either, for the case where the actual completion figures differ from what's on record.
- **33 new tests in a new `property-gift-on-sale.test.js`**: the beneficiary and sale normalisers (defaults, clamping, unrecognised-exemption fallback, non-array/non-numeric inputs); the four worked examples the module comment prints (even split, partial `giftedPct`, negative-equity zero-net, uneven weights); the weights-not-summing-to-100 equivalence; the rounding-remainder split; zero-share and no-beneficiary edge cases; exemption pass-through; the description fallback; a null date carried onto every gift; two integration tests running the produced gifts straight through `lifetimeGiftLedger` and `inheritanceTaxWithGifts`; and `propertySaleGiftsFromProperty`'s defaulting and override behaviour. 2136 tests total, up from 2103. `npm test` still isn't in `.github/workflows/ci.yml` (flagged since #2), so these run locally but not on the PR; `build`/`check` (0 errors, same 2 pre-existing `<slot>` warnings) and `lint` remain the CI gates and all three pass.

**Trade-offs / deviations from prompt:**
- **Engine only, and no persistence — the same shape #138/#139 landed in, and the same reason.** `src/routes/estate/+page.svelte` is still the placeholder; #140 ("if I died today engine — estate-plan.js and the beneficiary/IHT data model") is the open issue where a beneficiary list gets an `AppData` home, and this module deliberately doesn't pre-empt it — `PropertyGiftBeneficiary` here is a plain function-argument shape, not something written into the data model, so #140's beneficiary type can land on its own terms without this module having staked out the name first. Every function here is pure: a sale and a beneficiary list go in, `Gift`s come out, nothing is stored.
- **When the gift actually happens is simplified to the completion date.** A real sale completes through a solicitor's client account and the gift to each beneficiary may follow days or weeks later; this module treats the sale date as the `Gift` date throughout, which is the conservative reading for IHT purposes (it starts the seven-year countdown as early as it can rather than as late as a real timeline might allow) rather than an attempt to model conveyancing or transfer timing, which nothing in this app's data captures anyway.
- **Capital Gains Tax on the sale itself is out of scope**, deliberately — README.md's Estate & IHT suite and this issue are both about Inheritance Tax, a sold second home or buy-to-let can trigger CGT quite separately from anything here, and nothing in this module reduces `salePrice` for it. Named in the module's "what this deliberately does not model" section rather than silently ignored.
- **No UI, and no browser drive** — there was no surface to click through; this follows the same engine-then-panel split the milestone has used throughout (#134→#158, #136→#161, #139/#138 both engine-only), and unlike those, no follow-up panel issue exists yet for this one specifically, since #166 ("if I died today panel and Estate-tab wiring") is presumably where a gift-on-sale row would eventually surface.

## Household/Partner: partner in the data model — 2026-08-08
<!-- METRICS:household-partner-partner-in-the-data-model -->
- **Execution Duration:** __DURATION__ seconds
- **Model:** __MODEL__
- **Turns:** __TURNS__
- **Input Tokens:** __INPUT_TOKENS__
- **Output Tokens:** __OUTPUT_TOKENS__
- **Estimated Cost:** $__COST__ (from Claude Code's reported total_cost_usd)
- **Ballpark cost, comparable GPT-5-tier model:** $__GPT_BALLPARK__ (illustrative -- see methodology note above)
- **Ballpark cost, comparable Gemini-tier model:** $__GEMINI_BALLPARK__ (illustrative -- see methodology note above)

**Decisions:**
- **`Partner` is `Profile` with the household-wide assumptions removed, not `Profile` with fields added.** The issue is explicit that a second person in the household does not need their own `journey_stage`, `inflation_rate`, `currency`, `tax_region` or `retirement_target` — those describe the household's planning posture, which the primary profile already sets once. `Partner` keeps exactly the fields README.md's "Partner profile" line names (DOB, retirement age, salary, pension, NI years) plus a display `name`, and `DEFAULT_PARTNER` shares `DEFAULT_PROFILE.retirement_age` (67) for the one assumption the two do have in common rather than restating the figure.
- **`AppData.partner` defaults to `null`, not to a `createPartner()` record with blank fields.** The issue calls this out directly — "no partner" is the normal case for a solo user and has to be representable as an absence, not a second profile silently populated with zeroes that would need a "has a partner" flag to disambiguate from a real one. `createAppData()` therefore sets `partner: null`, and `validatePartner` is a no-op on `null` — there is nothing to range-check about a partner that was never entered.
- **`normalisePartner` treats "not a plain object" as `null`, the same instinct `asRecordOrNull` already applies to the activity log's `snapshot`.** A document from before this issue simply has no `partner` key (`undefined`) and a document that recorded "no partner" has `partner: null` — both need to come back as `null` — but so does a hand-edited Gist where `partner` became a string, a number or an array. The alternative, running it through `asRecord`'s "coerce anything non-object to `{}`" and defaulting every field, would manufacture a phantom partner (empty name, `null` DOB, age 67, zero salary) out of `partner: "oops"`, which is worse than refusing to guess.
- **No `SCHEMA_VERSION` bump.** The comment above `SCHEMA_VERSION` reserves a bump for changes `normaliseAppData` alone cannot absorb; adding a new top-level key with a well-defined "missing means null" default is exactly what `normaliseAppData` is for, and `migrateAppData` already just delegates to it. A document written by the previous build round-trips through `normaliseAppData` unchanged except for gaining `partner: null` — there is nothing for a migration step to do.
- **`ni_qualifying_years` on `Partner` mirrors `Pension.ni_qualifying_years`, not `Profile`** — `Profile` has no NI field at all (a solo user's State Pension years live on their `Pension` records), but README.md's partner line names NI years explicitly and a partner has no `Pension` collection of their own to hang it off, so it sits directly on `Partner`, nullable and range-checked 0–60 the same way `pensions[].ni_qualifying_years` is.
- **`validatePartner` was inserted immediately after `validateProfile` in `validateAppData`**, and its five checks (`dob_month` 1–12, `dob_year` in range, `retirement_age` 16–120, `gross_salary` non-negative, `pension_pct` 0–100, plus `ni_qualifying_years` 0–60 or null) reuse the exact bounds `validateProfile`/`pensions[]` already use for the equivalent fields, rather than inventing new ones — a partner's retirement age is exactly as plausible-checked as the user's own.

**Trade-offs / deviations from prompt:**
- **Out of scope, per the issue's own narrowing: any UI.** The entry form on Settings (`src/components/PartnerProfile.svelte`, `src/routes/settings/+page.svelte` wiring, its component test) is #170 — the issue was split this way after the original combined issue hit `error_max_turns`, the same engine/panel split this milestone already uses for #136/#161, #131/#132 and #140/#166. There was no UI surface to click through, so no browser drive this session.
- **Household net worth lens (#143), joint retirement forecast dual markers, household budget/cashflow and Marriage Allowance wiring are all untouched** — this issue is the data model only, and every one of those reads `AppData.partner` rather than building it. `Partner.name`'s doc comment says as much ("labels the household net worth lens") without pre-empting #143's own design.
- **11 new tests in `model.test.js`** (2136 → 2147): a `partner` entry in the README-shape table; `createPartner`'s defaults and overrides; `createAppData().partner` defaulting to `null`; `normaliseAppData` on a document with no `partner` key at all, an explicit `partner: null`, a non-object `partner` (string/number/array), and a populated partner with an unknown field dropped; and `validateAppData` accepting `null` and a populated partner, and rejecting out-of-range `dob_month`/`retirement_age`/`pension_pct`/`ni_qualifying_years`. The existing round-trip test now carries a populated `Partner` through `JSON.parse(JSON.stringify(...))` alongside the profile it already exercised. `npm test` still isn't in `.github/workflows/ci.yml` (flagged since #2), so these run locally but not on the PR; `build`/`check` (0 errors, same 2 pre-existing `<slot>` warnings) and `lint` remain the CI gates and all three pass.

## Household/Partner: ownership % on holdings + the net worth lens engine — 2026-08-08
<!-- METRICS:household-partner-ownership-pct-and-net-worth-lens-engine -->
- **Execution Duration:** __DURATION__ seconds
- **Model:** __MODEL__
- **Turns:** __TURNS__
- **Input Tokens:** __INPUT_TOKENS__
- **Output Tokens:** __OUTPUT_TOKENS__
- **Estimated Cost:** $__COST__ (from Claude Code's reported total_cost_usd)
- **Ballpark cost, comparable GPT-5-tier model:** $__GPT_BALLPARK__ (illustrative -- see methodology note above)
- **Ballpark cost, comparable Gemini-tier model:** $__GEMINI_BALLPARK__ (illustrative -- see methodology note above)

**Decisions:**
- **No model change, as the issue itself established.** `ownership_pct` has been on `Investment` since the core data model — `createInvestment` defaults it to `100`, `normaliseInvestment` reads it via `asNumber(source.ownership_pct, 100)`, `validateAppData` range-checks it 0–100, and `csv-import.js`/`xlsx-export.js` already round-trip an "Ownership %" column. This issue is entirely about the one place a holding's fields are actually set (`InvestmentHoldings.svelte`'s add/edit form) and the engine that turns the stored percentage into a number a chart can plot (`net-worth.js`).
- **The form field mirrors `bought_for`/`year_purchased` in spirit but not in type**, and the difference is the whole reason it needs its own parse helper. Those two are deliberately `type="text"` so an untouched field stays the string `''` until submission; `holding-ownership-pct` is `type="number"` (a percentage wants the browser's numeric affordances — the spinner, the numeric keyboard on mobile), which means `bind:value` silently promotes the bound state to a real `number` the moment it's touched. `parseOwnershipPct` therefore branches on `typeof raw === 'string'` before trimming, so it copes with `''` (untouched), a string mid-edit and a `number` once Svelte has taken over — and reads blank or unparseable as `100`, not `0`, because `Number('') || 0` (the pattern every sibling numeric field uses) would silently zero out any holding whose owner field nobody thought to touch. The value is clamped to 0–100 in the same function, so the form can never hand the store something `validateAppData` would immediately flag.
- **The holdings list shows the percentage only when it isn't 100** (`· 60% yours`), appended to the existing "value · bought for · monthly contribution" line rather than a new one — the issue's own instruction, and consistent with how the list already stays quiet about fields at their default (no "unknown" purchase price shown as `£0`, for instance).
- **`net-worth.js` gets a fifth numbered convention, not a parallel module.** `NetWorthLens` (`'household' | 'you' | 'partner'`), `NET_WORTH_LENSES`, `DEFAULT_NET_WORTH_LENS` and `netWorthLensLabel` sit alongside the existing exports, and `netWorthPoint`/`netWorthSeries` both take an optional `{ lens }` that defaults to `'household'` — so every one of the 60-odd pre-existing call sites across the test suite, unmodified, keeps producing byte-for-byte the same numbers they always did. That default-parameter object (`{ lens = DEFAULT_NET_WORTH_LENS } = {}`) is the same shape `netWorthChartYExtent`'s `options` argument already uses, so a caller passing nothing, `{}`, or `{ lens: 'household' }` are all indistinguishable.
- **Scaling happens before `sumInvestmentValues` filters `exclude_from_net_worth`, not after**, which is what makes the lens and the mortgage-style exclusion compose instead of fight: `scaleInvestmentsForLens` maps each investment to a clone with its `value` multiplied by the ownership share and every other field — including `exclude_from_net_worth` — untouched, then hands the whole array to the same `sumInvestmentValues` convention 2 already relies on. A holding excluded from net worth stays excluded under every lens; nothing about "which holdings count" changes, only "how much of each counted one" does.
- **`you` and `partner` are `ownership_pct / 100` and `(100 - ownership_pct) / 100` of the same clamped percentage**, so they always sum back to the household share to the penny — a property the test suite pins directly rather than trusting the arithmetic to happen to work out. The clamp (`Math.min(100, Math.max(0, investment.ownership_pct))`) runs inside `scaleInvestmentsForLens` itself, not just in the form: a document that never passed through `validateAppData` (a hand-edited Gist, a build from before this issue) could carry an out-of-range value, and an unclamped `you` share above 100% would produce a `partner` share below zero — a negative slice of a holding, and the sum-back-to-household property silently breaking with it.
- **Debts get no lens, deliberately, and the module comment says why rather than inventing a split.** Nothing in the data model records which half of a debt belongs to which person, so every lens nets off the identical debt total — meaning a `you` or `partner` net worth understates its share of a genuinely joint mortgage. Guessing a 50/50 debt split with no data behind it would have looked more complete and been actively misleading; the honest thing is to say so in the doc comment, which is what convention 5 does.
- **`netWorthLensLabel`'s `partner` fallback reads `Partner.name`'s own doc comment rather than re-deciding the rule.** #142 (`model.js`/`types.js`, landed just before this issue) already states "Display name … labels the household net worth lens" on `Partner.name`, whose default is `''` — so `netWorthLensLabel('partner', partnerName)` treats a blank or whitespace-only name exactly like a missing one and returns the literal word `'Partner'`, matching the issue's spec precisely rather than leaving an empty string to render as a blank toggle option.
- **The forecast band gets no lens, and convention 5 says so explicitly** — `forecastBandSeries` is untouched by this issue, `forecastBand` in `forecast.js` never sees a lens argument, and the household projection is the only one the chart's shaded confidence band will ever show. That's the exact boundary #172 (the chart toggle, `NetWorthChart.svelte` + `+page.svelte` wiring) has to design its layout around, so it's stated as a hard constraint in the header rather than left for that issue to discover by reading the code.

**Trade-offs / deviations from prompt:**
- **Out of scope, per the issue's own narrowing: the chart toggle itself.** The Household/You/Partner control on `NetWorthChart.svelte`, its `+page.svelte` wiring and `NetWorthChart.test.js` are #172 — the issue was split this way after the combined version hit `error_max_turns` (100), the same engine/panel split this milestone already uses for #142/#170, #136/#161, #131/#132 and #140/#166. This session built only the data-entry field and the engine #172 will present.
- **No `InvestmentHoldings.test.js`.** The issue's own instruction was not to add one "unless a server-rendered smoke test in the style of `PensionTracker.test.js` is cheap to add" — but `PensionTracker.test.js`'s own comment explains why that pattern exists there and not for `InvestmentHoldings`/`DebtTracker`: a `render`-from-`svelte/server` smoke test only reaches the *initial* render, and the ownership field, like every other add/edit-form field this component already has, only does anything interesting once a user interacts with it (types into it, submits, edits an existing holding). A smoke test here would pin the empty state and nothing the field actually does, so it's left to the `net-worth.js` unit tests (which exercise `ownership_pct` end to end) and manual verification.
